<term>Hermetic Philosophy</term> <note>A philosophical and spiritual tradition based on ancient writings that influenced alchemy, astrology, and magic.</note>
<term>Pelican in its piety</term> <note>In alchemy, this symbol represents the "rubedo" or reddening stage, where the substance is perfected through a process of self-nourishment and intense heat.</note>
<term>Rose Cross</term> <note>A symbol consisting of a cross with a rose at its center, representing the union of the spiritual and the material; the primary symbol of the Rosicrucian movement.</note>
<term>Rosicrucianism</term> <note>A secret spiritual and cultural movement that emerged in the early 17th century, combining elements of Christian mysticism, alchemy, and Kabbalah.</note>
<term>Alchemy</term> <note>An ancient branch of natural philosophy that aimed to transform base metals into gold and discover a universal cure for disease (the Elixir of Life).</note>

# DEDICATION

and I have reflected deeply within myself on whom I should especially choose as a Patron for this edition; at last, my choice settled primarily upon Your name. And this was not without merit: for a certain manuscript codex of this book—which previously appeared in print so many times only in a mutilated <note>original: "mutilus"; meaning the text was incomplete, fragmented, or corrupted by previous editors</note> form—has now, for the first time from the Vatican, come into my hands, complete in all its parts and more intact. How great <margin>however</margin> the desire for this work has always been in the <term>Spagyric art</term> <note>original: "Spagyricâ"; a term referring to alchemy, specifically the chemical preparation of medicines by separating and recombining the "pure" essences of natural substances</note>, others will know best. Therefore, I did not hesitate to commend to You and to Your loyalty the first-fruits <note>original: "primitias"; a metaphor for the first results of a harvest or a new labor offered as a gift</note> of this newer and fuller edition, relying on the confidence that, because You excel in both the exact knowledge of such things and in a refined judgment, You are best able of all to take up this Patronage. Furthermore, since Your many and great merits toward me exist,

# DEDICATION

I considered it a mark of ingratitude—a vice for which most mortals in this perverse age are now ill-reputed—if I were to let slip this natural opportunity and not dedicate this distinguished Author to Your name above all as a form of repayment <note>original: "redhostimenti"; a formal word for a reciprocal gift or compensation for favors received</note>. Therefore, I pray that Your Generosity <note>original: "Generositas"; used here as a formal honorific title for the patron</note> might receive with a serene countenance this work, whatever its worth may be; it is indeed small in physical size, yet in the **Spagyric art** <term>Spagyric</term> <note>referring to alchemy, specifically the chemical preparation of medicines by separating and then recombining the "pure" essences of natural substances</note> it is of the greatest importance and most beneficial.

Greater things will follow, with a more favorable fortune. In the meantime, do not grow weary of extending for countless years those streams of Your favor with which You have thus far irrigated my meager circumstances. # The Keepers of the Vatican Library
## wish true wisdom to the
## Students of Alchemy.

**S**ince *many followers of alchemy <note>original: "Alchymiæ"; the medieval forerunner of chemistry, focused on the transformation of matter</note> of every class are found in these times, who investigate the hidden causes of natural philosophy so that they might elicit the Stone—commonly called "of the Philosophers" <note>original: "Lapis Philosophorum"; a legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting immortality</note>—from winding paths of paper, and wring it from the hands of Arnold <note>original: "Reynoldi"; likely referring to the famous medieval physician and alchemist Arnold of Villanova</note>, Raymond <note>original: "Raymundi"; referring to Raymond Lully (Ramon Llull), a philosopher to whom many alchemical works were attributed</note>, Geber <note>original: "Gebri"; Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational figure of Islamic alchemy</note>, and several Monks—not to mention even the highest Theologians—the result is that the more clever ones end by interpreting perversely, the more inclined ones by affirming ambiguities, the more dull-witted by believing in Chimeras <note>original: "Chymæras"; here used to mean impossible or delusional fantasies</note>, the more eager ones by experimenting, and the lavish ones by pouring out and wasting their wealth. They are brought to this end: that they consume their time, promise vain things, babble bombastic words, and are held as a laughingstock and a joke by everyone, so that at last, in place of the Stone* <note>The text ends mid-sentence here; the next page likely describes the disappointing reality they find instead of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

<summary>This preface from the Vatican Library's keepers critiques the various types of alchemists who waste their lives and money chasing the Philosopher's Stone through confusing texts.</summary>
<keywords>Vatican Library, Alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Arnold of Villanova, Raymond Lully, Geber, scientific skepticism, 17th-century science</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>The text continues the preface by the "Keepers of the Vatican Library" addressed to students of alchemy. It follows a list of errors made by those who misinterpret the foundational texts of the art, ending with the previous page’s description of those who waste their wealth on false promises.</meta>

[stupidly] squander their money: hence an art so rare, indeed a science so supernatural, is held and believed to be an illusion and mere deception. Nor is it a wonder, since the students of this divine invention assiduously read Geber <note>original: "Gebrum"; Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most influential figure in medieval alchemy</note> and admire him as their own Aristarchus <note>original: "Aristarchum"; a reference to Aristarchus of Samothrace, a famous critical scholar of Homer, used here to mean an ultimate authority</note>. They cling to him, keep watch over him, and devote themselves to him, just as Alexander of Macedon was accustomed to do with the *Education of Cyrus* <note>original: "pediæ Cyri"; Xenophon’s biography of Cyrus the Great, which Alexander the Great reportedly kept under his pillow</note>. And since that [text] overflows with infinite errors, and is carried about as something blind, crippled, or even distorted—and like a Scyllan Satyr <note>original: "Satyrus Scyllanus"; likely a reference to a harsh or monstrous figure from myth, representing incoherent speech</note>, he does not speak but gnashes his teeth; he does not teach but un-teaches, wrapping and overturning the minds of those who read him in a thick fog. We have decided to come to the aid of your vain labors and expenses by healing his wounds, applying an eye-salve <note>original: "collyrium"; a medicinal lotion for the eyes, used here metaphorically to represent clarity or truth</note> to bleary eyes, eliminating the imbalance of humors <note>original: "dyscrasiam"; a medical <term>term</term> for an unhealthy mixture of the body's elements, here referring to the corruption of the alchemical texts</note>, and restoring more beautiful parts to his truncated limbs (as was done for Pelops) <note>In Greek mythology, Pelops was restored to life with an ivory shoulder after being cut into pieces</note>, so that at last, like Medea’s boiled ram <note>Medea demonstrated her power of rejuvenation by turning an old ram into a young lamb by boiling it in a cauldron</note>, [it might] be trans-

<summary>The authors critique the current state of alchemy, blaming corrupt and confusing texts—specifically those of Geber—for leading students astray. They promise to "heal" and restore these texts to their true, clear form to prevent further financial and intellectual ruin.</summary>

...stupidly squander their money. From this, it follows that an art so rare—or rather, a science so supernatural—is held and believed to be an illusion and a mere deception. Nor is it a surprise, since the students of this divine invention read Geber <note>original: "Gebrum"; Jabir ibn Hayyan, the 8th-century "father of chemistry" whose works were central to medieval alchemy</note> incessantly and admire him as if he were their own Aristarchus <note>Aristarchus of Samothrace was a famous ancient critic and scholar; here, it means they treat Geber as an infallible authority</note>. They cling to him, stay awake over him, and devote themselves to him, just as Alexander the Great <note>original: "Alexander Macedo"</note> was accustomed to do with the *Education of Cyrus* <note>original: "pediae Cyri"; Xenophon’s biography of Cyrus the Great, which Alexander famously kept under his pillow</note>. And since that author [Geber] overflows with infinite errors, and is circulated in a form that is blind, crippled, and indeed distorted—and like a Satyr of Scylla <note>original: "Satyrus Scyllanus"; a reference to a confusing or monstrous figure that makes noise rather than sense</note>, he does not speak but gnashes his teeth; he does not teach but "un-teaches," enveloping and overturning the minds of his readers in a thick fog. We have decided to meet your vain labors and expenses by healing his wounds, applying eye-salve <term>collyrium: an ancient medicinal lotion for treating eye ailments</term> to bleary eyes, eliminating the imbalance <term>dyscrasiam: a medical term for an unhealthy mixture of the body's "humors"</term>, and by restoring more beautiful limbs to the severed parts (just as was done for Pelops <note>In Greek mythology, Pelops was butchered but restored to life by the gods with a shoulder made of ivory</note>), so that finally, like the ram boiled by Medea <note>The sorceress Medea demonstrated her power to restore youth by boiling an old ram with magic herbs, from which a young lamb emerged</note>, it might be made—

...[to be purified], and to become young again, to be animated, and to shine, and to seem entirely restored to himself, by bringing forth Machaon <note>original: "Machaonem"; in Greek mythology, Machaon was a master healer and the son of Asclepius. He was one of the heroes hidden inside the Trojan Horse.</note> from the **Vatican Library**, just as if from the Trojan Horse. He who, toiling over this cure, has kept watch most attentively, and—rescuing what was given up for dead <note>original: "conclamatum"; a Roman term for a person whose death has been formally announced after ritual shouting, signifying they are beyond hope.</note> from the gates of **Proserpina** <note>The Queen of the Underworld; here representing the "death" or loss of the original text.</note>—has restored it to life, light, purity, and to you; therefore, show favor to this charming man, and admire him as he discourses so learnedly and profoundly. 

His words promise and bestow secret doctrine; his thoughts, pearls; and his precepts, pure gold <note>original: "aurum obrizum"; high-quality, refined gold often mentioned in the Bible and alchemical texts.</note>. Furthermore, how much you—most of whom are seekers of wealthy nature—must owe to our diligence (for we have provided the lamp of **Cleanthes** <note>original: "Cleantis lucernam"; Cleanthes was a Stoic philosopher who worked as a water-carrier at night to study philosophy, making his "lamp" a symbol of late-night, hard-won wisdom.</note> to you who walk through **Cimmerian darkness** <note>original: "Cimmerias tenebras"; a mythological eternal darkness said to shroud the land of the Cimmerians.</note>, and we have led you out from the **cave of Trophonius** <note>original: "antrum Trophony"; an oracle in Greece where visitors reportedly became so terrified they lost the ability to laugh; here it symbolizes the confusion and despair of studying "blind" alchemical texts.</note> where you were cast down, and brought you into the light) if ever [the hard-won fruits] of such a varied, such a hidden, such a heavenly treasure...

...to be cleansed, and to grow young again, to be animated and shine, and to seem restored entirely to itself, being brought forth from the Vatican Library just as Machaon <note>original: "Machaonem"; in Greek mythology, the son of Asclepius and a master physician who was among the heroes hidden in the Trojan Horse</note> was from the Trojan Horse. He who, sweating over this cure, has watched over it most attentively, and rescuing it from the very gates of Proserpina <note>original: "januis Proserpinæ"; Proserpina was the Queen of the Underworld, here representing the "death" or loss of the manuscript</note> after it had been given up for dead, has restored it to life, light, and brilliance—and to you: show favor, therefore, to this charming man, and admire him as he discourses so learnedly and profoundly. His words promise and bestow secret doctrine; his sentences, pearls; and his precepts, pure gold <note>original: "aurum obrizum"; a technical term for gold refined to the highest possible purity</note>.

How much you—most of whom are seekers of the riches of nature—owe to our diligence! For you who were walking through Cimmerian darkness <note>original: "Cimmerias tenebras"; a proverbial term for absolute darkness, referring to a mythical people said to live in perpetual mist and gloom</note>, we have provided the lamp of Cleanthes <note>original: "Cleantis lucernam"; Cleanthes was a Stoic philosopher who worked at night to support his studies; his lamp symbolizes the light of persistent intellectual labor</note>, and you who were cast down into the cave of Trophonius <note>original: "antrum Trophony"; a subterranean Greek oracle where visitors were said to be overwhelmed by what they saw; here it represents the confusion of false alchemical texts</note>, we have led out and brought into the light. If ever [the enjoyment] of such a diverse, such a hidden, such a heavenly treasure labored over... <meta>The catchword "sudata" (labored/sweated) suggests the next page continues the sentence with "desudata."</meta>

<summary>The authors describe their restoration of a corrupted alchemical manuscript from the Vatican Library, comparing the effort to a medical rescue from the underworld. They present the corrected text as a guiding light for students previously lost in the darkness of confusing and fraudulent sources.</summary>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page concludes the editors' preface. It continues the sentence from the previous page, shifting from the metaphor of the "Cave of Trophonius" to an appeal to the reader to value this newly restored edition of Geber's work.</meta>

...[(once this) hard-won knowledge <note>original: "sudata cognitio"; literally "sweated-over knowledge," referring to the intense physical and mental labor required to master alchemical secrets.</note> has reached you] you shall be the judges. In the meantime, read this often: drink it in and labor over it, and in your boldness keep the Gods appeased, so that this most secret truth—which has been hidden by so many lies and obscurities, called by every name in the world, and found by few in books yet always beloved; perceived by even fewer through a teacher, yet never abandoned; and infused into the very few through divine revelation, yet never despaired of—may finally be opened to you. 

So that you may hold and give thanks to the most thoroughly corrected copy from the **Vatican Library**, from which this most ornate edition has come forth, and to our labor undertaken on your behalf, acquire this new and complete **Geber** <note>original: "Gebrum"; referring to Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–c. 813), the legendary Islamic polymath and "father of chemistry" whose Latinized works were the bedrock of Western alchemy.</note>, a most rich gold-mine <note>original: "aurifodinarum"; literally a place where gold is dug.</note>, at any price. 

->And so, Farewell.<-

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page serves as the "Errata" or list of corrections for the preceding text. It follows the Index of Chapters, ensuring that scholars and practitioners using this alchemical manual have the most accurate version of the instructions and philosophical arguments, as even a small error in a chemical recipe or a philosophical term could lead to significant misunderstanding.</meta>

<image-desc>A small decorative typographic ornament featuring a central circle with the character "o" inside, flanked by symmetrical leafy flourishes.</image-desc>

## Errors to be corrected.

In the Dedication, second page, line 10: instead of **Quantum à** <note>original: "Quantum à"; meaning: "How much from"</note>, read **Quantum autem** <note>original: "Quantum autem"; meaning: "How much, however"</note>.

Page 1, line 8: instead of **we abbreviate** <note>original: "abbreviamus"</note>, read **we have abbreviated** <note>original: "abbreviavimus"</note>.

| Page | Line | Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 9 | 26 | instead of **of the tides**, read **your bronze**. <note>original: "pro æstuum, l. æs tuum." The printer likely mistook the spacing between the words "aes" (bronze/copper) and "tuum" (your).</note> |
| 12 | 4 | instead of **refista**, read **this thing**. <note>original: "pro refifta, l. res ista." Another spacing error combined with the "long s" (f) which looks like an 'f'.</note> |
| 16 | 14 | instead of **strong** [neuter], read **strong** [masculine]. <note>original: "pro forte, l. fortem." A grammatical correction of the case ending.</note> |
| 39 | 27 | instead of **illiustrates**, read **illustrates**. <note>original: "pro illiuftrat, l. illuftrat." A correction of a double "i" and the long-s typography.</note> |
| 49 | 3 | instead of **him/it**, read **her/it**. <note>original: "pro eum, l. eam." In alchemical texts, pronouns often refer to specific substances (like 'Mercury' or 'the Stone') which have assigned genders.</note> |
| 56 | 12 | instead of **they may be removed**, read **it may be removed**. <note>original: "pro removantur, l. removeatur." A correction from plural to singular.</note> |
| 59 | 10 | instead of **refixa**, read **with the matter fixed**. <note>original: "pro refixa, l. re fixa." Correction of word spacing; "fixed matter" is a crucial state in alchemical processing.</note> |
| 83 | 19 | instead of **which**, read **because**. <note>original: "pro qua, l. quia."</note> |
| 95 | 19 | instead of **which** [nominative], read **which** [accusative]. <note>original: "pro quæ, l. quas."</note> |
| 106 | 11 | instead of **not not**, read **not**. <note>original: "pro non non, l. non." A simple repetition error by the typesetter.</note> |
| 122 | 11 | — — — read **never**. <note>original: "l. nunquam."</note> |
| 138 | 30 | instead of **double-**, read **etc.** <note>original: "pro duplic- l. &c."</note> |
| 139 | 21 | instead of **passing through**, read **to be passed through**. <note>original: "pro transeundem, l. transeundum." A grammatical correction of the gerundive form.</note> |
| 157 | 27 | — — — read **of Silver**. <note>original: "l. Argenti."</note> |
| 199 | 13 | instead of **this** [singular], read **these/this**. <note>original: "pro hoc, l. hæc."</note> |
| 218 | 14 | instead of **impassable**, read **impalpable**. <note>original: "pro impallabilis, l. impalpabilis." A correction of a misspelling; "impalpable" refers to a powder so fine it cannot be felt.</note> |
| 229 | 15 | instead of **persectum**, read **perfect**. <note>original: "pro persectum, l. perfectum." The printer accidentally used an 's' (long s) instead of an 'f'.</note> |
| 238 | 22 | instead of **Two**, read **I say**. <note>original: "pro Duo, l. Dico."</note> |

## OF THE FIRST BOOK
# OF THE SUM OF GEBER
### *The most clear-sighted Philosopher*
### Concerning
# THE PERFECTION OF THE MAGISTERY
## IN ITS OWN NATURE,
### *PREFACE.*

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'T' featuring ornate foliate designs and two intertwined avian or serpentine figures.</image-desc>

All our knowledge <note>original: "scientiam"</note>, which we have condensed from the sayings of the ancients through various compilations in our previous volumes, we shall here reduce into a single summary. Whatever was left incomplete in the books written by us, we have sufficiently compensated for in this tradition of our current book, and we have supplied what was lacking with a brief explanation. What was kept hidden by us in one place, we have made manifest in that same place within this volume, so that the completion of such an excellent and noble branch of Philosophy may be revealed to the Wise. Know therefore, dearest son, that in this work, the entire operation of the <term>Art</term> <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy—the experimental and philosophical practice of transforming matter.</note> is sufficiently contained in general chapters and through universal discussion, without any omission. By God, he who shall have worked according to this book, tr- <meta>The page ends mid-word; the catchword "rum" indicates the next page begins with "verum," meaning "truly."</meta>

...truly <note>original: "verum"</note> shall rejoice at having discovered the goal of this art. But know, most dear son, that whoever is ignorant of the natural principles within himself is already far removed from our art: because he does not have a true root upon which to ground his intention. And he who knows his natural principles and all their causes, but has not yet **ATTAINED** <note>original: "ADEPTUS"—in alchemy, an "Adept" is one who has successfully mastered the secret of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> the true end and profit of this most hidden art, nevertheless has an easier path to the principles of the art—even if ignorance clouds his understanding regarding the method of our <term>Work</term> <note>The "Work" (Latin: *opus*) refers to the actual laboratory and philosophical process of alchemical transformation.</note>. Such a person is only a little distance from the entrance of the art.

But he who knows the principles of nature, the causes of <term>minerals</term> <note>Alchemists studied how metals and minerals "grew" in the earth to try and replicate the process in the lab.</note>, and the way they are generated (which aligns with the intention of nature): very little is withheld from him regarding the completion of the work, without which our science cannot be perfected. For the Art cannot imitate nature's work in every single detail, but imitates it as far as it is rightly able. 

Therefore, most dear son, we reveal this secret to you: craftsmen often fail because they want their actions to imitate nature in every specific difference of its properties. Therefore, labor to study our volumes; turn them over in your mind frequently, so that you may gain a true understanding from our words. In them you will find the foundation upon which to ground your mind, and

...[nd] so that you may know how to repel errors from yourself by using them, in those things where you are able to imitate nature in the craftsmanship of your work.

Therefore, let us first briefly set down all the **obstacles** by which the craftsman is hindered in the work, lest he reach the true end. We shall speak of those qualities which the practitioner of this art ought to possess within himself; and first, we shall argue against the ignorant and the **Sophists** <note>In alchemical literature, "Sophists" were those who used false logic or deceptive recipes to deny the true possibility of metal transformation.</note> who—because of their own ignorance and inability to seek the profit of this art's **Mastery** <term>Mastery</term> <note>Latin: *Magisterium*. This refers to the completed alchemical process, the "Magistery" or the production of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>—destroy the art and claim it does not exist.

We will therefore set forth all their arguments, and afterwards we will most clearly demolish them: so that it may be made plain enough to the wise that their sophistries contain no truth. Secondly, we will discuss **natural principles**, which concern the intent of nature, and likewise the manner of their generation and mixing with one another through the work of nature. Furthermore, we will subsequently discuss the effects according to the opinion of the ancient **Philosophers** <note>Original: *Philosophorum*. Alchemists viewed themselves as natural philosophers who studied the hidden laws of the physical world.</note>. Fourthly, however, we will relate the Principles which relate to the intent of this work of ours: in which we are able to imitate nature, and the method of mixing and altering according to the course of nature, bringing its causes back to the focus of our own work.

## *On the obstacles by which the*
## *craftsman of this work is hindered.*

### CHAPTER I.

There are, therefore, two types of obstacles that arise in this work: natural inability, and a lack of the necessary funds or the distraction of labor <note>The author suggests that poverty or being too busy with other work prevents the focus required for alchemy.</note>.

However, we say that natural inability is manifold. Specifically, it can exist on the part of the craftsman’s physical **organs** <term>Organs</term> <note>Latin: *organi*. In medieval philosophy, "organs" refers to the physical body parts—such as the eyes, brain, or hands—through which the soul acts and perceives.</note>, and on the part of the soul itself. Regarding the craftsman’s organs, it is manifold: for example, the organ may be weak or entirely corrupted. Regarding the inability of the soul, it is also manifold: either because the soul is perverse within its organs, possessing no uprightness or reason because of those faulty organs—such as the soul of an insane or foolish person—or because the soul is "fanciful" <note>original: *fantastica*. This refers to a mind dominated by unreliable mental images or "phantasms" rather than clear logic.</note>, being easily and improperly receptive to contradictory forms, jumping suddenly from one known thing to its opposite, or from a universal truth to its contrary based on mere appearances.

### CHAPTER II.

We have now defined the obstacles of this work for you in general terms. Now, however, we address you in more specific language in this chapter, and more clearly; we shall describe all these obstacles to you most fully and in sequence. We say, then, that if a person does not have his organs fully functioning, he will not be able to reach the completion of this work—as, for example, if he were blind, or in

...mutilated in his extremities <note>The text concludes a point from the previous page: if a person is missing hands or fingers, they cannot perform the delicate work of the laboratory.</note>, since he is not assisted by the limbs through which this art is perfected, which serve as nature's ministers. By these natural incapacities of the body, therefore, the craftsman is hindered in his intention.

## *On the obstacles on the part of the soul.*

### CHAPTER III.

We have set before you one chapter in which we described, in plain language, the manifest obstacles depending on the craftsman’s body. It remains now to describe the obstacles on the part of the soul, which are the greatest obstacles against the completion of this work.

We say, therefore, that he who does not possess a natural talent <term>Talent</term> <note>Latin: *ingenium*. In this period, *ingenium* referred to an innate, natural ability to understand and invent.</note>, and a soul that subtly scrutinizes natural principles and the foundations of nature—as well as the crafts that can follow nature in the properties of its actions—will not find the true root of this most precious science. Such are many who are "stiff-necked" <note>original: *duram habent cervicem*. This is a biblical idiom used here to describe those who are stubborn, dull-witted, or unteachable.</note> and empty of all ingenious insight; these are people who can scarcely understand common logic, and similarly learn to manage common, everyday works only with great difficulty. Among these, we also find many to have a flighty soul...

...any imagining opinion whatsoever <note>This sentence fragment completes the thought from the previous page: "an [opinionated person who follows] any imagining opinion..."</note>. But what they believe to have found to be the truth is entirely fantastical, deviant from reason, full of error, and remote from natural principles; for their brain, being filled with many smoky vapors <term>Vapors</term> <note>original: *fumositátibus*. In medieval physiology, "fumosities" were gases produced by digestion or bodily humors that were thought to rise to the brain, clouding the intellect and causing confusion or hallucinations.</note>, cannot receive the true understanding <note>original: *intentionem*</note> of natural things.

There are also others besides these who have a fickle soul, moving from opinion to opinion and from will to will; like those who for a moment believe this thing and desire it without any foundation of reason, but a little while later believe and desire something else. These are so changeable that they can hardly complete even the smallest part of what they intend, but instead leave it unfinished <note>original: *diminutum*, literally "diminished."</note>.

There are similarly others who cannot perceive any truth in natural things any more than a beast—such as the mentally impaired, the insane, and children. There are also others who despise science and do not believe it exists; science similarly despises them and repels them from the goal of this most precious work.

And there are others who are slaves to money. Although they desire this science and affirm it to be wonderful and true, they are afraid to incur the necessary expenses. Therefore, even if they affirm it and investigate it according to reason, they never reach the practical experience <term>Experience</term> <note>original: *experientiam*. In this context, "experience" refers specifically to hands-on laboratory practice and experimental verification, distinguishing it from "theory."</note> of the work because of their greed for money. To these people, therefore, our science does not come. For how shall he who is ignorant, or has neglected to investigate science, arrive at it?

## *On the obstacles arising from accidental chance.*
### CHAPTER IV

**W**e have reduced the obstacles of this art to two chapters; these are the things that delay the final goal and which arise from radical principles <note>In this context, "radical principles" refers to the fundamental physical or intellectual makeup of the practitioner.</note> according to the nature of the practitioner of this precious business. It is therefore expedient to only recount the obstacles arising from the outside, coming from accidental contingencies and chances by which this glorious work is hindered. 

We have seen, therefore, certain clever and ingenious people, by no means ignorant of the works of nature, following nature herself wherever possible in principles and works. Their investigation is not fantastical in all things regulated by the actions of nature beneath the lunar circle <note>original: *infra lunarem circulum*. In medieval science, the "sublunary circle" refers to the Earthly realm—everything below the orbit of the moon—which was the only place where change, decay, and the transformation of elements could occur.</note>. These people, however, weighed down by extreme poverty and a lack of resources, are compelled to set aside this most excellent <term>Mastery</term> <note>original: *Magisterium*. This is a technical term for the entire process of the Great Work or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. There are also many others (besides those just mentioned) who are curious but held back by various cares and anxieties of this world, occupying themselves entirely with every secular business; from such people, this precious science of ours flees. Now, therefore, it is sufficiently clear to you from the preceding chapters what the obstacles are that withdraw one from this art.

[...ple, it is necessary for the] natural [practitioner] to be learned and advanced. For no matter how naturally deep an intellect he possesses, and even if he has acquired a desire for this <term>artifice</term> <note>original: *artificio*. Here referring to the alchemical craft or the "Great Work."</note> and has mastered <term>natural philosophy</term> <note>The study of nature and the physical universe, which in the Middle Ages and Renaissance was the precursor to modern science.</note>, he will still not reach its goal except through <term>doctrine</term> <note>original: *doctrina*. This refers to formal instruction, the study of authoritative texts, and the transmission of established knowledge from a teacher.</note>. This is because what is not attained through natural talent is supplemented in its deficiency by teaching. It is also necessary for the practitioner to be aided by the deepest scrutiny and <term>natural industry</term> <note>original: *industria naturali*. In this context, "industry" refers to a combination of diligence, practical skill, and the clever application of one's natural wits to a specific task.</note>. For no matter how much knowledge he has acquired through teaching, unless he is aided by natural industry, he will not be invited to such most precious feasts. 

In a critical moment, he might correct his error through his own industry—a remedy he would not know how to apply if he were grounded in teaching alone. Similarly, he might correct an error in a moment through the knowledge acquired from teaching, which he could not avoid by natural industry alone. Thus, art is aided by intellect, and intellect is aided by art in return. 

*It is likewise necessary for him to be of a constant will in the work, so that he does not presume to try now this, now that; for our art is not brought to perfection through a multitude of different things.* For there is one stone and one medicine in which the <term>Mastery</term> <note>original: *magisterium*. The successful completion of the alchemical process, resulting in the Philosopher's Stone.</note> consists. To this we add no foreign substance, nor do we take anything away, *except that in its preparation we remove what is superfluous.* 

Therefore, he must apply himself diligently to the work until its very completion, so that he does not leave the work cut short; for he will acquire neither knowledge nor profit from an incomplete work, but rather despair and loss <note>original: *dam[num]*. The text cuts off here at the end of the page, with the catchword "num" indicating the completion of the word *damnum*, meaning loss or damage.</note>

# THE BOOK

It is also expedient that the practitioner should not be ignorant of the principles and primary roots of this art, which belong to the very essence of the work; for he who ignores the principles will not find the end. And we shall tell you all those principles in a full discourse, which is also sufficiently open and manifest to the prudent, according to the requirements of this our art. 

Therefore, it is likewise expedient to be temperate and sparing in anger, lest, by a sudden impulse of wrath, he scatter and destroy what has already been begun <note>Alchemical processes often required months of steady heat; a single moment of frustration leading to the breaking of a vessel or the quenching of a furnace would ruin the entire endeavor.</note>. 

Similarly, let him guard his money, lest he distribute it vainly and presumptuously; so that if, by chance, he does not find the art, he is not left in the misery and desperation of poverty. Or lest perhaps, when he has already approached the end through his <term>investigation</term> <note>original: *indagationem*. A systematic search or inquiry into the secrets of nature.</note>, his funds are consumed, and the wretched man is forced by poverty to abandon the true goal—just as those who, being ignorant in the beginning, squander their entire treasure. 

When such people have reached their limits, they have nothing further with which to work. Hence it is said that such people are buried in sorrow: both because they squandered their money on useless things, and because they lose that most noble knowledge (which they would have otherwise tracked down). 

For you ought not to consume your goods, since you will reach the completion of the <term>mastery</term> <note>original: *magisterium*. A technical term for the successful completion of the alchemical process or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> at a low cost, provided you are not ignorant of the principles of the art which we have handed down to you, and have understood them correctly. If, therefore, you lose your <term>money</term> <note>original: *æs*. Literally "bronze" or "copper," but commonly used in Latin to refer to money or physical wealth.</note> by not paying attention to our warnings—which we have written for you openly and clearly in this booklet—do not unjustly gnaw at us, nor bring blasphemies against us, but attribute it to your own ignorance and presumption. Therefore, this knowledge...

knowledge <note>original: *doctrinam*. For most wretched and unhappy is he to whom God, after the end of his work and labor, denies the sight of the truth: because he always concludes and ends the span of his life in sorrow. Such a man, established in perpetual labor and besieged by every misfortune and unhappiness, loses all consolation, joy, and delight of this world, and consumes his life in sorrow without profit. *Similarly, you should study, when you are engaged in the work, to seal in your mind all the signs that appear in any **decoction** <term>decoctione</term> <note>The process of heating or "cooking" a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or change its state.</note>, and to inquire into their causes.* These, therefore, are the things which are necessary for a craftsman fit for our art. But if any of those things we have recounted are lacking, he should not involve himself in this art.

### On the Persuasions of Sophists.
#### C A P. VI.

Since in the summary of this our book we have already set forth all the impediments of this work, and the in- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; likely "instruction" or "doctrine" (*doctrinam*).</note>

<summary>The author warns that alchemy requires divine favor and warns that pursuing deceptive "sophistical" methods leads to spiritual and financial ruin. He emphasizes the need for a practitioner to be observant of all physical changes during the heating process.</summary>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the practical advice on observing signs during decoction (heating) to a theoretical defense of alchemy. The author moves from instruction to a logical dispute against those who claim the "Great Work" is impossible, particularly focusing on the "Sophists" who argue that humans cannot replicate nature's secret proportions.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>

-># BOOK I<-

Because we have given you enough **instruction** <note>original: *Doctrinam*. This refers to the theoretical and practical training provided in the preceding sections.</note> for you to firmly grasp the art, it is now appropriate, according to the intention of our purpose, for us to dispute against the **Sophists** <note>In this context, "Sophists" refers to critics or false philosophers who use clever but flawed logic to argue that alchemy is impossible.</note> and the ignorant. First, we shall set forth their own arguments, according to the promise we made at the beginning to determine these matters. Finally, by following their logic, we shall demonstrate to the wise through clear proof that their arguments contain no truth.

There are various people who deny and attempt to destroy the art. Some do so simply out of ignorance, while others strive to reject it based on premises derived from a supposed cause. For there are some who assert that the art does not exist as we have described it, sophistically strengthening their opinion in the following way:

The species and diversities of things are distinct because the **proportions** <note>The specific ratio or "recipe" of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) believed to make up all matter.</note> of the elements mixed together are diverse and distinct from one another. For example, a donkey is different in species from a human because it possessed a very different proportion of elements in its composition. This same logic can be applied to all other diversities of things, and therefore also to minerals. 

They argue that if the proportion of the ingredients to be mixed is unknown—the very thing by which the form and perfection of a thing is achieved—then how can we know how to form the mixture or the substance to be mixed? But we are ignorant of the proportions of the **Sun** <note>original: *Solis*. In alchemical texts, "the Sun" is the standard term for Gold.</note> and the **Moon** <note>original: *Lunae*. The standard alchemical term for Silver.</note>, as well as the proportions of the elements within them. Therefore, they argue, we must be ignorant of how to form them. From these points, they conclude that this art is useless and impossible.

Similarly, they use another argument to destroy our **Mastery** <term>Magisterium</term> <note>The *Magisterium* refers to the highest skill of the alchemist or the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. They say: even if you did know the proportion of the elements, you are nonetheless ignorant of the manner in which they are mixed together; for nature procreates these things in caverns, mines, and hidden places. Therefore, since you are ignorant of the manner of their mixing, and

<meta>The text cuts off here, continuing with the catchword "hoc" (this) on the next page.</meta>

<summary>The author begins a formal defense of alchemy by listing the skeptical arguments of "Sophists," who claim that because the exact elemental proportions of gold and silver are unknown and their natural formation is hidden deep within the earth, humans cannot possibly replicate them.</summary>
<keywords>Sophists, elemental proportions, gold (Sun), silver (Moon), Magisterium, mineral formation, alchemical theory</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Sophist" or skeptical arguments against alchemy. While page 11 focused on the impossibility of knowing the exact proportions of elements, page 12 moves to the impossibility of replicating natural heat and the argument from historical failure (i.e., that if alchemy were real, the great kings and philosophers of the past would have already succeeded).</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>

->## BOOK I<-

...you are singularly ignorant of how to achieve this. They argue further in a similar vein: Even if you were to know this [the proportions] properly, you would still be ignorant of how to balance the **active heat** <note>original: *calorem æquare agentem*. In alchemy, "balancing" the heat was considered the most difficult task—too much would "burn" the matter, and too little would leave it "cold" and lifeless.</note> during the process of mixing, which is the means by which this thing is perfected. For nature possesses a specific quantity of heat by which it brings metals into being, and you do not know its measure. Likewise, you are ignorant of other variations in nature’s active causes, without which nature cannot fulfill its purpose. Therefore, since these things are unknown, the entire method of the art’s operation will also be unknown.

Furthermore, I offer you an argument based on experience. This science has been sought for so long by wise men that if it were possible to reach it by any path, they would have completed it thousands of times by now. Likewise, since the **Philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophi*. In this context, it refers to the great authorities like Aristotle or Hermes Trismegistus, whose writings were often intentionally obscure.</note> did not dare to pass it down in their books, and we find no clear truth in them, it is probable enough that this science does not exist. Similarly, many of this world's Princes and Kings, who possessed infinite treasure and a multitude of scholars, desired to discover this art; yet they were never able to reach the fruit of this most precious art. This is surely a sufficient argument that the art is **frivolous** <note>original: *frivola*. Used here to mean something that is empty, groundless, or a fool's errand.</note>.

Likewise, we cannot imitate nature even in the "weak" mixtures of species. For instance, we are unable to follow nature in forming a donkey **organized** <note>original: *organizatum*. Referring to the biological "organization" of a living body's organs and systems.</note> according to its natural state. We do not know how to fashion a donkey or similar creatures, whose compositions are weak and almost obvious to our senses. Therefore, we are even less able to understand the **mixture of metals** <term>metallorum mixtio: the complex elemental ratio believed to give a metal its specific properties, like weight and color</term>, which is a most powerful and dense mixture...

<summary>This page lists several skeptical arguments against alchemy: the human inability to control heat like nature does, the lack of clarity in philosophical texts, and the failure of wealthy kings to achieve the Great Work. The author uses the analogy that if humans cannot even create a simple animal like a donkey, they certainly cannot create complex metals.</summary>

While the previous page focused on the difficulty of duplicating nature's heat and the historical failure of kings to find the stone, this page argues that metals are distinct species (like animals) that cannot be changed into one another, and that the "tempered heat" required for their growth cannot be artificially accelerated without destroying them.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>BOOK.</header>

...we will remain ignorant; which is even deeply hidden from our senses and experiences: a sign of which is the difficulty of dissolving metals into their constituent parts. Similarly, we do not see an **ox** transmuted into a **goat**, nor any other **species** <note>original: *speciem*. In medieval science, species were considered fixed by nature. A common argument against alchemy was that lead and gold were as different as a cow and a goat.</note> reduced into another by means of artifice. How, therefore, since metals differ in species, can you strive to transform them from one to another according to their species, so that you make one species out of another? This seems absurd enough to us, and remote from both the given truth and natural principles.

Similarly, nature perfects metals over thousands of years; how could you, in the artifice of transmutation, endure for thousands of years, when you are scarcely able to reach the age of a hundred years? 

If, however, it were answered in such a way that what nature cannot perfect in a very long span of time, we fulfill in a short time through our artifice—because artifice in many things completes the deficiency of nature—we say that this is impossible in metals. This is especially true since they are made of very subtle **vapors** <note>original: *fumi*. Alchemists believed metals grew from sulfurous and mercurial vapors "thickening" underground.</note>, which require a **tempered decoction** <note>original: *temperata decoctione*. A slow, steady "cooking" or boiling process regulated by the earth's internal heat.</note>, so that the humidity within them may be thickened according to a degree of equality, and not flee from them and leave them deprived of all moisture. Similarly, they [metals] undergo striking and stretching <note>This refers to the physical properties of metals, such as malleability and ductility.</note>. 

If therefore you wish to shorten nature's time of decoction in mineral and metallic bodies through your artifice, it will be necessary to do this through an **excess of heat**. However, this heat will not be balanced; rather, by dissolving the humidity, it will dissipate and destroy it from their bodies. For only tempered heat is the [agent] of humidity...

### *On the natural principles upon which*
### *nature founds its actions.*
## CHAPTER VII.

We say indeed that the principles upon which nature founds its action are of the hardest and strongest composition: and they are **Sulphur** and **Quicksilver** <term>Sulphur and Quicksilver: In medieval alchemy, these were not just the common elements, but "philosophical" principles representing the active/combustible (Sulphur) and passive/metallic (Quicksilver) qualities of all metals.</term> (as certain Philosophers say). ...thickening, and perfecting the mixture, without exceeding its bounds. Similarly, being and perfection are granted by the stars, as if by the prime movers and finishers of nature, attending to the generation and corruption <note>original: "generationis & coruptionis" – a standard Aristotelian phrase referring to the birth and decay of all physical things.</note>—and the non-being—of species. This, moreover, happens suddenly and in an instant when one or more stars, through their movements, reach a determined position in the firmament from which the perfection of being is granted. This is because every single thing, from a certain alignment of the stars, acquires its existence in a moment; and there is not just one single position, but rather many, differing from one another, just as their effects are diverse.

Similarly, this is the order in natural things: since it is easier to destroy than to build, yet we can hardly destroy **Gold**; how then can we build it so quickly? For these aforementioned sophistical <term>Sophistical: referring here to "Sophists," thinkers who use clever but fallacious arguments to deny the truth of alchemy.</term> reasons, and others less apparent than these, they believe they can destroy this divine art. All these aforementioned persuasions belong to the Sophists, who simply deny our art.

### *On the natural principles upon which nature bases its actions.*
### CHAPTER VII.

We say, certainly, that the principles upon which nature founds its action are of the toughest and strongest composition; these are Sulphur and <term>Quicksilver<note>original: "Argentum vivum," literally "living silver," known today as Mercury.</note></term> (as certain Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the long lineage of alchemical and natural history authorities, such as Geber or Aristotle.</note> say). Because they are of the hardest composition and the most difficult to break down, their thickening together and hardening is such that they can be compressed and stretched by a mallet without shattering. This is only possible because the <term>viscous moisture<note>A "sticky" or oily humidity that was thought to act as a glue within minerals.</note></term> is preserved during their mutual mixing through a gradual and long-lasting <term>thickening<note>original: "inspissatio."</note></term>, and through a very balanced <term>cooking<note>original: "decoctio."</note></term> within the mine.

But we pass down to you a general rule, my son: „ no thickening of any moisture occurs, unless „ there is first an <gloss>evaporation</gloss> of the most subtle moist parts, „ and a preservation of the coarser moist parts, „ so that the moisture in the mixture overcomes the dry. „ And thus, when the dry and moist are mixed— „ so that the moist is tempered by the dry and the dry „ by the moist—this substance becomes <term>omniomera<note>original: "omniomera," from the Greek "homoiomeria." It refers to a substance that is uniform throughout, where every tiny part has the same nature as the whole.</note></term> „ in its parts; that is, in its parts it is homo- „

<page-num>16</page-num>
-><header>F I R S T</header><-

...-geneous <note>original: "genea," completing "homogenea" from the previous page.</note> and balanced between hard and soft, and capable of being **extended under impact** <note>This refers to malleability—the ability of a metal to be hammered into sheets without breaking.</note>. But this does not happen except through a long-lasting mixing of **viscous moisture** and fine earthy matter, down to the smallest particles, until the moisture becomes one with the dry, and the dry becomes one with the moisture. *And the <term>resolution<note>original: "resolutio." In this context, it refers to the evaporation or breaking down of a substance into its components.</note></term> of this subtle vapor does not happen suddenly, but rather gradually, over thousands of years; and this is because the substance of the principles is uniform in nature.* If, therefore, the evaporation of the excess moisture happened suddenly—since the moisture is not distinct from the dry due to the strong mixing—the moisture would evaporate along with the dry; thus the whole would vanish into smoke. Nor could the moisture be separated from the dry during that breakdown, because of the strong union they have with one another.

We have indeed seen a clear example of this in the <term>sublimation of spirits<note>original: "spirituum sublimatione." In alchemy, "spirits" are volatile substances like sulfur, mercury, or ammonia that can be vaporized by heat and then solidified.</note></term>: for when a sudden evaporation occurs in them through sublimation, the moisture is not separated from the dry, nor the dry from the moisture, in such a way that their mixtures are totally divided into parts; rather, their whole substance rises, or only a small part of their components is dissolved.

Therefore, the gradual, long-lasting, and equal <term>evaporation<note>original: "resolutio."</note></term> of the subtle, smoky moisture is the cause of the <term>thickening<note>original: "inspissationis." The process of a liquid or vapor becoming a solid metal.</note></term> of metals. We cannot achieve this thickening in this same way. Therefore, in this matter, we are not able to follow nature. For we cannot imitate nature in every variation of its properties.

## *On the refutations of the Sophists’ arguments.*
### C H A P T E R VIII.

Our intention, therefore, is not to follow nature in its basic principles, nor in the proportion of the elements that can be mixed: nor in the manner of their mixing with one another: nor in the balancing of the **thickening heat** <note>original: "caloris inspissantis." This refers to the specific temperature required to "thicken" or solidify vapors into solid metal over long periods.</note>, since all these things are impossible for us and entirely unknown. It remains, then, to dismantle the arguments of the **Sophists** <note>original: "Sophistarum." In alchemical literature, "Sophists" were skeptics or false philosophers who used logical tricks to argue that the transmutation of metals was impossible.</note>, who deny that this exalted art can exist. 

If they say that we are ignorant of the proportion of elements, the way they mix together, the balancing of the heat that solidifies metals, and many other causes and circumstances that govern nature’s actions: we certainly admit this to them. But they do not, for this reason, destroy our **divine science** <note>original: "scientiam divinam." A common name for alchemy, suggesting it is a sacred knowledge revealed by God rather than a purely mundane craft.</note>; for we neither wish to know those things, nor are we able, nor can they be relevant to our work. 

Instead, we adopt this principle for ourselves: another method for the **generation of metals** <note>original: "generationis metallorum." The process by which metals are formed or "born."</note>, in which we *can* follow nature. If they also say that the **Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophos." Referring to the great authorities of natural history, such as Aristotle.</note> and the Great Leaders of this world have desired this science and failed to find it: we reply that they lie. For we have read that certain Leaders—though few, and particularly the ancient and wise ones, as well as some found in our own time—have already investigated this science through their own diligent effort. However, they did not wish to pass it on to such people, whether by word or by writing...

<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: lue-</meta>

<summary>The author defends alchemy against skeptics (Sophists) by admitting that while alchemists cannot replicate the exact geological processes of nature, they have discovered an alternative, valid method for creating metals. He asserts that the art is a "divine science" known to a select few wise men and ancient leaders who kept it secret.</summary>

[...fai]led, since they are unworthy. Therefore, because they have not seen anyone possess this **science** <note>original: "scientiam." Here meaning the specialized knowledge of alchemy.</note>, error has fallen upon their minds, leading them to believe that no one has ever discovered this knowledge. Toward this end, they also argue fancifully, asserting that our impotence is such that we cannot even imitate nature in **weak mixtures** <note>original: "debilis mixtionibus." In Aristotelian physics, "weak" mixtures refer to biological organisms which are easily dissolved or killed, whereas "strong" mixtures refer to durable minerals.</note>, such as in the mixture of an ass or an ox; therefore, they claim we cannot do so in strong mixtures either. 

We say that their error is manifold, because in their mode of arguing there is no logical necessity that forces us to concede that our art does not exist. They corroborate their fantasy and error through arguments based on similarity or by arguing from the greater to the lesser, which contain no necessity, but only contingency—as is the case in most of their claims. 

We also show this by another path, demonstrating that they assign no apparent similarity between the weak composition of the mixture of animals and the firm and strong forms of **minerals** <note>original: "mineralium."</note>. This is because, in animals and other living things where the composition is weak, the proportion of elements alone is not what completes the being. It is not merely the mixable parts of the proportion, nor the qualities of those mixables, nor the mixing that results from their mutual action and reaction <note>original: "actione... et passione." This refers to the active and passive qualities—like heat and cold—interacting to form a substance.</note> arising from the gathering of those qualities. Rather, it is the **soul** <term>anima: in this context, the "vital spark" or life force that animates a biological body, distinguished from the mere physical matter of minerals</term>, according to the opinion of many; this soul comes from the hidden things of nature, such as from the **quintessence** <term>essentia quinta: the "fifth element," a celestial substance distinct from earth, air, fire, and water</term>, or from the **prime mover** <term>primo motore: the ultimate source of motion or "First Cause" in the universe, often identified with God or the highest celestial sphere</term>. We say this according to the common sentiment, for we are ignorant of its hidden nature. Therefore, although there is a weak mixture in such living things, we nevertheless [do not] know how to complete them—

<summary>The author refutes the skeptical argument that if humans cannot create life (a "weak mixture"), they cannot create gold (a "strong mixture"). He argues that life requires a "soul" or "quintessence" which is hidden from man, whereas minerals are governed by physical proportions that the alchemist can master.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's rebuttal against the "Sophists" or skeptics who argue that alchemy is impossible because humans cannot create living animals. The author distinguishes between "weak" biological mixtures (which require a soul) and "strong" mineral mixtures (which only require the correct elemental proportion). <page-num>19</page-num>
<header>BOOK</header>

we know, because we do not know how to infuse the **perfecting principle** <note>original: "perfectivum." In medieval philosophy, this refers to the final form or quality that completes a thing's nature.</note>, which is the **soul** <note>original: "anima." Here used in the Aristotelian sense: the animating principle of a living body.</note>. Through this it is indeed clear: that our failure to produce an **ox** or a **goat** is not due to a defect in the **mixture** <term>mixtio: the physical blending of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water)</term>, but from the failure to infuse the soul. For we would know how to make both a weak and an even weaker, or a strong and an even stronger composition, if we were to imitate the natural path and course through our art. 

In metals, therefore, the perfection is less than in those living things: and their perfection is concerned more with **proportion** and **composition** than with other matters. Therefore, since there is a lesser perfection in them than in the other things we have described, we can more freely perfect them; but those living things we cannot. For God, the most high and glorious, has diversified these perfections among themselves in many ways. 

For in those things where the composition according to nature was **weak** <note>Meaning biological bodies, which decay easily.</note>, He placed a greater and more noble perfection, which is according to the soul. But He added certain things of a **stronger** and firmer composition, such as stones and minerals, but in them He placed a lesser and less noble perfection, which arises from the manner of the mixture. 

It is clear, therefore, that their comparison <note>The skeptics' comparison between creating an animal and creating gold.</note> is not good: because we are not ignorant that an ox or a goat exists not by reason of its composition, but by its **perfected form** <note>original: "formæ perfectæ."</note>, since the perfection in an ox or a goat is more noble and more hidden than that which consists in metals. Similarly, they also argue in another way, claiming that **species** <note>original: "species." In this context, the essential category of a substance, such as lead versus gold.</note> cannot be changed into another species. We say, therefore, that they lie once again, just as...

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of alchemy against skeptics. Having previously explained that humans cannot create life because they cannot provide a "soul," the author now argues that humans can still facilitate the transformation of non-living matter by acting as "ministers" or assistants to nature's own processes.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>BOOK ONE</header>

they are more often accustomed to saying something other than the truth concerning these matters—namely, that one **species** <term>species: here meaning a distinct type or category of substance, similar to the modern concept of a chemical element or biological class</term> is changed into another species according to this path, when an individual of one species is transformed into another species. For we see a **worm**, both naturally and through the craft of nature, changed into a **fly**, which differs from it in species; and a **strangled calf** transformed into **bees** <note>This refers to the ancient belief in "bugonia," where bees were thought to be spontaneously generated from the rotting carcasses of cattle.</note>; and **wheat** into **cockle** <note>original: "lolium." A common weed found in grain fields, often believed in the Middle Ages to be a degenerate form of wheat.</note>, and a **dog** transformed into a **worm** through the putrefaction of boiling <note>Likely a reference to the appearance of maggots in decaying or heated organic matter.</note>. 

But we do not do this; rather, **nature** does it, whom we serve. Similarly, we do not change **metals**, but nature does, for whom we prepare some matter through our craft, since she acts by herself and we are her administrators. 

And if they argue for another reason to reinforce their **sophistic opinion** <note>Sophistry refers to clever but false reasoning used to deceive or win an argument rather than find the truth.</note>: namely, that nature perfects metals over thousands of years, whereas you cannot survive for thousands of years. 

We say that nature, acting upon its own principles (according to the opinion of the ancient **Philosophers**), completes its work in thousands of years. But because we cannot follow those specific principles, it does not matter whether nature completes this in thousands of years, or more, or fewer, or in a single moment; their persuasion does not hold. This is because we cannot, nor do we wish to, imitate nature in its original principles. 

For we see that in thousands of years, nature does not cook **stones** into **lime** <note>original: "calcem." Lime was produced by heating limestone in a kiln, a foundational process in both construction and alchemy.</note>; yet a human does this in a short time by his natural ingenuity using **accidental heat** <term>accidental heat: artificial heat, such as that from a furnace, as opposed to the "essential" or "natural" heat of the sun or the earth's core</term>. Therefore

For indeed, in the purification of **metals**, through the ingenuity and skill of man, they are reduced to a pure and **nobler substance** <note>In alchemy, "noble" refers to metals like gold and silver that do not rust or decay.</note> in a short time, because nature cannot supply their deficiencies so quickly. We have already determined this in our previous, abbreviated negative discourse, and we shall demonstrate it more fully in the following sections.

Nevertheless, according to the opinion of some clear-sighted and perceptive people, **nature** completes its intention suddenly—namely, in a single day or even a shorter time. Even if this were true, we are still not able to imitate nature in its first principles, as we have shown more clearly through demonstration. However, we acknowledge the rest of the question, for we believe it to be entirely true.

And if they should say that the **perfection** <note>original: "perfectio." In this context, the transformation of a "base" metal like lead into a "perfect" metal like gold.</note> of metals is granted by the position of one or more **stars** which we do not know, we reply that it is not necessary for us to know this position. For there is no species of **generable and corruptible things** <term>generable and corruptible: a philosophical category referring to all things in the physical world that are born, change, and eventually decay</term> in which generation and corruption do not occur among some of its individuals every single day. From this, it is clear that the position of the stars is, every day, the power that perfects or corrupts the individuals of any **species**.

Therefore, it is not necessary to wait for a specific position of the stars, though it would be useful; instead, it is sufficient only to prepare the way for nature, so that she—who is wise—may arrange things through the suitable positions of the moving bodies <note>original: "mobilium corporum." This refers to the planets, which were believed to influence earthly chemistry as they moved through the sky.</note>. For nature cannot bring a work to completion without the motion and position of these moving things. Whence, if you have arranged the craft of nature and considered all the things that arise from the contingencies of this **mastery** <term>mastery: original "magisterii," often called the "Magisterium" or Great Work, the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</term>, then duly...

...[they] will be perfected under the proper conditions, through a nature suitable to them, without consideration of that <note>The author is referring to the specific "position of the stars" mentioned on the previous page.</note>. For we see that when we desire to bring a **worm** into being from flesh, or from some animal capable of rotting, we do not immediately consider the position of the **stars**. Instead, we look to the condition of the surrounding air and other causes that bring **putrefaction** <term>putrefaction: in alchemy, the process of breaking down a substance through heat and moisture, often viewed as the first step toward new life or transformation</term> to completion, regardless of the stars. From such observation, we know enough to produce **worms** <note>This refers to the historical belief in "spontaneous generation," where living creatures like maggots were thought to arise naturally from decaying matter without parents.</note> in accordance with nature. For nature finds a suitable position for itself, even if that position remains unknown to us.

And if they should say that **perfections** are granted in an instant, whereas we do not complete our perfection immediately, and conclude from this that it cannot be perfected through the skill of this art: we say that their heads are empty of human reason and that they are more like **beasts** than men. For they draw a conclusion from premises that have no relation to the point being made. Such an argument holds as much weight as: "**The donkey runs, therefore you are a goat**" <note>original: "Asinus currit, ergo tu es capra." This was a standard humorous example used in medieval schools to illustrate a non-sequitur— a logical fallacy where the conclusion does not follow from the premise.</note>.

And this is so because, even if the **preparation** does not happen in an instant, the final form or perfection is not prevented from being granted in an instant. For the **preparation** <term>preparation: the alchemist's physical work of cleaning, heating, and refining materials</term> is not the perfection itself, but rather the **making-ready** to receive that final form. 

Furthermore, if they say that it is easier to destroy natural things than to build them by skill, and since we can hardly destroy **gold**, they conclude that it must be impossible to construct it. We respond to them in this way: because...

<meta>This page continues the author's refutation of skeptics who claim alchemy is impossible. He transitions from addressing illogical arguments to a more systematic analysis of the "sophists'" misunderstandings regarding the nature and composition of gold.</meta>

...[their arguments] do not lead to a necessary conclusion: one that would force us to believe that **gold** cannot be constructed. For that which is difficult to build is likewise difficult to destroy; yet it is not impossible for it to be built. We have assigned the cause of this difficulty to the fact that it possesses a strong **composition**; for this reason, its **resolution** <term>resolution: the process of breaking a substance down into its primary components or elements</term> is more difficult, and thus it is destroyed only with great effort. 

It is this very difficulty that leads them to the opinion that its construction is impossible. However, because they are ignorant of its natural composition, they are consequently ignorant of both its destruction and its artificial construction according to the course of nature. They have perhaps attempted to understand that it is of a strong composition, but they have not attempted to grasp just how great the strength of that composition truly is.

### *On the arguments of those who deny the art*
### *from the given facts.*
## CHAPTER IX.

And so, my dearest son, we have sufficiently recounted the fantasies of the **Sophists** <note>In this context, "Sophists" refers to skeptics or philosophers who use clever but fallacious arguments to deny the possibility of alchemical transmutation.</note>. It remains for us, since we have secured your attention by our promise, to proceed according to that promise toward those things which must be determined based on the reasonings of those who deny the art from the given facts. 

Whatever principles have been determined, however, belong to the intention of nature; we must discuss their essence more fully in the following sections. After that determination, we shall also speak of them insofar as they are the principles of this our **Mastery** <term>Mastery: often called the *Magisterium*, this refers to the highest level of alchemical practice and the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone</term>...

...[of the] **mastery** <note>original: "magisterii"</note>. For now, however, let us first bring forward the arguments of those who deny the art based on the given facts, as well as their intentions. 

We have discovered that those who assume this art exists do so in many ways, driven by diverse intentions. Some seek it in **Spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that vaporize or "flee" when heated</term>. Still others truly affirm that this art and **mastery** <term>mastery: the "magisterium," the perfected alchemical process or the Great Work</term> is to be found in all vegetable things. 

Among all of these, some have judged well in part and badly in part. Others, however, judging this divine mastery poorly in its entirety, have passed their errors down to posterity. It was only through the multiplication of their errors that we were able to gather the truth—though with great difficulty, through the conjecture of laborious caution, long and tedious experience, and the investment of great expense. Their errors very often fought against the reasoning of our own mind and led us toward despair. 

Let them be cursed, therefore, for they have left eternal blasphemies to those who follow, born of their own error. They have poured out a curse upon **philosophers** <term>philosophers: "philosophantes," here specifically referring to those practicing the alchemical art or natural philosophy</term>, leaving behind not the truth, but a diabolical instigation after their deaths.

And the person opposing these theorists believes he is simply opposing the art itself. Yet, for the most part, both sects are empty of reason.

they flee away, while only the bodies remain in the heat, because they cannot endure the pressure of that fire’s severity due to their "flight" <note>original: "fugam," referring to volatility or the tendency to evaporate</note>, which has not been removed from them. "If you, **children of doctrine** <term>children of doctrine: a traditional term for students or initiated practitioners of alchemy</term>, wish to transform bodies, then if it is possible to do this through any **medicine** <term>medicine: in alchemical terms, a substance or elixir capable of 'curing' base metals into noble ones</term>, it must be done through the **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances like sulfur or mercury</term> themselves. But it is not possible for these spirits to adhere usefully if they are not fixed to the bodies; instead, they flee and leave the bodies impure. Furthermore, it is not possible for fixed spirits to enter the bodies, since they have become like earth, which cannot be infused; and yet, when enclosed in the bodies, they appear to be fixed, though they are not. Either they recede from the bodies while the bodies remain, or both flee together. Therefore, since it is impossible to find this **art** by any means in more closely related **matter**, it will not be found in more remote matter either; thus, it is found nowhere."

Our response, therefore, is this: because they do not know all that is knowable concerning this, they do not discover the entire operation. Consequently, they base their argument on a logical fallacy <note>original: "consequentis... vitium," referring to the fallacy of the consequent, where one assumes that because a specific method failed, the goal itself is impossible</note> by assuming their own limited knowledge is sufficient.

# THE FIRST BOOK

<page-num>28</page-num>

...they were ignorant of how to inquire into the final step. In all **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that "fly" from the fire</term> the method of preparation is similarly the same, except that in **Quick-silver** <note>original: Argento vivo</note> and **Tutty** <note>original: Tutiis; an impure form of zinc oxide collected from the flues of furnaces</note> we are excused from the greater labor, which consists of the removal of **combustibility** <note>original: adustio; the scorched or burnt quality of a substance</note>. For these do not have a combustible and flammable sulfurousness, but only "flight" <note>original: fugam; volatility or the tendency to evaporate when heated</note>.

**Magnesia** <note>a term used broadly for various minerals, often manganese or an ore of magnesium</note> and **Marcasite** <note>iron pyrite or similar metallic ores</note>, however, possess every kind of sulfurousness: Marcasite has more, and Magnesia has less. Yet all possess flight. Quick-silver and **Sal Ammoniac** <note>original: Armoniacum Sal; ammonium chloride</note> have the most flight; **Sulfur** has less, and its companion <note>likely arsenic</note> has even less than that; fourthly, Marcasite has even less; fifthly, Magnesia has less still; and sixthly and finally, Tutty has the least of all.

Yet each of these participates in flight, some more and some less. Therefore, because of this flight, a severe delusion comes upon certain **experimenters** <term>experimenters: used here to describe practitioners who work by trial and error without understanding the underlying theory</term> in their preparations and similarly in their **projections** <term>projections: the final stage of alchemy where the "medicine" is cast upon a base metal to transform it into gold or silver</term>. 

And for this reason, they argue and fail in their work, making assumptions as they do with Sulfur; and to those making such assumptions about Sulfur, we respond. There are also others striving in their experiments to **fix** <term>fix: to stabilize a volatile substance so it can endure the heat of a furnace without evaporating</term> the spirits into the **bodies** <term>bodies: the stable, solid metals (like lead, tin, or copper) that the alchemist seeks to transform</term> without any other preparation preceding this. But for them, delusion has similarly brought burdens <note>original: angarias; forced labor or distress</note> and despair, and they have been compelled by this to believe that this science does not exist and to argue against it.

For the cause of their confusion and their misplaced diligence is that during the infusion of the bodies, they let the spirits go in such a way that they do not adhere to the bodies, but rather, by the harshness of the fire...

<meta>The catchword "aufu-" at the bottom of the page suggests the next page begins with "aufugiunt," meaning "they flee away," continuing the description of how volatile spirits escape the crucible.</meta>

<summary>This page classifies various alchemical "spirits" according to their volatility (flight) and sulfurous content, explaining that failure to "fix" these spirits is the primary cause of alchemical failure and skepticism.</summary>
