<meta>This page continues the technical rebuttal against critics of alchemy. It addresses the specific physical problem of "flight" (volatility) and why skeptics believe that "spirits" (volatile agents) can never be successfully combined with "bodies" (metals) to achieve transmutation.</meta>

# BOOK 29

<page-num>29</page-num>

...they flee away, with only the **bodies** <term>bodies: the solid, metallic components of a substance</term> remaining in it <note>referring to the fire or the vessel</note>, since they cannot endure the pressure of the ruthlessness of that fire because of their **flight** <note>original: fugam; the tendency of a substance to evaporate or sublimate when heated</note>, which has not been taken away from them. A similar delusion also occurs at times when the bodies flee the fire along with the spirits; this happens when the **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic</term> have not been **fixed** <term>fixed: a process in alchemy where a volatile substance is made stable under heat</term> and have not adhered inseparably to the innermost depths of the bodies, because the quantity of the volatile substance overcomes the quantity of the fixed substance. For this reason, these critics argue just as the first ones did, and we respond to them in the same way.

The entirety of their proof is therefore as follows: "If you, the sons of the doctrine, wish to convert bodies, then if it were possible for this to be done through any **medicine** <term>medicine: the transformative "elixir" or "tincture" sought by alchemists</term>, it must necessarily be done through the spirits themselves. However, it is not possible for spirits that are not fixed to adhere usefully to the bodies; instead, they flee and leave those bodies behind in an impure state. Furthermore, it is not possible for them to enter the bodies once they have been fixed, since they have then been turned into **earth** <note>original: terra; the alchemical element associated with solidity and lack of fluidity</note>, which does not flow in. And yet, even when they are enclosed within the bodies and appear to be fixed, they are not truly so. Either they withdraw from the bodies while the bodies remain, or both flee together. Therefore, since it is in no way possible to find this art within matter that is more closely related <note>likely referring to metallic minerals</note>, it will certainly not be found in more distant matter; therefore, it is found nowhere."

Our response, therefore, is this: because they do not know everything that can be known about this subject, they do not discover the complete operation from it. Consequently, they mistake the deficiency of their own results for a limit to the power of the art itself.

THE FIRST BOOK

# On those who suppose the Art is found in bodies, stones, salts, alums, boraxes, or organic substances.
## CHAPTER 11.

Certain people have placed that Art in **bodies** <term>bodies: the solid metals</term>, yet when they came to the actual work, they were deceived. They estimated that both types of blueish **tin** <note>original: *Stannum*; in this context, the author refers to the group of "base" metals like lead and tin</note>—namely, the leaden and the white—were, by their pure whiteness, very much assimilated and close to the nature of the **Sun** <term>Sun: the alchemical symbol for Gold</term> and the **Moon** <term>Moon: the alchemical symbol for Silver</term>. They believed the blueish type [lead] to be very close to the Sun, but little to the Moon; and the white type [tin] to be very close to the Moon, but little to the Sun.

Therefore, some of these people—estimating that tin (namely **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for the metal Tin</term>) is much like the Moon, and believing it to differ only in its **creaking** <note>original: *stridore*; referring to the "tin cry," a distinct crackling sound made when bending a piece of tin</note>, its softness, and the speed at which it melts—argued that it is easily liquefied and soft due to an excess of its moisture. They explained that it possesses that creaking sound because of the fugitive substance of **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: Mercury</term> falling between its parts. Thus, they exposed it to the fire and, **calcining** <term>calcining: the process of heating a metal to high temperatures until it oxidizes into a fine powder or "ash"</term> it, they held it in the fire for as long as it could endure, until it was turned into a white **ash** <note>original: *calce* or calx; the powder remaining after a metal has been burned</note>. However, when they later tried to wrap and **reduce** <term>reduce: the process of returning a calcined powder back into a solid, metallic state</term> it, they were unable to do so, and deemed it to be impossible.

Even some of those who did manage to reduce something from it found the same creaking, softness, and speed of melting as before. Therefore, they believed the work to be impossible by this path, and they were led into disbelief, so that they think the Art is hard-

<meta>catchword: hard- (continuing the word "hardened" or "difficult" on the next page)</meta>

<summary>This page critiques alchemists who attempt to turn lead and tin into gold and silver based on their physical whiteness. It describes failed attempts to "fix" these metals through calcination, noting that the metals either refuse to return to a solid state or retain their original base properties.</summary>

<keywords>Alchemy, Tin, Lead, Jupiter, Calcination, Reduction, Mercury, Transmutation, Tin Cry, Metallurgy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "creaking" of tin (Jupiter) and the attempts by failed practitioners to alter its physical properties through repeated heating and chemical additives. ...of that duration could not be found. Some of these people, indeed, **calcined** <term>calcined: heated a metal in a furnace until it oxidized into a powder or "ash"</term> and **reduced** <term>reduced: the process of melting the powdered oxide back into a solid, metallic state</term> the metal; then, by removing its **dross** <note>original: *scoriam*; the stony waste or scum separated from metals during smelting</note> through the application of a stronger fire, they calcined and reduced it again. By frequently repeating this work upon it, they saw that the metal became hardened and lost its **creaking sound** <note>original: *stridore*; the "tin cry" mentioned on the previous page, which alchemists sought to remove to make tin more like silver</note>. However, because they did not entirely remove the speed of its **liquefaction** <term>liquefaction: the property of melting; tin and lead were criticized for melting at temperatures too low to withstand the "testing" fires of the goldsmith</term>, their minds erred, and they judged that the goal could not be reached.

Accordingly, others among them—wishing to grant this same metal hardness and a delay in its melting by the administration of two other **bodies** <term>bodies: the seven known metals (Gold, Silver, Mercury, Copper, Iron, Tin, Lead)</term>—fell into a delusion; for whatever of the two metals were mixed with it were ruined, nor did that preparation aid them in this matter.

And so, since they could not prepare the metal either by hardening it or by the use of fire, they excused themselves for the long delay in discovering the Art. These people claimed the Art was impossible, and believing this, they argued insistently against the Art, asserting that it did not exist. Because of this, and by adding many **medicines** <term>medicines: alchemical reagents or elixirs added to "cure" a base metal of its "disease" or imperfections</term>, they saw these additions caused no change and were not suitable for their needs, but rather corrupted the material and acted against their intentions. Therefore, they threw away their books, turned their heads away, and finally declared the Art to be frivolous. To these people, we offer our first response.

A similar mode of delusion is also found regarding **Saturn** <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for the metal Lead</term>, with only this difference: it does not ruin other metals <note>Lead was often used as a solvent in cupellation to purify gold and silver, whereas tin was known to make other metals brittle</note>, and it returns from its **lime** <term>lime: or 'calx,' the powdered oxide state of a metal after calcination</term> faster than **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for the metal Tin</term>. Yet, they are still unable to remove its **lividness** <note>original: *lividitatem*; the dull, blueish-grey color of lead that distinguishes it from the bright white of silver</note>...

<summary>This page details how failed alchemists attempted to "fix" tin by repeatedly refining it and adding other metals, only to give up and declare alchemy a fraud when they couldn't change its melting point. It then introduces lead (Saturn) as another metal that similarly resists transformation despite its different chemical behaviors.</summary>

...because they are ignorant; therefore, they are unable to **whiten** <term>whitening: also known as "Albedo," the second major stage of the alchemical work where the substance is purified and turned white</term> it with a proper whitening. Nor could they, through their own **imagination** <note>original: *phantasiam*; here referring to a misguided theoretical concept</note>, stably associate it with **stable bodies** <term>stable bodies: "fixed" metals, like gold and silver, which do not vaporize or change when exposed to intense heat</term>, without it happening that the substance recedes from the mixture when subjected to a strong fire or the exertion of heat. 

And that which most deceives them in the preparation of this substance is the assumption that the **science** <note>original: *scientiam*; the secret knowledge of the alchemical Art</note> can be found in nothing but this metal alone. Because after two **reductions** from its **ash** <term>ash: the "calx" or powdered oxide of a metal after it has been burned in a furnace</term>, it gains no hardness but rather a greater softness than it had before, and they did not see this corrected in other variations, they thought the secret was more closely and better found within it. But they did not find it. Because of this, they are forced to believe and argue that this science does not exist but is a delusion; thus, they err like those who came before them. 

Others, indeed, mixing the hard with the hard and the soft with the soft because of their suitability, wished to **transmute** <term>transmute: to fundamentally change one metal into another, specifically into gold or silver</term> the bodies into one another. They were unable to do so because of their ignorance. For example, by mixing **Gold** <note>original: *Solem*; literally "the Sun," the alchemical symbol for gold</note> or **Silver** <note>original: *Lunam*; literally "the Moon," the symbol for silver</note> with **Copper** <note>original: *Venere*; literally "Venus," the symbol for copper</note> or any other of the metals, they do not transmute them into Gold or Silver with a firm transmutation. Rather, by the exertion of a strong fire, each of those metals is either separated from the mixture, or burned away, or returns to its former nature. Some of these mixtures, indeed, endure longer in the fire, while others endure less, as has been sufficiently determined by us elsewhere. These delusions, therefore, arising on account of ignorance, make...

...make <note>original: *faciunt*; the word begins on the previous page</note> such people distrust this art and argue that it does not exist. Others, however, inquiring into this more intimately and deeply, reflected and devised a plan, wanting to find a way to stably harden hard things united with soft ones: namely, to bring united and perfect things back to perfection with imperfect ones, and generally to **transmute** <term>transmute: to fundamentally change the nature of a base metal into a precious one</term> them into one another, so that they might be transformed by a firm and lasting transmutation. 

And therefore they wished to find the likeness and affinity of these things, both through **medicines** <term>medicines: alchemical reagents or elixirs used to "cure" or transform metals</term> and through the administration of fire—namely by thinning thick things, like **Venus** <term>Venus: the alchemical name for copper; original: *Venerem*</term> and **Mars** <term>Mars: the alchemical name for iron; original: *Martem*</term>, and by thickening subtle things, like **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for tin; original: *Jovem*</term> and things similar to it. And thus they did not find a proper balance; and therefore they denied that the art exists. 

Others indeed, looking even more deeply, wanted to alter the bodies by the extraction of their **souls** <term>souls: in alchemy, the "anima" or soul refers to the volatile essence or spiritual principle of a metal that gives it its specific properties</term>, and with the extracted soul, they hoped to alter another soul. Similarly, their experience could not arrive at this goal, but they lost faith in their intention, judging from this that the art could not be found. Others, attempting to perfect it by fire alone, were deceived in their opinion, because they could not reach it, nor did they know how. And these people thought because of this that the art did not exist,

# ->BOOK ONE<-

To all of these things we respond as we did before. But those who have placed the Art in **Glass** <term>Glass: alchemists often experimented with glassy fluxes (vitrum) to protect metals or act as a medium for transformation</term> and **Gems** attempted to make alterations in bodies <note>In this context, "bodies" refers to the base metals being worked upon</note> through Glass and Gems, and they were unable to do so, because that which does not penetrate does not alter. And indeed, Glass and Gems themselves do not penetrate the metals; therefore they do not alter them. Even if they tried to unite Glass in such a way—though this is hardly possible—they still do not achieve their goal, because they make Glass from the bodies [of the metals] themselves. Because of this error, they judge that the error lies within the entire Art, and thus they argue that it does not exist.

To these we respond that they work in an improper manner; and therefore, by concluding improperly, they cannot destroy this Art based on their own errors. There are also others who suppose this Art is found in **Salts** and **Alums**, **Vitriols** <term>Vitriols: sulfate minerals, typically green or blue, used for their corrosive and reactive properties; original: *Vitris*</term>, and **Boraxes** <term>Boraxes: alkaline mineral salts used primarily as fluxes to help metals melt; original: *Bauraciis*</term>. They can indeed experiment with these, but we believe the Art is found in them least of all. 

Therefore, if after their experiments they found little use for **transmutation**—namely by dissolving, congealing, and roasting—they do not thereby destroy the Art, since the cause is their own ignorance <note>original: *inscientia*; the author argues that the practitioner's lack of knowledge, not a flaw in alchemy itself, is the reason for failure</note>. It is possible, however, for some alteration to be found in all these things, but it is very remote and extremely laborious. Furthermore, it is proven to be even more laborious in all other growing things; therefore, some place the Art in all **vegetable matter** <term>vegetable matter: organic plant materials; many alchemists sought the "Stone" in the plant kingdom rather than the mineral</term>. They claim it is possible, but not for them: since they would fail in the labor before the work could possibly be perfected. Therefore, even if such people do not find the Art...

...through their own failed labors, they argue that the Art can be discovered by no amount of labor at all. However, all those previously mentioned who wandered in error chose only one specific medicine <note>Medicine: in alchemy, the "medicine" (medicina) refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone, the agent used to "heal" or transform base metals into gold</note> of their own and considered no other besides it. And indeed, these have all now been refuted.

Yet many others—an almost infinite number—attempt to make a composition of all these things, or of certain combinations, through a diverse process of creation. They proceed ignorantly and unknowingly, and their error extends to infinity. This is because there is an infinite diversity in the way mixable substances <term>mixable substances: materials capable of being combined into a single compound; original: *miſcibilium*</term> are created, and an infinite diversity and number of the things that can be mixed. In both of these infinities, one happens to err infinitely—sometimes through excess and sometimes through deficiency—and for such errors, correction is impossible.

But we, wishing to avoid length and wordiness, do not want to dwell on these infinite errors. Instead, we shall hand down a universal knowledge in brief statements, through which seekers will clearly be able to amend the infinity of their errors and correct them. Now, we will speak of the natural principles according to their causes, just as we caught your attention by mentioning them at the beginning.

## On the natural principles of Mercury, Sulfur, and Arsenic.

opinion, who were of the sect of those imitating our art, that the <term>natural principles: the fundamental building blocks of metals in alchemical theory; original: *principia naturalia*</term> in the work of nature are the fetid spirit and living water, which we also allow to be called "dry water." We have divided the fetid spirit: for it is white in its hidden state, as well as red and black. Both [aspects] are present in the <term>mastery: the skillful process or Great Work of alchemy; original: *magisterio*</term> of this work, yet in their outward appearance, both tend toward redness. It is fitting, therefore, for us to expand and broaden our discourse, and to provide a separate chapter for each individual natural principle. 

In general, however, we say that each of them is of the strongest composition and of a uniform substance. This is because, within them, the earthy parts are united <note>original: *per minima*; a philosophical term referring to the smallest possible particles of matter, similar to the concept of atoms</note> at the smallest level with the airy, watery, and fiery parts in such a way that none of them can release the other during dissolution. On the contrary, each part is dissolved along with every other part due to the strong union they share with one another at the smallest level. This is achieved by an equal heat hidden in the bowels of the earth among the minerals, multiplied and balanced according to the proper course of nature, as required by their essence, according to the opinion of certain <note>original: *antiquorum*; the ancient philosophers or early authorities of the craft</note> ancients. 

Others, however, have said differently: that the principle was not **Quick-silver** <note>mercury; original: *Argentum vivum*</note> in its own nature, but rather altered and converted into its own earth; and that **Sulphur** was similarly altered and changed into earth. ...they said that the principle was something other than the <term>fetid spirit: likely referring to sulfur in its raw, impure state; original: *spiritus fætens*</term> and the <term>fleeing spirit: a volatile substance that evaporates easily, usually associated with mercury; original: *spiritus fugiens*</term> within the intention of nature. And the reason by which they were moved was because they do not find in silver mines, or those of other metals, anything that is <term>quicksilver: mercury; original: *Argentum vivum*</term> in its own nature, nor anything that is <term>sulfur: original: *Sulphur*</term> in its own nature in a similar way. Similarly, they also affirm this by another reasoning: namely, that there is no transition from one contrary to another except through a middle state <note>original: *dispositionem mediam*; in Aristotelian physics, a state between two extremes, such as a "lukewarm" state between hot and cold</note>. Therefore, since this is so, they are forced to admit and believe that there is no transition from the softness of quicksilver to the hardness of any metal, except through a state that lies between their hardness and softness. But in the mines, they do not find anything in which this middle state is preserved. For this reason, they are compelled to believe that quicksilver and sulfur, in their own nature, are not the principle intended by nature, but rather something else—something which follows from the alteration of their essences at the root of nature into an earthy substance.

And this is the process: first, each of them is converted to an earthy substance; then, from both of these earthy natures, a very thin smoke is released by the heat multiplied in the <note>original: *viscere terræ*; literally "the bowels of the earth," a common alchemical metaphor for the subterranean forge of nature</note> bowels of the earth. This double smoke is the immediate matter of metals. This smoke, however, once <term>cooked: or "decocted," the process of refining through heat; original: *decoctus*</term> by the tempered heat of the mine, is converted into the nature of a certain earth. Therefore, whatever fiction...

...takes on a certain alteration, which water flowing down through the bowels of the <term>mine: or ore-vein; original: *minera*</term> and the sponginess of the earth dissolves; it is then uniformly united to it by a natural and firm union. Therefore, those holding this opinion have said that the water flowing through the passages of the earth finds a dissolvable substance from the earth's own material within its bowels and dissolves it. The water then becomes uniformly united with it until both the dissolved substance of the earth and the flowing water that dissolved it become one through a natural union. All elements come together in such a union according to a due and natural proportion, and they are mixed <note>original: *per minima*; a reference to the medieval scientific theory of "minima naturalia," the smallest particles of a substance that still retain its specific properties</note> until they create a uniform mixture. Through successive <term>cooking: the natural process of maturation via internal heat; original: *decoctio*</term>, this mixture is thickened and hardened within the mine and becomes a metal. 

These thinkers are also close to the truth, yet they do not grasp the pure truth. Now that we have determined the natural principles of metals in a general discourse, it remains for us to establish a specific chapter for each principle. Since there are three—<term>Sulfur: original: *Sulphur*</term>, <term>Arsenic: original: *Arsenicum*</term>, and <term>Quicksilver: mercury; original: *Argentum vivum*</term>—we will first write a chapter on Sulfur, second on Arsenic, and third on Quicksilver. Then, for each of the metals, which are the effects of these principles, we will provide a specific chapter according to how they result from the work of nature. Finally, we must move on to those things which are the foundations of this <term>mastery: the art of alchemy; original: *magisterium*</term> and their operations, assigning the causes for all of these.

## *Concerning Sulfur.*
### CHAP. XIII.

We say, therefore, that **Sulfur** is a <term>fatness: or oiliness; original: *pinguedo*</term> in the <term>ore: or mine; original: *minera*</term> of the earth, thickened by a temperate <term>cooking: maturation through heat; original: *decoctio*</term> until it is hardened and becomes dry; and when it has been hardened, it is called Sulfur. Indeed, Sulfur has a very strong composition and is of a uniform substance in all its parts, because it is <note>original: *homogeneum*; meaning it is consistent throughout rather than a mixture of visible parts</note> homogeneous. Therefore, its oil is not removed from it by distillation, as oil is from other things that contain oil. 

Those, therefore, who seek to <term>calcine: to reduce a substance to a powder by heat; original: *calcinare*</term> it without losing any of its substance worth mentioning labor in vain; for it is not calcined except through great industry, and much of its substance is dissipated. Indeed, out of a hundred parts, you will scarcely save three for yourself after its calcination. 

Likewise, it cannot be <term>fixed: made stable and non-volatile under heat; original: *figi*</term> unless it is first calcined. Nevertheless, it can be mixed with other things, and its <note>original: *fuga*; literally "flight," referring to the substance's tendency to evaporate or sublimate when heated</note> flight can be somewhat delayed, and its burning can be suppressed; it is more easily calcined when mixed with a companion. Therefore, he who seeks to draw the <note>original: *opus*; the "Work" or alchemical process</note> Work out from it should not attempt to do so by preparing it on its own, because the process is perfected through a mixture. Without a companion, the <term>Magistery: the master-process or "masterpiece" of alchemical transformation; original: *magisterium*</term> is prolonged until one reaches despair. But when joined with its companion, it becomes a <term>tincture: a substance capable of infusing color and quality into a metal; original: *tinctura*</term>, and it gives full weight to each of the metals, and purifies them of their foulness and brightens them. It perfects them through the Magistery, without which it performs none of these things, but rather...

...receives, which flowing water through the <term>bowels of the ore: the internal veins of the earth where minerals form; original: *viscera minerae*</term> and the sponginess of the earth dissolves, and it is uniformly united to it in a natural and firm union. And therefore, those who hold this opinion have said that water flowing through the passages of the earth finds a dissolvable substance from the earth's own material within its bowels; it dissolves this substance and is uniformly united with it, until both the substance of the earth dissolved in the bowels and the flowing water that acts as the solvent become one by a natural union. To such a union all elements come, according to a proper and natural proportion, and they are mixed <note>original: *per minima*; an alchemical and Aristotelian term meaning mixed at the most microscopic or fundamental level</note> through their smallest parts until they form a uniform mixture. This mixture, through successive <term>decoction: the process of thickening or ripening a substance through steady, natural heat; original: *decoctio*</term>, is thickened and hardened within the ore and becomes a metal. 

These thinkers are indeed close to the truth, yet they do not grasp the truth in its pure form. Now that we have determined the natural principles of metals in a general discourse, it remains for us to set down the primary heading for each individual principle. Since there are three—**Sulfur**, **Arsenic**, and **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argentum vivum*; literally "living silver," the standard name for mercury</note>—we shall first write the chapter concerning Sulfur, second concerning Arsenic, and third concerning Quicksilver. 

Following those, we shall set down a specific chapter for each of the metals that are produced from them, according to how they are formed by the work of nature. Finally, we must move on to those things which are the foundations of this <term>mastery: the art of alchemy; original: *magisterium*</term> and their operations, assigning the causes for all of them.

# Concerning Sulfur.
## CHAPTER 13.

We say, therefore, that **Sulfur** is a fatness <note>original: *pinguedo*; in alchemy, this refers to an oily, combustible substance that provides the "soul" or "fire" of a metal</note> in the ore of the earth, thickened by a tempered <term>decoction: a process of ripening or thickening through steady heat; original: *decoctio*</term> until it becomes hardened and dry; and when it has been hardened, it is called Sulfur. Indeed, Sulfur has a very strong composition and is of a uniform substance in all its parts: because it is <term>homogeneous: a substance where every part is the same as the whole; original: *homogeneum*</term>. Therefore, its oil is not taken away from it by distillation, as occurs with other things that contain oil. 

Those who seek to <term>calcine: to reduce a substance to a powder by means of intense heat; original: *calcinare*</term> it without losing any of its substance (which would be a cause for concern) labor in vain, because it cannot be calcined except by great industry, and much of its substance is dissipated; for out of a hundred parts, you will scarcely reserve three for yourself after its calcination. Similarly, it cannot be <term>fixed: to make a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates when heated; original: *figi*</term> unless it is first calcined. It can, however, be mixed, and its flight <note>original: *fuga*; a common alchemical term for evaporation or volatility</note> can be delayed a little, and its burning can be restrained; and when mixed, it is more easily calcined. 

Therefore, he who seeks to draw out the work from it should not try to draw it out by preparing the sulfur by itself, since the <term>magisterium: the "mastery" or the completed alchemical process; original: *magisterium*</term> is perfected when it is mixed with its partner. Without that partner, the work is prolonged even unto despair. When joined with its companion, it becomes a <term>tincture: a substance capable of "dyeing" or transforming the nature of a metal; original: *tinctura*</term>, and it gives the complete weight to each of the metals, and it purifies them from foulness and makes them shine. It perfects them through the mastery, without which it provides none of these things, but ra- <note>The text ends mid-word with the catchword "tiùs," likely for the word *potius*, meaning "rather."</note>

...more quickly corrupts and blackens; therefore, do not use it without mastery itself. He who knows how to mix it in preparation and make it friendly to metallic bodies will know one of the greatest secrets of nature, and the unique way of perfection, though there are many ways to achieve a single effect and a single intent. 

And whatever body is **calcined** <term>calcination: the process of reducing a metal to a powder (oxide) by heating it in air; original: *calcinatur*</term> by it, without doubt acquires weight. **Copper** <note>original: *Æs*; refers here to base copper or bronze</note> also takes from it the likeness of **Gold** <note>original: *Sol*; "the Sun," the alchemical symbol for gold</note>. When associated and roasted with **Mercury** through **sublimation**, it becomes **cinnabar** <term>usifur: an archaic term for cinnabar or red mercuric sulfide; original: *usifur*</term>.

Furthermore, **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argentum vivum*</note> is not coagulated by it into Gold or **Silver** <note>original: *Luna*; "the Moon," the alchemical symbol for silver</note> through any weak artifice, as certain foolish philosophers have thought. And so we say that whatever bodies have less humidity are more easily calcined by **Sulfur** than those which have much. 

By God most high, this substance illuminates every body: for it is a light and a **tincture** <term>tincture: a substance or "spirit" capable of infusing and changing the color or quality of a metal; original: *tinctura*</term>. It is also dissolved with great difficulty, because it does not have salty parts, but oily ones, which are not easily dissolved in water. Those things which are easily or difficultly dissolved in water we shall show clearly enough in the chapter on solution. 

It is indeed **sublimed** <note>original: *sublimatur*; the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then collects as a solid again</note> because it is a spirit; and if it is mixed with **Copper** <note>original: *Venus*; the planetary name for copper</note> and burned with it, a wonderful violet is produced. Likewise, it is mixed with **Mercury**, and from them, through **decoction** <term>decoction: a process of thickening or ripening a substance through steady heat; original: *decoctio*</term>, a heavenly and delightful color is made. 

Let no one think that **Sulfur** completes the work by itself...

# *On Arsenic*
## CHAPTER XIV.

It remains for us to discuss **Arsenic**. We say for the present that it is made of a subtle matter and is similar to **Sulfur**; therefore, it need not be defined differently than Sulfur. However, it differs from Sulfur in this: it is a **tincture** <term>tincture: a substance or "spirit" capable of infusing and changing the color of a metal; original: *tinctura*</term> of whiteness <note>original: *albedinis*; referring to the *albedo* stage of alchemy where the metal turns silver</note> and likewise of redness <note>original: *rubedinis*; the *rubedo* stage where it turns gold</note> with ease, whereas Sulfur results in redness easily, but whiteness only with great difficulty. 

There are two types of both Sulfur and Arsenic: the lemon-yellow <note>original: *citrinum*</note> and the red, which are useful to this art; the many other types are not. Furthermore, Arsenic is **fixed** <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so it does not evaporate; original: *figitur*</term> just as Sulfur is. The **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then collecting it as a purified solid; original: *sublimatio*</term> of both is better when performed from the **calxes** <term>calx: the crumbly powder, or oxide, left after a metal has been roasted or "burned"; original: *calcibus*</term> of metals. However, Sulfur and Arsenic are not the perfecting medicine of this work, nor its completion; rather, they are an aid to perfection in certain cases. The bright, scaly, and easily split <note>original: *scissile*; likely referring to the mineral's crystalline cleavage</note> variety is preferred.

# *On Mercury*
## CHAPTER XV.

Indeed, **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argentum vivum*</note>, which is also called **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurius*</note> by the custom of the ancients, is a viscous water...

in the bowels of the earth <note>original: *visceribus terræ*</note>, of a subtle substance, white and earthy, [formed] by a most tempered heat, united in a total union through the smallest parts <note>original: *per minima*; a medieval physical concept where substances are mixed so thoroughly that they are unified at the most basic level</note>, until the moisture is tempered by the dryness, and the dryness by the moisture equally. And therefore, it flees a flat surface easily because of the moisture of its water; however, it does not stick [to it], even though it possesses a viscous moisture, because of that dryness which tempers it and does not permit it to adhere. It is also (as some say) the "matter of metals" along with **Sulfur** <term>Sulfur-Mercury theory: the medieval belief that all metals are composed of varying ratios and purities of sulfur and mercury</term>. It also adheres to three minerals with ease: namely to **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>, and **Sol** <note>Gold</note>. To **Luna** <note>Silver</note>, however, it adheres with more difficulty. To **Venus** <note>Copper</note>, it is more difficult than to Luna. But to **Mars** <note>Iron</note>, it adheres in no way at all, except through artifice. 

From this fact you may surely draw out a very great secret. For it is friendly and peaceable toward metals, and it is a medium for joining **tinctures** <term>tincture: a substance or "spirit" capable of infusing and changing the color of a metal</term>. No metal is submerged in **Mercury** <note>Quicksilver</note> except for Sol. Nevertheless, Jupiter and Saturn, Luna and Venus are dissolved by it and mixed; and without it, no metal can be **gilded** <term>gilding: specifically fire-gilding, where gold is dissolved in mercury (forming an amalgam), spread on a surface, and the mercury is burned off</term>. It is **fixed** <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so it does not evaporate</term> and is a tincture of redness <note>original: *rubedinis*; the "Rubedo" or final red stage of the Great Work</note>, of most exuberant restoration, shining in brilliance, and it does not recede from the mixture so long as it exists. However, it is not our **medicine** <term>medicine: the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone in its curative form for metals</term> in its raw nature, but it can sometimes assist similarly in certain cases.

# On metallic bodies, which are the effects of the principles of nature.

## CHAPTER XVI.

We speak now of the metallic bodies, which are the result of these principles of nature. There are six of them: <term>Gold</term> <note>original: *Aurum*</note>, <term>Silver</term> <note>original: *Argentum*</note>, <term>Lead</term> <note>original: *Plumbum*</note>, <term>Tin</term> <note>original: *Stannum*</note>, <term>Copper</term> <note>original: *Æs*; while this can mean bronze or brass, in this context it refers to the base metal copper</note>, and <term>Iron</term> <note>original: *Ferrum*</note>. We define a metal as a body that can be mixed, a mineral that can be melted, and one that can be stretched in every direction under a hammer <note>A classic medieval definition of metallic malleability</note>. Metal, as we have said, is of a dense substance and of a very strong and firm composition. Metals also have a great affinity for one another; however, the perfect does not perfect the diminished <note>the "imperfect" base metals</note> simply by being mixed with it. 

For if Gold is mixed with Lead through melting, the Lead does not become hard because of this; instead, the Lead vanishes from the mixture and is burned away. Gold, however, remains in the testing <note>original: *examinatione*; likely referring to cupellation, a process where lead is oxidized to purify gold or silver</note>, and similarly, in the treatment of the others, it follows the same course. But according to our **mastery** <term>magisterium: the alchemical art or the specific process of the "Great Work" to transform and "perfect" matter</term>, the perfect does indeed perfect and aid the imperfect. In our mastery, we perfect the imperfect by itself without adding any outside substance, and we also perfect the perfect by this same mastery. And through God, they change one another and are changed, and they perfect one another and are perfected; nevertheless, we can by no means perfect any one of them alone without the help of something else.

### CHAPTER XVII.

We have provided you with a summary of the nature and "intentions" of metals in the general chapter; however, we shall now offer you a specific discourse on each one, and first concerning **Gold**. We say, therefore, that Gold is a yellow, heavy, silent <note>original: *mutum*; this likely refers to Gold's lack of "cry" when bent—unlike tin—or its lack of a distinct metallic scent</note>, and shining metallic body, which has been evenly digested in the belly of the earth and washed for a very long time by mineral water. It is capable of being stretched under the hammer and is meltable, withstanding the testing of the <term>cupel</term> <note>original: *cineritii*; the process of refining gold in a vessel made of bone-ash</note> and of <term>cementation</term> <note>original: *cementi*; a method of purifying gold by heating it with salt and other minerals to remove impurities</note>. From this definition, you may conclude that a substance is not Gold unless it possesses all the causes and characteristics of Gold. Nevertheless, whoever thoroughly "yellows" a metal from its very roots, brings it to a state of equilibrium, and cleanses it, produces Gold from any kind of metal.

Therefore, by observing the work of nature, we consider that **Copper** <note>original: *Æs*</note> can be changed into Gold through its own natural artistry. For we have seen in copper mines from which water flowed, carrying with it very thin flakes of copper, that the water washes and cleanses these flakes by its long and continuous flow. Then, indeed, once the water ceased to flow, we saw these flakes "cooked out" by the heat of the sun for three years amidst dry sand; among these flakes, the truest Gold was found. We judged, therefore, that they had been cleansed by the benefit of the water, and through the heat of the sun and the dryness of the sand, having been evenly digested, they arrived at a state of balance. Therefore, by imitating nature,

<summary>This page defines Gold as the most perfect metal—characterized by its weight, color, and resistance to fire—and argues that other metals like copper can be naturally or artificially transformed into gold through cleansing and "digestion."</summary>

HEADER: BOOK.

...[flow of the water], when we can, we alter it in a similar way, yet in this we are not quite able to follow nature perfectly. **Gold** is also the most precious of metals, and it is a <term>tincture</term> <note>original: *tinctura*; a substance capable of imparting its color and properties to others</note> of redness, because it colors and transforms every metallic body. It can be <term>calcined</term> <note>original: *calcinatur*; reduced to a powder through heat</note> and dissolved, though without much practical use <note>likely referring to the fact that Gold does not lose its essential weight or nature during these processes</note>; and it is a medicine that gladdens the body in its youth. It is easily made brittle with **Mercury** and the "odor" of **Lead** <note>original: *odore Plumbi*; alchemists observed that lead vapors or small amounts of lead make gold extremely brittle and easy to crush</note>, and it can be ground down. In actual practice, there is nothing that shares more of its nature than **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note> and **Luna** <note>Silver</note>. In terms of weight, "deafness" <note>original: *surditate*; the lack of a ringing sound when struck</note>, and resistance to decay, it is most like **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>. In color, it resembles **Venus** <note>Copper</note>. In its "potency" or power, it is most like **Venus**, then **Luna**, then **Jupiter**, and then **Saturn**; and finally **Mars** <note>Iron</note>. This is one of the secrets of nature. In the same way, <term>spirits</term> <note>volatile substances like sulfur or arsenic</note> are mixed with it and "fixed" <note>made resistant to heat</note> through it by means of great skill—a skill that cannot be reached by a "stiff-necked" or stubborn craftsman.

## On the Moon.
### CHAPTER XVIII.

Having concluded the chapter on the **Sun**, let us now speak of the **Moon** using its common name, **Silver**. We say that **Silver** is a white metallic body, of a pure whiteness, clean, hard, resonant, and enduring in the <term>cupel</term> <note>original: *cineritio*; a refining process using a vessel made of bone-ash</note>. ## *On Jupiter [Tin].*

Therefore, among the bodies diminished from perfection, **Jupiter** is more perfect, being by the root of its nature akin to the **Sun** [Gold] and the **Moon** [Silver]—more so to the Moon, however, and less to the Sun.

## On Jupiter [Tin].
### CHAPTER XX.

Therefore, not omitting the tradition of **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>, we signify to the **sons of doctrine** <note>original: *filiis doctrinæ*; a common address to students of the alchemical arts</note> that it is a white metallic body, not purely leaden-gray, and ringing a little: sharing in little earthiness: possessing by its root a screech <note>original: *stridorem*; likely referring to the "tin cry," the crackling sound made when bending a bar of tin</note>, softness, and a speed of melting without becoming red-hot <note>original: *ignitione*; reaching a glowing heat</note>: not awaiting the **ash-test** or the **cement-test** <note>original: *cementum*; a dry refining process using salts and heat</note>, yet it is extendable under the hammer. And whoever knows how to remove its defect of making bodies brittle will suddenly rejoice in enjoying its profit. For it agrees with the **Sun** and **Moon**, and shall never be separated from them.

## On Venus [Copper].

# On Mars [Iron].
## CHAPTER XXII.

The account and whole secret of **Mars** <term>Iron</term> belongs to the work of nature: for it is a metallic body, very leaden-colored, but a little red, sharing in an impure whiteness. It is hard and subject to fire <note>original: *ignibile*; referring to the metal's tendency to oxidize or "burn" in the heat of a furnace rather than melting cleanly</note>, not meltable by standard fusion, extendable under the hammer, and very resonant. **Mars** is, however, difficult to handle because of its inability to be melted; yet if it were melted without a medicine <note>In alchemy, a "medicine" is a substance—often a form of the Philosopher's Stone—used to "heal" or transform a base metal into a perfect one</note> that changes its nature, it joins with the **Sun** <term>Gold</term> and **Moon** <term>Silver</term>, and will not be separated by the assay of separation <note>the process of testing and parting metals to determine purity</note> without great effort. 

But if it is prepared <note>purified or chemically treated</note>, it joins and cannot be separated by any artifice, provided its nature of fixation <term>the ability to remain stable under heat</term> is not changed by the mere removal of its impurity. Therefore, it is easily a tincture of redness <term>rubedo</term>; but a tincture of good whiteness <term>albedo</term> is most difficult to achieve. # THE SUMMARY OF GEBER
#### *The most keen-sighted philosopher,*
## ON THE PRINCIPLES OF MASTERY, AND THE PERFECTION OF THE SAME
### PREFACE.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring an elaborate scrolling foliate and floral design set within a square frame.</image-desc>
There are two things for which the principles must be determined: namely, those of this mastery <term>the Great Work of alchemy</term> and the perfection of the same. Therefore, the principles of this art are the modes of its operations, by which the craftsman applies himself to this mastery, which are indeed diverse from one another. For one mode is **Sublimation** <term>purifying a solid by vaporizing it and letting it collect again at the top of a vessel</term>, and another is **Descension** <term>filtering or melting a substance downward using heat applied from above</term>, and one is **Distillation** <term>separating components of a liquid through evaporation and condensation</term>, and one of the modes is **Calcination** <term>reducing a substance to a powder through intense heat and oxidation</term>, and another is **Solution** <term>dissolving a solid into a liquid state</term>, the sixth is **Coagulation** <term>the process of thickening or solidifying a liquid</term>, the seventh truly is **Fixation** <term>making a volatile substance stable so it can withstand fire without evaporating</term>, and the eighth is **Ceration** <term>softening a substance until it reaches a waxy consistency</term>; of each of these we shall provide an account. Perfection, however, consists of the consideration of those things by which one arrives at it, and of the consideration of helpful things, and of the consideration of the thing itself which finally perfects, and of the consideration of the thing from which it is comp- <note>original: *cogo-*; the word is cut off by the page break, likely leading to a form of *cogoscere* (to know/recognize) or *cogere* (to bring together)</note>

# *On Why Sublimation Was Invented*

## CHAPTER I.

Following, therefore, our proposed plan, we speak of **Sublimation**. The cause of its invention was that our ancestors—as well as ourselves and those who will come after us—found nothing that could be united to **bodies** <note>In alchemy, "bodies" refer to the solid metals, such as gold, silver, or lead</note> except **spirits** <term>volatile substances that vaporize when heated</term> alone, or something containing the nature of a body or a spirit within itself. 

They observed that when these spirits were projected upon bodies without being cleansed, they either failed to produce perfect colors or they completely corrupted, burned, blackened, and defiled the metals. This occurred according to the specific nature of the spirits themselves. For some are **burning** <note>original: "adurentes"; referring to substances that cause oxidation or "scorching" in the metal</note>, such as **Sulfur**, **Arsenic**, and **Marcasite** <term>iron pyrite or similar metallic sulfides</term>, and these indeed corrupt the metal entirely. 

Others, however, do not burn, such as every kind of **Tutia** <term>zinc oxide collected from furnace chimneys</term>, and these give imperfect colors. This is because those substances which burn, blacken, and defile do so for a twofold reason: first, because the **oiliness** <term>unctuositas: the flammable, fatty principle thought to exist within minerals</term> of Sulfur—the property of which is to ignite easily and turn black through fire, thereby corrupting the metal—has not been removed from them. 

The second cause is **earthiness** <term>terreitas: the solid, impure, and non-volatile dregs</term>, which likewise was not separated from them. In those substances where a perfect color is not achieved, the cause is earthiness alone, which produces a leaden or pale hue. Burning can also create this leaden color. Therefore, we have devised ways to cleanse those spirits possessing it from their burning oiliness, and to cleanse all of them from their earthy superfluity. And to...

<summary>This chapter explains that sublimation was developed to purify "spirits" (volatile minerals like sulfur and arsenic) of the "oiliness" and "earthiness" that otherwise ruin or discolor metals during alchemical transformation.</summary>

<meta>catchword: Concerning (De)</meta>

...this we can achieve through no **mastery** <note>original: "magisterium"; here referring to the expert skill or specific chemical operation of the alchemist</note> except by **Sublimation** alone. For when fire is raised, it always lifts up the more subtle parts; therefore, it leaves behind the grosser, heavier parts. From this, it is evident that through **Sublimation**, the **spirit** <term>a volatile mineral substance that can vaporize</term> is cleansed of the "earthiness" which previously hindered its ability to enter into the metal and gave it an impure color.

Indeed, experience will make this plain to your own sight: you will see that the spirits have been freed from that earthiness. You will see them become more brilliant and more "permeable," and you will see that they enter and penetrate the density of **bodies** <note>In this context, the solid metals like copper or lead being treated</note> more easily, without making them foul as they did before.

That **combustibility** <term>adustio: the property of scorching or "burning" the metal, which alchemists sought to remove</term> is removed by **Sublimation** is proven by experiment. For **Arsenic**, which was impure before its sublimation, does not allow itself to be ignited after it has been sublimated; instead, it simply evaporates without catching fire. You will find the same result in experiments with **Sulfur**.

Because, therefore, we have seen no substances other than these spirits adhere to bodies and cause them to transform, we have had no choice but to prepare them by this purification—which is **Sublimation**. This, then, was the necessary reason for the invention of this process. Let us now describe its entire procedure and its true essence without omission.

<summary>This page explains the chemical theory behind sublimation, arguing that heat purifies volatile "spirits" by separating them from heavy, earthy impurities. It cites the change in Arsenic and Sulfur—which stop burning and start vaporizing cleanly after treatment—as proof that the process works.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, purification of spirits, arsenic, sulfur, alchemical theory, adustio, subtle and gross parts</keywords>

### *Concerning the types of Sublimation, and how it*
### *ought to be performed and organized.*

#### CHAPTER II.

We say, therefore, that <term>Sublimation</term> <note>original: "Sublimatio"; the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collects again as a solid on the cooler upper parts of a vessel</note> is the elevation of a dry substance by fire, such that it adheres to its vessel. Indeed, <term>Sublimation</term> is varied according to the diversity of the "spirits" <note>volatile mineral substances</note> to be sublimated; for some are done with intense heat <note>original: "ignitione"; literally "ignition" or firing</note>, some with a moderate fire, and others with a gentle <note>original: "remisso"</note> fire.

If, therefore, **Arsenic** or **Sulfur** are to be sublimated, it is necessary that they be sublimated by a gentle fire. Because they possess very "subtle" <note>fine or light</note> parts joined uniformly to "gross" <note>thick or heavy</note> ones, their entire substance would rise at once without any purification; indeed, they would be blackened and even scorched. 

Therefore, so that one may separate the impure, earthy substance, it was necessary to discover techniques of two kinds: namely, the proportioning of the fire, and purification through the mixing of "dregs" <note>original: "fæcum"; the inert or solid remains left after a process</note>. This is because mixing the substance with dregs catches the gross parts and keeps them weighed down at the bottom of the <term>Aludels</term> <note>pear-shaped ceramic pots used in a vertical stack for sublimation</note>, not allowing them to climb. 

For this reason, it is necessary for the craftsman to apply a threefold degree of fire to the **Sublimation**. The first degree should be proportioned in such a way that only the altered, purer, and more lucid parts ascend; by this, he may clearly see them being cleansed from the dreg-filled earth. The second degree is so that whatever of their pure essence remained in the dregs may be sublimated with a greater force <note>original: "expressionè"</note>...

<summary>The author defines sublimation as the rising of dry matter via heat and explains that different substances require specific "degrees" of fire to prevent the impure, heavy parts from rising along with the pure vapors.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, arsenic, sulfur, aludel, degrees of fire, purification, dregs, alchemy techniques</keywords>

<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>S E C O N D [B O O K]</header>
<meta>catchword: sublimation</meta>

...pressure of fire, namely with the firing <note>original: "ignitione"; referring to the intense heating of the vessel</note> of the **Aludels** <note>pear-shaped ceramic vessels used in sublimation</note> and the dregs <note>original: "fæcum"; the solid remains used as a filtering medium</note> themselves, which the craftsman can see clearly with his own eyes. The third grade is that a very weak fire, without dregs, should be applied to that which has already been sublimated from the dregs; and that which rises from it becomes a very "subtle" <note>fine or light</note> substance, which is of no value to this work, because it is the medium through which burning <note>original: "adustio"</note> is completed in **Sulfur**. 

Therefore, the whole intention of **Sublimation** is this: that once its earthiness <note>original: "terreitate"</note> is removed through the proper administration of fire, and after rejecting its most subtle and smoky part—which causes burning and corruption—there remains for us that part which consists in such a state of balance <note>original: "æqualitate"</note> that it performs a simple melting <note>original: "fusionem"</note> over the fire without any burning, while also fleeing from the fire <note>evaporating</note> without catching flame. 

That the most subtle part is indeed combustible is proven by the most obvious arguments: because fire converts into its own nature each thing that is most similar to it. But in every combustible thing, that which is subtle is more similar to fire, and that which is even more subtle is even more similar still. Therefore, that which is most subtle is the most similar of all to the nature of fire. 

Likewise, this is proven by experience, since **Sulfur** or **Arsenic** that have not been sublimated catch fire very quickly—**Sulfur** indeed more easily. However, the sublimated substance does not catch fire directly, but flies away and thins out without burning, though melting occurs first. Thus, from these things, it is clear that our discourse is true. 

<summary>This page describes the final stages of sublimation, explaining that the goal is to remove both the "earthy" impurities and the highly flammable "subtle" parts to achieve a stable, purified substance. It argues through both logic and observation that purification makes volatile minerals like Sulfur resistant to catching fire.</summary>

# BOOK I.

<page-num>59</page-num>

...the proof <note>original: "probatio"; completing the word from the previous page</note> indeed, regarding the administration of the dregs <note>original: "fæcum"; the solid material placed in the bottom of the vessel to hold back impurities</note> in their proper proportion, is this: that a material should be chosen with which the **spirits** <note>volatile substances like Sulfur or Arsenic that can "fly" or vaporize when heated</note> to be sublimated are most compatible, and with which they may be most deeply mixed. This is because a substance to which they are more closely united is more powerful in retaining the impurities of those things being sublimated than one to which they are not; the reasonable proof of this is manifest enough. 

The proof that the administration of dregs is necessary is this: if the **Sulfur** or **Arsenic** to be sublimated is not joined with the dregs of some "fixed" <note>original: "refixa"; a substance that has been made resistant to the heat of the fire</note> material, then it would necessarily ascend with its entire substance remaining unpurified. Those who are practiced in **sublimations** know through experience that we speak the truth. 

The proof that it is necessary to provide such dregs—to which the substances to be sublimated are suited and united in their very depths—is this: if the dregs were not mixed down to the smallest parts <note>original: "per minima"; an alchemical term referring to mixing at the level of the smallest particles</note>, then the same result would occur as if they had no dregs at all. Namely, their entire substance would ascend without any purification. For if they ascend with their whole substance when they are without dregs, then it follows that they must also ascend when they are sublimated from dregs with which they are not properly united. 

Experience knows this to be necessary. Anyone who has seen and known this understands that when he has sublimated from a substance foreign to their nature, he has sublimated in vain, finding the materials in no way purified after their ascent. But if he has sublimated with the **calx** <note>original: "calce"; the fine powder or "ash" produced by roasting a metal or mineral</note> of some "body" <note>usually referring to a metal like copper or tin</note>, then he has sublimated well, and has been able to purify the substance perfectly and with ease. It is therefore...

# BOOK II

<page-num>60</page-num>

<header>S E C O N D</header>

...the purpose of **dregs** <note>original: "fæcum"; the solid, non-volatile matter placed at the bottom of a vessel to filter or anchor the substance being purified</note> is that they should be provided from the **calxes of metals** <term>calxes: the powdery remains or oxides of metals after they have been burnt or "calcined"</term>. For within these, the process of **sublimation** is easy to perform, whereas with other substances it is most difficult. Therefore, there is nothing else that can take their place. 

We do not say that sublimation is impossible without the calxes of bodies <note>original: "calcibus corporum"; "bodies" is the standard alchemical term for the seven metals</note>, but rather that it is most difficult and involves the longest labor and a delay that leads even to despair. However, there is this much goodness in that method: whatever is sublimated without dregs, or without the calxes of bodies, results in a great quantity. If it is done with dregs, the quantity is less; and furthermore, that which is done with the calxes of bodies results in the smallest amount of all, yet it is of the easiest labor and the shortest duration. 

That which most effectively relieves the need for dregs made of metallic bodies is every kind of **prepared salt** <note>original: "Salis præparati"; salt that has been purified or chemically altered, such as common salt or sal ammoniac</note> and things of a similar nature. For us, sublimation with salts yields a large quantity, and it is possible to separate the dregs from the substances being sublimated through the **dissolving of the salts** <note>because salt dissolves in water while many metallic impurities do not, the alchemist can easily recover his material</note>, which cannot happen with any other materials. 

The proper proportion of dregs is such that the quantity of the dregs is proportional to the quantity of the material to be sublimated. In this matter, even an unrefined craftsman cannot err; for a moderate craftsman, setting half the weight of the dregs relative to the weight of the material to be sublimated is sufficient, and in this he ought not to fail. For the practiced and especially the expert, the smallest portion of them suffices. For the smaller the quantity of the dregs, the greater the abundance of the sublimation [needs... <meta>catchword: "ces-" from the original "necessaria" or "necessitatem"</meta>]

<summary>This page discusses the efficiency of using metallic calxes and prepared salts as the "dregs" or solid medium in the sublimation process. It explains the trade-off between the ease of the operation (fastest with metals) and the final yield (highest when no dregs are used, but extremely difficult).</summary>

# BOOK II

<page-num>61</page-num>

->**L I B E R.**<-
<note>original: "LIBER" - Book [II]</note>

...is necessary for them to be present: provided that the reduction of the fire is made proportional to the variation in the removal of the **dregs** <note>original: "fæcum"; the solid residue remaining after sublimation</note>. For with a small quantity of dregs, a small fire should be used to reach the perfection of the **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again on a cooler surface, used to purify substances</term>; with a large quantity, a large fire; and with a greater quantity, an even greater fire. 

But because fire is not a thing that can be measured <note>meaning there were no thermometers to give an exact numerical value for the heat</note>, it happens very often that one who is not experienced in this work goes astray, both due to the variety of the proportion of the dregs, and also due to the variation in the **Furnace** and the wood being burned, and similarly of the vessels and their fitting together; about all of which it is necessary for the attentive craftsman to be concerned. 

But we provide a general rule: first, it is useful to use a small fire to drive out only the wateriness from the materials to be sublimated. Once this moisture is removed, if anything rises because of that fire, do not increase the heat at the beginning, so that by the weakest fire the most subtle part may be removed and set aside separately—for this is otherwise the cause of **burning** <note>original: "adustionis"; the scorching or chemical degradation of the substance due to excessive heat</note>. 

When, however, nothing or very little rises anymore—which can be tested by inserting **cotton** <note>original: "bombacis"</note> or a **wick** <note>original: "licinii"; a piece of lint or cord used as a probe</note> into the lower opening of the **Aludel** <term>aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic or glass vessel used in sublimation to collect the purified vapors</term>—then let the fire beneath it be strengthened; and the experience of the wick will show how strong the fire ought to be. For if only a little of the substance to be sublimated comes out and it is clean, the fire is still too low; let the fire be increased. If, however, much comes out but it is unclean, then the heat is excessive; let the fire be reduced. But if it is both plentiful and clean, then the correct proportion has been found. Whether it is clean or unclean, much or little, can be discovered...

If the distance is smaller, it will produce a smaller fire: in all these things, it is very common to make mistakes.

The correction of these errors, then, is that the furnace should be constructed according to the desired intensity of the fire: thick-walled with open vents and a large distance between the **Aludel** <term>Aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic pot used in alchemy, often stacked in series, to collect the solid results of sublimation</term> and the furnace supports, if one seeks a great fire. We shall teach you to find all these proportions through true measurement and determined experience.

...**two fingers** thick. Once the **Furnace** is built, you should make four **air-holes** <note>original: "auriculas"; literally "little ears," referring to the vents or intake holes</note> for it, spaced equally and in correct proportion, so that the heat of the fire is distributed evenly to all its parts.

Then, an **iron rod** <note>original: "stipite ferreo"</note> should be secured in the middle of the furnace upon its **ledges** <note>original: "spondilibus"; internal supports or ribs</note>. This rod should be placed at a distance of one palm's width (including the thumb) from the bottom of the furnace. On top of this, at a height of one finger's thickness, the **Aludel** <term>Aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic pot used to collect the solid results of sublimation</term> vessel should be fixed and sealed with clay to the furnace, as shown in the following diagram <note>the original text refers to a "present description" or illustration</note>.

If the **air-holes** allow the smoke and flame to escape freely and well, and if the fire passes freely throughout the entire furnace around the Aludel, then the proportions are correct. If not, they are not. In that case, widen the air-holes; if this fixes the issue, all is well. If it does not, it inevitably follows that the distance between the Aludel and the furnace walls is too small. Therefore, the walls should be scraped back to increase this distance, and then it should be tested again. The widening of the air-holes and the adjustment of the **ledges** <note>original: "spondilium"</note> must be repeated until the furnace clears itself of smoke, the flame around the Aludel is bright, and it exits freely through the vents. This provides sufficient practical knowledge for any quantity of **sublimation** <note>the process of turning a solid into vapor and back to solid to purify it</note>, regarding the determination of the furnace's size, the widening of its vents, and the proper distance between the Aludel and its walls.

BOOK.

## On the Thickness of the Furnace.
### CHAPTER IV.

The determination of the thickness of the furnace walls is as follows: if you require a great fire, the thickness should be greater, measuring the span of a palm including the thumb. <note>The author uses bodily measurements—the "palm with its thumb" refers to a full hand-span—to provide practical building ratios for heat retention.</note> If, however, a moderate fire is needed, it should be the measure of a palm alone; and if a smaller fire, it should be formed to the thickness of two fingers.

## On the Proportion of Wood.
### CHAPTER V.

Similarly, the proportion of the wood must be observed. Solid wood provides a strong fire, while spongy <note>original: "spongiosa"; likely referring to porous, light, or partially decayed wood</note> wood provides a weak one. Dry wood yields a large fire that ends quickly, while green wood yields a small fire that lasts a long time. Likewise, solid wood lasts a long time, whereas spongy wood provides a fire that is substantial but easily extinguished.

Therefore, by considering the distance of the aludel <term>aludel: a series of pear-shaped pots or tubes used as condensers in the process of sublimation</term>, the size (large or small) of the vents <term>auricularum: literally "little ears," these are the side-openings or air-holes that regulate draft</term>, the thickness or thinness of the furnace walls, and the variety of wood used, one can investigate the different qualities of all fires through true experience. 

By adjusting the width of these vents or the furnace window—through which the wood is fed—and by managing the variety of wood and its addition or removal, one can determine the exact duration of the fire. In this way, certain knowledge is gained regarding how long each fire can be maintained at its specific intensity and with steady consistency.

## *On the Shape of the Aludel.*

### CHAPTER VI.

The design of the vessel called the aludel <term>aludel: a pear-shaped chemical apparatus used as a condenser in the sublimation process, where vapors turn back into solids</term> is such that it should be fashioned as a thick vessel of glass. Indeed, it would not be effective if made of any other material, unless perhaps that substance was similar in nature to glass. For only glass and materials like it—since they lack pores—are able to hold spirits <term>spirits: in alchemy, these are volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or ammonia that vaporize when heated</term> so that they do not escape or be destroyed by the fire. 

Other materials do not work, because through their pores the spirits are gradually diminished and vanish. Nor are metals effective for this purpose, because spirits—through "friendship" <note>original: "amicitiam"; refers to chemical affinity or the tendency of certain substances to react and bond with metals</note> and their natural affinity—penetrate into the metals and unite with them. For this reason, spirits passing through metal vessels simply vanish. This is clearly proven by the principles we have established, and experience has found that we have spoken the truth here. Therefore, nothing else excuses us from using glass in the construction of the aludel.

Let there be fashioned, therefore, a round glass bowl, the bottom of which should have a slight curvature. In the middle of its rim <note>original: "spondilium"; literally a vertebra or joint, here referring to the connecting edge or shoulder of the vessel</note> let a glass band be formed surrounding it on the outside, and upon that band let a round wall be cast, e... <meta>catchword: que; likely "equally" or "evenly" (æqualiter)</meta>

<summary>This page explains why glass is the only suitable material for an aludel, as its non-porous nature prevents volatile "spirits" from escaping or reacting with the vessel itself.</summary>
<keywords>aludel, glass-making, spirits, chemical affinity, laboratory apparatus, sublimation, porosity</keywords>

...through the multiplication [of cycles], they may fall into it, toward its bottom, and the multitude of repeated <term>sublimations</term> <note>Sublimation is the process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then condensed back into a solid to purify it.</note> will keep you occupied for a long time. There is also another goal: that what rises as powder toward the opening of the <term>Aludel</term> <note>An Aludel is a series of pear-shaped earthenware pots used as condensers in the sublimation process.</note> head should always be kept separate from that which is found melted, dense, and in pieces. This latter material is found near the bottom, clear and transparent, having climbed and adhered to the joints <note>original: "spondilia"; referring to the segments or ridges where the parts of the vessel meet.</note> of the vessel. This is because this lower material has less <term>adustion</term> <note>Adustion refers to the "burnt" or "scorched" quality of a substance, representing combustible impurities that must be removed.</note> than that which is found to have climbed near the opening. This is clearly proven in the preceding sections, both by reason and by experiment. The proof of the quality and perfection of <term>sublimation</term> has already been stated: it is that which is found clear and bright, and does not burn when exposed to flame. This, therefore, is the perfect goal of the <term>sublimation</term> of <term>Sulfur</term> and <term>Arsenic</term>. If it is not found in this state, the work must be repeated upon it, considering all its intended goals, until it is found to be so.

## *On the <term>Sublimation</term> of <term>Mercury</term>.*
### CHAPTER VII.

Now, let us determine the entire goal of the <term>sublimation</term> of <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argenti vivi"; the common name for liquid mercury.</note>. The complete sum of its purpose is the purification of its <term>earthiness</term> <note>original: "terreitatis"; the heavy, solid, and impure qualities associated with the element of Earth.</note> and the removal of its <term>wateriness</term> <note>original: "aqueitatis"; the excessive moisture or phlegmatic quality that makes mercury too fluid.</note>. Indeed, we are excused from the labor of removing its <term>adustion</term>, since it does not

[it] has, but only flight <note>original: "fugam"; referring to the volatility of Mercury, which "flees" from the fire.</note> and superfluous earthiness. Therefore, we say that the method of separating its superfluous earth is mixing it with things with which it has no affinity, and repeating its <term>sublimation</term> from them many times. These substances include <term>talc</term>, the <term>calx</term> <note>Calx is the powdery residue left after a substance has been roasted or "calcined" to remove volatile components.</note> of eggshells, or of white marble, and similarly very finely ground glass and every kind of prepared salt. For it is cleansed by these; but by other things with which it has an affinity—unless they are bodies in a state of perfection, such as the <term>Sun</term> <note>Gold</note> and the <term>Moon</term> <note>Silver</note>—it is not cleansed but rather corrupted. This is because all such things possess a <term>sulfureity</term> <note>The "sulfureity" refers to the presence of combustible or impure sulfur-like qualities that can "stain" the metal during heating.</note> which, rising with it during <term>sublimation</term>, corrupts it. You can see the proof of this in experience: if you sublimate it from <term>Tin</term> or <term>Lead</term>, you will see it stained with blackness after the <term>sublimation</term>. Therefore, its <term>sublimation</term> is better performed with those things with which it does not agree. It would be better with those it agrees with if they did not have <term>sulfureity</term>. For this reason, <term>sublimation</term> from <term>calx</term> is better than from all other things, because it has little affinity and possesses no <term>sulfureity</term>. The method for removing its superfluous wateriness is this: when it is mixed with the <term>calxes</term> from which it must be <term>sublimated</term>, it should be ground and mixed with them through <term>imbibitions</term> <note>Imbibition is the process of soaking or moistening a solid substance with a liquid so it can be absorbed.</note> until none of it <note>original: "Argenti vivi"; Mercury</note> is visible; and afterwards, over a very slow fire, the wateriness of the <term>imbibition</term> is removed. As this departs, the wateriness of the <term>Mercury</term> departs along with it; yet the fire must be so light that through it [the] <term>Mercury</term>...

the whole substance does not ascend <note>original: "tota substantia non ascendat." This refers to the material not vaporizing entirely, leaving impurities behind.</note>. And when you see it appearing very white—exceeding snow in its whiteness—and sticking to the **joints** <note>original: "spondilibus"; likely referring to the connecting ridges or segments where the parts of the vessel meet.</note> of the **Aludel** <note>A series of pear-shaped earthen pots used as condensers in the sublimation process.</note> as if it were "dead" <note>This implies the Mercury has lost its fluid, "living" character and has become a stable, white solid.</note>: then repeat the **sublimation** upon itself without the dregs.

We will expressly provide the method of its **fixation** in what follows. Once you have fixed it, repeat the remaining part of the **sublimation** over it, so that it may be similarly fixed. Watch closely when you test it over the fire: if it produces a good **fusion** <note>Meaning it melts properly and uniformly.</note>, then you have repeated the **sublimation** sufficiently.

If it does not, add some part of **sublimated Quicksilver** to it, and repeat the **sublimation** until it becomes fusible. If it possesses a bright, pure, and very white color, then you have cleansed it well; if not, you have not. Therefore, do not be negligent in its preparation through **sublimation**, because the quality of the cleansing determines the quality of the **perfection** that will follow during **projection** <note>The final stage of the "Great Work" where the prepared alchemical agent is cast upon base metals to transform them into gold or silver.</note>—whether upon any of the **imperfect bodies** <note>The "base" metals like lead or iron.</note> or upon unprepared **Quicksilver** itself.
