## *On the Sublimation of Marcasite.*
### C H A P T E R VIII.

The core intention for the **sublimation** of **Quicksilver** <term>Mercury (Argentum vivum)</term> having now been sufficiently delivered, let us proceed to the **sublimation** of **Marcasite** <term>A mineral (iron sulfide) often used in this period to describe metallic ores that have a brassy luster but have not yet been refined into pure metal</term>. There are, however, two ways to sublimate it. The first is performed without **ignition** <note>Heating the vessel until it glows red-hot.</note>. The second is performed with **ignition**, and this is because it possesses a dual substance: namely, one is pure **Sulfur** in its matter; the other substance is **mortified Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argentum vivum mortificatum." This refers to Mercury that has been chemically altered so it is no longer a liquid, "living" metal.</note>.

The first part is useful just like **Sulfur**; the second is useful as **mortified Quicksilver**, specifically when it is moderately prepared. Let us therefore take up this latter one, since by it we are excused from having to obtain [fresh] **Quicksilver** and from the heavy labors of its **mortification**. The entire method of its **sublimation**, therefore, is that it be ground and placed in an **Aludel** <term>A series of pear-shaped ceramic pots used as condensers in the sublimation process</term>, and its **Sulfur** be sublimated without **ignition**: always removing very frequently what is sublimated from it, for the reason already stated; and increasing the strength of the fire until the **ignition** of the **Aludel** and its first...

The author highlights Marcasite as a valuable substitute for Mercury because it provides "mortified" material without the difficult labor of fixing liquid Mercury.</summary>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>S E C O N D</header>

The **sublimation** <note>The process of heating a substance into vapor and collecting it as a solid again.</note> of **Marcasite** <term>A metallic mineral, likely iron pyrite or a similar sulfide</term> ought to be performed in a vessel intended for the **sublimation** of **Sulfur**, until the **Sulfur** has withdrawn; and afterward, in that same vessel successively and in an orderly manner, until everything that consists of **Sulfur** within it has departed. You will be able to prove this by these manifest experiments:

For when all of its **Sulfur** has been elevated <note>Vaporized and risen to the top of the vessel.</note>, you will see the color of that which is sublimated after it change into a most brilliant white, mixed with a very bright and pleasant celestial <note>original: "cæleſtino." A sky-blue or azure color.</note> color. Otherwise, that which is of the nature of **Sulfur** will be burned and will give a flame like **Sulfur**; but that which has been sublimated second after it will neither be inflamed nor show any properties of **Sulfur**, but rather those of **mortified Quicksilver** <note>Mercury that has been chemically altered so it is no longer a liquid, "living" metal.</note> in the repetition of the **sublimation**.

Therefore, let us collect that substance by its own method of **sublimation**, which is as follows: let a most solid and well-baked **earthen vessel** <note>Pottery made of clay</note> be made, to the length of half a man's height; and to a width through which a hand can enter. Let a base be made which can be separated and joined, in the likeness of a very deep platter. Let it be measured from the opening of that vessel to near the bottom, to the measure of the length of one hand including its fingers; and from that place up to its head, let the vessel be **glazed** <term>Vitrification (vitrificatio); applying a glass-like coating to the interior to prevent the vapors from escaping through the porous clay</term> on the inside with a very thick glazing. Upon its head, let an **alembic** <term>A helmet-shaped cap used in distillation and sublimation to catch and condense vapors</term> with a wide snout be placed; for in such a vessel it is sublimated.

Therefore, let the base be joined with its vessel by a **clay** <note>original: "lutum." A sealant or "lute" used to make the apparatus airtight.</note> of firm tenacity, and upon that base let the **Marcasite** be spread, and upon the head...

# THE APPARATUS AND THE FURNACE #

...separated <note>Continuing from the previous page: "Let a base be made which can be separ[ated]." -> **Figure of the Distillation Vessel** <-

<note>The following illustration depicts the complete chemical assembly described in the text.</note>

*The Distillation Apparatus:*

The furnace base is rectangular, constructed of sturdy brick or clay, with a small arched opening at the bottom center to serve as a fire door or ash pit. Within the belly of the **furnace** <term>The heat source or "athanor" in alchemical practice</term>, the coals are laid to provide a constant heat.

Resting atop the furnace is the **cucurbit** <term>A gourd-shaped vessel containing the raw material to be heated</term>, featuring a wide mouth to receive the prepared minerals. From this vessel, a tall, narrow vertical neck extends upwards, terminating in a domed **alembic** <term>The "helm" or cap of the still where vapors cool and condense</term>. 

A curved beak or spout projects downwards from the side of the cap; it is through this channel that the purified **distillation** <note>The process of heating a substance to create vapor and then cooling that vapor into a liquid or solid.</note> is gathered into a separate receiving vessel.

### THE ARRANGEMENT OF THE FIRE ###

In this **alchemy** <note>The historical practice of chemical philosophy aimed at purification and transformation.</note>, the regulation of the flame is of the highest importance. The **chemical apparatus** must be sealed tightly so that the volatile spirits do not escape into the air. One must ensure the heat is sufficient to lift the **sublimation** but not so fierce as to break the earthenware.

...the vessel of the alembic <note>a lid or "helmet" used in distillation to collect and cool vapors</note> with a wide nose, and let it be placed in the furnace: its property should be to provide strong heat, specifically for the melting of silver <term>Argentum: silver</term> or copper <term>Venus: the alchemical name for copper</term>, if the craftsman should require it. Seal the joints <note>luta: to seal with "lute," a fire-resistant clay or paste used to make airtight seals</note> around the vessel and the furnace, so that the escaping fire does not hinder you or the adhesion of the sublimation <note>the process where a solid turns into vapor and then back to solid on a cool surface</note>. There should also be six or eight holes about the size of a little finger, which should never be closed, so that through them the furnace can freely clear itself of smoke. Let those holes be at the junction of the furnace and its cover.

This is indeed a furnace for great heat, whose supports <note>spondilia: the walls or framework of the furnace</note> should be at a height of two cubits <note>approximately 3 feet</note>. In the middle, there should be a grate <note>original: "rota," here referring to the interior circular grate</note> pierced with very small and very frequent holes, firmly attached with lute. Let the tops of the holes be narrow and the bottoms wide, so that ash and coals can fall more freely through them, and keep them continuously open for the intake of fresh air. For the free and abundant intake of air through the lower holes is the single cause of the great heat throughout the furnace; therefore, you should practice

# SECOND [BOOK]

...and you will find it in there. The reason for the length of the vessel is so that a large portion of it extends outside the fire and is cooled; this is so the vapors of the sublimation <term>sublimatio: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collects as a solid on a cooler surface</term> find a place of cooling and adhere to it, and do not find a way to escape and be lost. This is well known to the person who, while sublimating in short aludels <term>Aludel: a pear-shaped pot or tube used as a condenser in the sublimation process</term>, found nothing of the sublimated material, because due to the shortness of the aludel, the heat was equal throughout the whole vessel. Therefore, the substance to be sublimated remained constantly converted into the form of a vapor and did not adhere anywhere, but instead gradually vanished through the pores of the vessel. In all sublimations, therefore, the vessel should extend for the greater part of its length into a place where it can be cooled.

The reason for the glazing <term>vitrificatio: the process of making the ceramic surface glass-like and non-porous</term> of the vessel is so that the rising vapors, in the place of their ascent, do not find the wall of the aludel to be porous and escape by penetrating through it. The place of their ascent is glazed so that their path of escape is contained <note>original: "reservetur illis via fugæ"; literally "the way of escape be reserved for them," implying the vapors are kept within the designated internal path rather than leaking out through the ceramic walls</note>; the bottom of the aludel, however, is not glazed, because the bottom itself sits in a great fire. Such heat would melt the glaze; if that melted, both the bottom and the substance to be sublimated would melt together and turn into glass. For it is a property of glass to melt everything and convert it to its own nature.

Therefore, once all these things and their causes have been considered, let the fire be made under the aludel until you are certain by true experience <note>original: "experientia," referring here to a practical test or trial</note> that the whole substance has ascended. This experience consists of inserting a well-baked earthenware rod, which has a small hole in its head reaching to its middle, about the size of a little finger...

# *On the Sublimation of Magnesia and Tutty.*
## CHAPTER IX.

The goal for the sublimation of <term>Magnesia</term> <note>Often referring to various magnesium minerals or sometimes used as a generic term for a "transformative" mineral.</note> and <term>Tutty</term> <note>Crude zinc oxide, usually collected from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note> is the same as the goal for the final sublimation of <term>Marcasite</term> <note>A term used historically for iron pyrites or other metallic-looking sulfides.</note>. None of these can be sublimated without <term>ignition</term> <note>The application of strong, direct fire or intense heat.</note>; therefore, they all share a single purpose, governed by the same principles and experimental trials. They follow one general rule: whatever must be sublimated by means of fire ought to be sublimated without adding extra <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "faeces"; refers to the sedimentary impurities or additives alchemists sometimes used to assist the process.</note>, because they contain sufficient—even excessive—impurities within themselves. The proof of this is the inherent difficulty of their sublimation.

---

# *On the Sublimation of Diminished Bodies.*
## CHAPTER X.

Similarly, all "diminished bodies" <note>Metals considered "imperfect" or "base" in alchemical theory, such as lead, copper, or tin, which were believed to be "diminished" versions of gold.</note> are sublimated; this follows the same order, and no difference occurs except that the fire for sublimation is more intense for these metallic bodies than it is for Magnesia, Marcasite, and Tutty. These bodies do not differ in their sublimation processes except that...

...ished bodies <note>original: "corpora"; in this context, the term refers to the base metals like copper or iron.</note> require the mixing in of a substance that lifts them; others indeed do not, so that their elevation <note>The process of rising as a vapor during sublimation.</note> may become easier. 

But one specific consideration and good practice found through experience in the sublimation of bodies is this: namely, that there should not be a large quantity of the body to be sublimated in the bottom of the <term>Aludel</term> <note>A pear-shaped ceramic or glass vessel used in alchemical sublimation, often stacked in multiple units to collect different grades of vapor.</note>, because a great mass hinders the sublimation. Additionally, there should be a flat surface at the bottom of the sublimation Aludel, and not a concavity, so that the body, spread evenly and thinly over the bottom, can be elevated equally; and that all its parts may be elevated fully and uniformly. 

The bodies indeed requiring the mixture of a "lifting" substance are <term>Venus</term> <note>The alchemical name for copper.</note> and <term>Mars</term> <note>The alchemical name for iron.</note>, on account of the slowness of their melting. Venus certainly requires <term>Tutty</term> <note>Crude zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note>; Mars indeed requires <term>Arsenic</term>; and with these they are elevated easily since they agree most closely with them. Therefore, after considering these things, let the sublimation be performed as it is for Tutty and its likenesses, and let their sublimation be arranged in this same order, following its specific causes and experimental trials.

# *On Descent.*

## CHAPTER XI.

Now that the goals of sublimation have been related along with all their causes, it remains for us now to show the method of <term>Descent</term> <note>Also known as *descensio*, this is a process where a substance is heated so that its essence or liquid form flows downward into a receiving vessel, rather than rising as in sublimation.</note>, with its causes, and likewise with its order determined and completed. Moreover, the reason for its discovery was threefold. One, namely, so that when some medicine <note>In alchemy, a "medicine" or "elixir" refers to any substance used to transform or "heal" base metals into noble ones.</note> is enclosed in that vessel,

...vessel, which is called the **Alchemist’s Descensory**, so that after melting <note>original: "fusionem"; the act of liquifying the metal through heat.</note> it may descend through its opening; and then we are certain that the melting has occurred. Another reason for this is so that the bodies <note>original: "corpora"; the metals being worked upon.</note> may be protected from a "weak combustion" <note>This refers to the unintended burning or oxidation of the metal if it remains in the heat too long.</note> by that descent, following their reduction from their **calxes** <note>original: "calcibus"; a "calx" is the crumbly, powdery substance (usually a metallic oxide) left over after a metal has been roasted or "calcined."</note>.

For when we attempt to reduce <note>In alchemy, "reduction" is the process of restoring a burnt powder back into a solid, usable metal.</note> weak bodies from their calxes, we are unable to reduce every part of them at the same time. If, therefore, that part which was first reduced back into a body had to wait for the rest of the reduction while still being subjected to the fire, a greater quantity of those parts would then vanish <note>The author is concerned that the restored metal would vaporize or turn back into smoke if left over the heat.</note>. It was necessary, therefore, to devise a way so that as soon as a part is reduced, it is removed from the fire. This is accomplished by means of the **descensory**.

There is also a third reason for the invention of this method for the bodies: it purifies them from every foreign substance. For the melted body descends clean, and leaves every foreign matter behind in the cavity of the vessel. Having established these necessities, and the method of "descent" <note>Downward distillation, where the product drips into a lower container.</note> having been invented, let its procedure be determined along with its previously described instrument and its underlying reasons.

We say, therefore, that its form should be such that its bottom is pointed and its walls are smooth and even, ending at the point of the bottom without any obstruction <note>original: "sine scrupulo"; without roughness or snagging points.</note>, so that everything may descend freely without sticking toward the hole at the bottom. And its lid, if necessary, should be shaped like a flat **dish** <note>original: "parapsidis"; a shallow bowl or plate.</note>, and should fit the vessel well; it should be made of good and strong **clay** <note>original: "terra"; literally "earth," referring here to ceramic material.</note> that does not easily melt under the pressure of the fire. Therefore, let the substance intended for descent be placed upon round rods, made of—

We shall, therefore, describe its method along with the other.

We then mix the calx with something that is not intended to melt, such as **egg white** <note>original: "albumen ovi"</note> and soap, and afterwards we melt it. We find that bodies are cleansed by this through many repetitions, though not with that "perfect" purification which we know to exist, but rather a "useful" purification. This is done so that when the **medicine of perfection** <note>The "medicine" refers to the transformative substance (the Elixir or Philosopher’s Stone) that the alchemist intends to apply to the metal later.</note> is received, the bodies may be more easily and perfectly transformed by it. For this is a preliminary administration...

### *On the threefold distillation, namely by*
### *Alembic, by Descent-distiller, and*
### *Filter.*

## CHAPTER XII.

It is appropriate, therefore, as we follow our intended path, to deliver a discourse on distillation along with its causes. Distillation, then, is the rising of watery vapors within their vessel. Indeed, distillation is performed in various ways. For some is done by fire, and some without fire. That which is done by fire is found to be of two kinds. One is by elevation into an Alembic <note>original: *alembicum*; the upper part of a still, resembling a cap or hood, where vapors collect and are channeled into a cooling tube</note>. The other is by chemical descent, through which oil is extracted from vegetables. 

The general reason for the invention of any distillation is the purification of a liquid from dregs and its cloudiness, and its preservation from rot <term>putrefaction: the decomposition or organic decay of matter, which alchemists sought to prevent to stabilize their medicines</term>. For we see that a distilled thing, by whatever kind of distillation, is made purer and is better protected from decay. Now, the specific reason for the invention of that distillation which is done by ascent into an alembic is the search for pure water without "earth" <note>In alchemical theory, "earth" refers to the solid, impure sediment or "feces" left behind after a substance is heated</note>. The proof of this is that you see distilled water has no dregs. The reason for the invention of pure water was the moistening <note>original: *imbibitio*; the process of a solid substance absorbing a liquid, often to soften it for further transformation</note> of many medi-

<summary>This page introduces Chapter XII, defining distillation as the elevation of vapors and categorizing it into three types: the alembic (upward), the descensory (downward), and the filter. It explains that the primary goal of distillation is the purification of liquids and the prevention of decay.</summary>

<keywords>distillation, alembic, descensory, purification, alchemy, putrefaction, imbibition, chemical oils</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the purposes of distillation, specifically focusing on the need for pure solvents and the distinction between the various methods of distilling (ascent, descent, and filtration).</meta>

-># SECOND [BOOK]<-

...of clean waters, so that whenever we require imbibition <term>imbibition: the alchemical process of a solid substance absorbing a liquid, often described as "feeding" the material to mature it</term>, we may have pure water that does not leave behind any dregs after it has been dissolved. If such dregs were present, our medicines and Spirits could not be purified, but would instead be tainted and corrupted. 

The reason for the invention of distillation by descent <note>original: *per descensum*; a method where the heat is applied from above or around the vessel so that the resulting oils or liquids flow downward into a receiver, preventing them from burning</note> was the extraction of pure oil in its natural state, since oil—due to its combustible nature—could not be successfully obtained through ascent. The goal of this investigation was to ensure that its color, which is mixed with its substance, might be preserved; for this can be helpful in certain cases. 

Truly, the reason for the invention of distillation by Filter <note>original: *per Filtrum*; a method of cold distillation using capillary action, where a cloth or wick draws liquid from one vessel to another</note>, which is performed without fire, was solely to achieve the clarity of the water. 

Let us therefore discuss every method of distillation along with its specific causes. There are two modes of that distillation which is performed by ascent. One is carried out using an earthen pot filled with ashes <note>original: *cineribus*; referring to an ash-bath or sand-bath, used to provide a high, even heat</note>; the other is done with water, with the vessel arranged in its proper order using grasses or woolly padding. This is done lest the cucurbit <note>original: *cucurbita*; the gourd-shaped lower vessel of a still where the substance is boiled</note> or the distillation Alembic be broken before the process is brought to completion. 

That which is done with ashes is performed with a greater, stronger, and sharper fire; however, that which is done with water requires a gentle and even fire. For water does not absorb the sharpness of the fire in the same way that ashes do. Therefore, through distillation with ashes, it happens that colors and the coarser earthy parts are raised upward. But through that which is done with water, the parts raised are more subtle, colorless, and closely approach the nature of simple wateriness. Thus, a more refined separation is achieved through that which...

# BOOK I.

...is accomplished with water, than by that which is drawn out through ashes <note>original: *cineribus*; referring to an ash-bath, a method of indirect heating similar to a sand-bath</note>. Experience knows well that this is true; for one who had distilled oil through ashes received the oil in the receiver scarcely altered. Therefore, wishing to separate its parts, he came by necessity to the point where he distilled it by water; and then, through the repetition of this process, he separated the oil into its purest elementary parts, such that he extracted the whitest and clearest water from the reddest oil, while the redness of that oil remained entirely at the bottom of the Alembic <term>Alembic: a vessel used in distillation; here referring to the boiling pot where the "dregs" or color remain</term>. 

By this mastery <note>original: *magisterium*; the specialized skill or chemical operation of the alchemist</note>, therefore, it is necessary to arrive at the specific separation of elements for every vegetable substance, for those things which proceed from a vegetable into existence, and for all similar things. 

Truly, through the method which is by descent <note>original: *per descensum*; a distillation where the liquid is forced downward by heat applied from above</note>, one can specifically reach the oil of any substance—namely, of all vegetables and their like. 

Truly, through the method which is by Filter <note>original: *per Filtrum*; a cold distillation using a wick or fabric to draw clear liquid from one vessel to another</note>, one can similarly reach the clarity of any liquid. 

All these things become necessary and manifest to those who know even a little; but he who is ignorant of this knows nothing of this mastery. Therefore, let him practice, and he will investigate these things with ease. 

The arrangement, then, of the distillation which is done by ashes is as follows: take a strong earthen pot and fit it into the furnace—similar to a sublimation furnace <note>a furnace designed for the process of turning a solid into vapor</note>—with the same distance from the furnace to the supports, following the same investigation, and with similar handles. Upon the bottom of this pot, place sifted ashes...

# BOOK I. #

is accomplished with water, rather than through that which is drawn out by **ashes** <note>original: *cineribus*; an ash bath, or *balneum cinis*, provides a dry, steady heat</note>. Experience has well proven this to be true; for when one had distilled oil through ashes, the oil was received in the **receiver** <note>the vessel that catches the distilled liquid</note> scarcely altered. Therefore, wishing to separate its parts, he arrived by necessity at the conclusion that it should be distilled through water. Then, through its reiteration <note>repeated distillation</note>, he separated the oil into its purest elemental parts, such that he extracted the whitest and clearest water from the reddest oil, while the redness of that oil remained entirely in the bottom of the **Alembic** <note>the upper part of the still, often shaped like a helmet, where vapors condense</note>. 

Through this **mastery** <note>original: *magisterium*; a technical alchemical term for a specific process or perfected method</note>, therefore, it is necessary to arrive at the determined separation of all the elements of every vegetable thing, and of that which has proceeded into being from a vegetable, and of every similar thing. Indeed, by that distillation which is by **descent** <note>original: *descensum*; a method where heat is applied from above so the product flows downward</note>, one can specifically obtain the oil of any thing—namely, of all vegetables and those similar to them. However, by that which is done by way of a **Filter**, the clarity of any liquid can similarly be achieved. 

All these things become necessary and manifest to those who know even a little; but he who is ignorant of this knows nothing of this mastery. Let him therefore practice, and he will investigate these things with ease. 

The arrangement, therefore, of that distillation which is done through **ashes**, is as follows: take a strong earthenware pot and fit it into a **furnace**, similar to a **sublimation furnace** <note>a furnace designed for turning solids into vapor</note>, with the same distance from the furnace to the ledges <note>original: *spondilibus*; the supports or rims within the furnace</note>, following the same investigation, and with similar handles <note>original: *auriculis*; literally 'little ears' or lugs for lifting</note>. Upon the bottom of this pot, let **sifted** ashes be placed.

## BOOK TWO ##

...spread to the thickness of a finger, upon which the distillation vessel should be placed, and it should be covered around its circumference with the same **ashes** <note>original: *cineribus*; an ash bath provides a dry, even heat and protects glass from the direct flame</note> right up near the neck of the **Alembic** <note>the upper part or 'head' of the still where vapors condense</note>. After this, the substance intended to be distilled in this manner should be poured into it. Finally, cover it with the Alembic, whose neck should specifically receive the neck of the other vessel inside it, so that the substance to be distilled finds no way to escape. Then, the Alembic should be **luted** <note>original: *lutetur*; sealed with a paste called 'lute' (often made of clay, dung, or flour) to make the connection airtight</note> to the lower **cucurbit** <note>original: *cucurbita*; the bottom vessel of the still, shaped like a gourd</note> from the inside, up to the hollow of the channel of the Alembic itself with its cucurbit, so that their joint is made firm; then the fire should be applied until it distills. 

Both the Alembic and its cucurbit should be made of glass, and the fire should be increased according to the requirements of the distillation, until the whole is seen to have been distilled by the great force of the fire. 

Regarding the second method of distillation, which is done through water <note>the *balneum Mariae* or water bath</note>, the arrangement is similar to the first in terms of the vessel and the Alembic. However, it differs from it in this: that an iron or bronze pot is taken and fitted to the furnace as was previously described. Then, upon the bottom of that pot, a layer of grass, or wool, or some other similar material should be built up to the thickness of two or three fingers, so that the cucurbit does not break <note>the padding prevents the glass from cracking against the hot metal or from the vibration of boiling water</note>. The cucurbit is then covered around its circumference with the same grass or similar materials up to the neck of the Alembic. Upon these, thin twigs should be scattered, and heavy stones should be placed upon the twigs, which by their weight press down the cucurbit, the Alembic, and the twigs themselves; and once they are pressed down and made firm...

<meta>This page concludes the instructions for the water-bath (bain-marie) distillation method and introduces two other techniques: distillation by descent (downward distillation) and distillation by filter (capillary siphoning).</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
->**BOOK**<-

...should hold the vessels firmly and stably upon the bottom of the pot, so that they do not float as they are lifted by the water itself; for such floating would cause the vessel to break and the loss of the substance being distilled. After this, water should be poured among the **twigs** <note>original: *sarmenta*; these were placed around the glass vessels to act as a cushion and stabilizer within the boiling water</note> until the pot is full, and fire should be applied beneath until the whole is distilled.

The arrangement of that distillation which is performed **by descent**, however, is this: a glass **descending vessel** <term>descensorium: a specialized apparatus for "downward distillation," where the heat source is placed at the top and the vapors are forced to condense and flow into a receiver located beneath the furnace</term> should be prepared with its cover, and both should be **luted** <note>original: *lutetur*; sealed with a paste to ensure the connection is airtight</note>. The substance intended for this type of distillation is placed inside, and fire is applied over the top of it, for then its distilled essence will flow downward.

The arrangement of that distillation which is performed **by filter**, however, is this: the substance to be distilled is placed in a **stone basin** <note>original: *concham lapideam*; likely a shallow, bowl-shaped vessel</note>. A well-washed and dampened **filter** <note>original: *filtrum*; in this context, it refers to a strip of cloth or wick used for capillary distillation, where liquid travels along the fibers and drips off the end</note> should have its wider end placed into the liquid, reaching all the way to the bottom of the basin. The thinner part of the filter should hang outside the opening of the basin, and a **receiving vessel** should be placed under the tip of that filter to catch the distillation. When the filter begins to drip, the water with which it was first soaked will come out first. Once that stops, the actual liquid to be distilled will follow. If this liquid is not clear, it should be returned to the basin as many times as necessary until it distills perfectly clear. Because these procedures are straightforward, they do not require extensive proof; therefore, we conclude our demonstration of them.

A description and the manner of all vessels for any kind of distillation will now be presented by us.

# BOOK.

...hold firmly and steadily upon the bottom of the pot, so that they do not float, lifted up by the water itself, because this lifting would be the cause of the vessel's breaking and the loss of the matter to be distilled. Afterwards, pour water among the twigs <note>original: "sarmenta"; these were likely used as padding or a "nest" to cushion glass vessels within the metal or ceramic pot</note> until the pot is full, and apply fire beneath until the whole is distilled.

The arrangement for that distillation which is done **by descent** <term>distillation by descent: a method where the heat is applied from above, forcing the vapors or oils to move downward into a separate container below</term> is that a glass descending-vessel be made, with its cover, and both should be sealed with lute <term>lute: a paste or cement used to make alchemical joints airtight</term>. Whatever is intended to be distilled in this way is placed inside, and a fire is made over its head; for its distillation will descend.

The arrangement for that which is done **by Filter** is that the substance to be distilled is placed in a stone basin <note>original: "concham lapideam"; literally a stone shell-shaped vessel</note>. Then, take a well-washed and dampened filter and place its wider part into the liquid, down to the bottom of the basin. Let the narrower part of the filter hang out from the opening of the basin, and beneath the end of that filter, place a receiving vessel for the distillation. Therefore, when the filter begins to drip: first, the water with which it was dampened will drip out; when that stops, the liquid to be distilled will follow it. If this liquid is not clear, it should be returned to the basin as many times as necessary until it drips out perfectly clear. 

And because these things are all simple, they do not require great proof; therefore, we have finished our demonstration of them. The description and method of all the various distillation vessels will now be delivered by us.

-> SECOND [BOOK] <-

# On Calcination.
## CHAPTER XIII.

After the account of distillation, let us deliver a discourse on **Calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air, typically to remove impurities or reduce it to a powder</term>. Calcination is, therefore, the pulverizing of a dry substance by means of fire through the removal of the moisture that binds its parts together.

The reason for its discovery is so that the combustible, corrupting, and defiling sulfurous quality <note>original: "sulphureitas adustiva"; in alchemy, "sulfur" was often used to describe the principle of flammability and corruption within a metal</note> may be destroyed by fire. This process varies according to the variety of things to be calcined. For "bodies" <note>metals</note> are calcined, and "spirits" <note>volatile substances like mercury or arsenic</note> are calcined, as well as other things foreign to their nature, though for different purposes. 

Since there are two kinds of imperfect bodies—namely the hard ones, such as **Copper** <note>original: "Venus"</note> and **Iron** <note>original: "Mars"</note>, and the soft ones, such as **Tin** <note>original: "Jupiter"</note> and **Lead** <note>original: "Saturnus"</note>—all of which are calcined, it was necessary for them to be calcined with different intentions, both specific and general. Yet the one primary intention for why they are calcined is that the combustible sulfurous quality that corrupts and defiles them may be removed. For every thing in which there is combustible sulfurousness is burned by fire, and this cannot be destroyed without calcination. Because the body of the metal is solid, the sulfurous quality is hidden and protected from the fire by the continuity of the substance of **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argenti vivi"; referring to the theory that metals are composed of sulfur and mercury</note>. Therefore, it was necessary to break apart its continuity <note>to pulverize or open the structure</note>, so that the fire, reaching more freely to every tiny part of it, might burn away the sulfurous quality from it: and

<meta>The catchword "non" indicates the next page begins with a negation or the word "not."</meta>

<summary>This page defines calcination as the use of fire to pulverize substances by removing their moisture and "sulfurous" impurities. It explains that calcination is necessary to "open" the solid structure of metals like copper, iron, tin, and lead so that fire can reach and cleanse their internal corruption.</summary>
<keywords>calcination, sulfurous quality, copper, iron, tin, lead, quicksilver, purification, alchemical theory, metallurgy</keywords>

from fire by the continuity of the **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the common alchemical term for Mercury</term> within it. And the common goal in that process is similarly the purification of "earthiness" <note>original: "terreitatis"; refers to the coarse, non-metallic impurities or dross found in ores</note>. For it has been discovered that through the repetition of **calcination** and **reduction** <term>reduction: the process of restoring a calcined metal or oxide back to its metallic state</term> of these substances, the bodies are cleansed, as we shall soon write in the following sections.

But the specific calcination of soft bodies <note>metals with low melting points, like lead or tin</note> is such that, along with these two goals, there is the intention to harden them and make them withstand fire through that calcination. This is achieved by a clever repetition of calcination upon them, which it is necessary for us to describe in the following instruction. For we have found through that clever method that they are clearly hardened. But **Tin** <note>original: "Jupiter"</note> is hardened more clearly and more quickly.

Moreover, the reason for the discovery of the calcination of **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that "fly" from the fire</term> is so that they may be better **fixed** <term>fixed: made resistant to evaporation or change when heated</term> and more easily dissolved in water. For every kind of calcined substance is more fixed than one not calcined, and is of easier dissolution; because the calcined parts, having been made more subtle by the fire, are more easily mixed with waters and converted into liquid—he who is experienced knows this. 

Indeed, the calcination of other things was a requirement for the preparation of spirits and bodies, about which we will determine more extensively in the following. However, none of these things is of their final perfection. Therefore, the method of calcination is diverse, due to the diversity of the things to be calcined. For bodies are calcined differently than spirits or other things, and even the bodies differ from one another; likewise, they are calcined in different ways. For soft bodies [have] one method...

<summary>This page explains that calcination serves to purify metals of "earthiness" and to harden soft metals like tin so they can withstand higher heat. It also notes that calcining volatile "spirits" makes them easier to dissolve in water and more stable under fire.</summary>

SECOND [BOOK]

they have a general goal for **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to oxidize it or change its physical state</term>, namely, that both can be calcined by fire alone. And whether they are prepared through the sharpness of salt or left unprepared, both are treated similarly. First, therefore, the process by fire alone is completed in this way: an iron or earthenware vessel should be taken, shaped in the likeness of a shallow bowl <note>original: "parapsidis"; a side-dish or plate-like vessel</note>, whose structure is firm. It should be fitted to the calcination furnace in such a way that coals can be thrown and blown under it.

Then, **Lead** or **Tin** should be cast into the bottom of that vessel, which should sit firmly upon an iron tripod or columns made of three stones. It should likewise be secured to the walls of its furnace, with four or three stones wedging it against the walls so that the vessel cannot move. The shape of the furnace should be the same as the form of the "furnace of great ignition" <note>likely referring to a blast furnace or high-heat furnace described in an earlier chapter</note>, about which we have already spoken, and about which we will speak again in a more complete discourse. 

Therefore, let a fire be lit in that furnace under the calcination vessel, strong enough to melt the metal body being calcined. When the melted body has created a black "skin" upon itself by the heat of the fire, this skin should be scraped away from it with an iron or stone shovel <term>pala: a tool like a spade or peel used to skim surface dross or oxide</term> which is of a material that will not be burned away and thus contaminate the **calx** <term>calx: the powdery "ash" or oxide left after a metal is roasted in fire</term>. This skimming <note>original: "excoriatio"; literally "skinning" or "flaying"</note> should be continued until the entire body is converted into powder. If the metal was **Lead** <note>original: "Saturnus"</note>, it should then be placed in a greater fire until its **calx** changes to a very bright yellow color <note>This describes the formation of massicot or litharge, common lead oxides</note>. If, however, it be **Tin** <note>original: "Jupiter"</note>, it should be exposed and left until its **calx** changes into a complete whiteness.

->**BOOK II.**<-

In this, however, we make the craftsman cautious <note>original: "sollicitum," implying a need for careful attention</note>, because **Lead** <term>Saturnus: Lead, associated with the planet Saturn</term> easily returns from its ash <term>calx: the powdery oxide or "crumbled" state produced by heating a metal</term> back to its solid body. **Tin** <term>Jupiter: Tin, associated with the planet Jupiter</term>, however, does so with great difficulty. Therefore, lest he happen to err in the preparation of Lead, after its first pulverization—specifically under a moderate fire—one must ensure it does not return to a solid state before its ash is perfected. This is because it requires a tempered fire and a gradual, cautious increase in heat until it is firmly fixed in its ash; only then can a greater fire be applied to it for the perfection of that ash, without the risk of it easily reverting to a solid mass.

Similarly, one should not err regarding the difficult reduction <term>reductio: the chemical process of turning a calx or ore back into its metallic state</term> of Tin. It may happen that when the craftsman attempts to reduce its ash, it fails to return to metal; instead, he finds it remains in its previous state or even turned into glass, leading him to then believe its reduction is impossible. For we say that if a great fire is not applied during the reduction of Tin, it will not return to metal; yet if the fire is too great, it may not necessarily reduce but instead be driven toward vitrification <term>vitrificatio: the process of turning a substance into glass through heat</term>.

And this occurs because Tin, in the depths of its **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum: Mercury or "living silver," considered by alchemists to be a primary component of all metals</term> nature, contains a fugitive <note>original: "fugitivam"; volatile or easily evaporated</note> substance. If it remains too long in the fire, this substance will escape, leaving the body deprived of its moisture <note>original: "humiditate"; in alchemical theory, the internal fluid that allows a metal to be fusible and malleable</note>. Consequently, it is found to convert to a glassy fusion rather than the melting of a metallic body. For anything deprived of its own moisture offers no fusion except that which creates glass; therefore, it necessarily follows that...

## On Calcination with Salts.

### CHAPTER XIV.

The method for the **Calcination** <term>Calcination: the process of heating a metal to turn it into a fine powder or "ash," known as an oxide</term> of these two bodies <note>Lead and Tin</note>, which is achieved through the sharpness <term>acuitate: the piercing or corrosive quality of the salt</term> of **Salt**, is as follows: a quantity of salt should be thrown over their surface. After this, through very frequent infusions <note>likely referring to adding liquid or more salt in stages</note>, they should be mixed with much stirring using an iron rod over the fire, until through the mixing of the salt the metal is turned into ash. Afterward, by the same method of completion, their ash <term>calx</term> should be perfected according to its specific requirements. 

However, there is a difference in the calcination of these two bodies: **Lead** <term>Plumbum: associated with the planet Saturn</term> is converted into a powder during the first labors of calcination more easily than **Tin** <term>Jupiter: associated with the planet Jupiter</term>. Yet, the ash of Lead is not perfected more easily than that of Tin. The reason for this depends on the fact that **Lead** <term>Saturnus</term> possesses a more fixed humidity than Tin, as well as a greater degree of earthiness <term>terreitatem: the solid, non-volatile component of a substance</term> than Tin.

For **Copper** <term>Venus: associated with the planet Venus</term> and **Iron** <term>Mars: associated with the planet Mars</term>, the method is the same, though it differs from the first two metals. This is because of the difficulty of melting them. The method is that they should be beaten into thin sheets <note>original: "laminati"</note> and placed in a strong fire. The intention is not to melt them, because of the great quantity of their earthiness and their high sulfur content <term>sulphureitatis: the combustible or "oily" principle in alchemical theory</term>...

## *On Solutions.*
### CHAPTER XV.

Our expanded discourse on **Dissolution** suggests that the **Solution** of a dry thing is its reduction into water. This is because the perfection of every solution is brought about with "subtle waters"—those that are extremely sharp, acrid, and astringent, having no dregs. Such examples include <term>distilled vinegar: a common alchemical solvent</term>, the juice of unripe grapes, very tart pears, and similarly distilled <term>pomegranates: used for their acidic properties</term>, and other things like these. 

The reason for the discovery of this process was the refinement of those substances which possess neither <term>fusion: the ability to melt</term> nor <term>ingression: the ability of one substance to penetrate and transform another</term>. Without this process, much of the usefulness of "fixed spirits" <note>original: "spirituum fixorum"; substances that are normally volatile but have been treated to resist the heat of the fire</note>, as well as those things of a similar nature, would be lost. 

For it is necessary that everything which is dissolved takes on the nature of <term>Salt: the alchemical principle of stability and body</term> or <term>Alum: a mineral salt used for its astringent and binding qualities</term>, or things similar to them. Now, it is their nature to allow for fusion before they undergo <term>vitrification: the process of turning into glass under intense heat</term>; therefore, dissolved spirits will likewise provide a similar fusion. Since, therefore, they agree much in their nature with the [metallic] bodies and with each other...

...occurs. Once <term>fusion: the state of being melted or liquefied</term> is achieved, it is necessary that through it the substances penetrate the bodies, and by penetrating, transmute them. But because we see that everything truly <term>calcined: reduced to a fine powder through intense heat</term> can be dissolved through the repetition of calcination and solution, we prove by this that all calcined things approach the nature of **Salts** and **Alums**. And thus it is necessary that they accompany one another in their properties.

...ny. <note>original: "mitari," the conclusion of the word *concomitari* (to accompany) from the preceding page.</note> Therefore, the method of **dissolution** <note>the alchemical process of "solutio," or turning a solid substance into a liquid form</note> is twofold: namely, by means of **warm dung** and by means of **boiling water**. These two methods share a single intention and produce a single effect. 

The method using dung is as follows: the <term>calcined material: a substance reduced to a fine powder through high heat</term> is placed in a **glass flask**, and over it is poured a quantity of **distilled vinegar** (or a similar liquid) equal to double its volume. The opening of the flask must be sealed well so that it does not "breathe" <note>meaning to prevent the escape of vapors or evaporation</note>, and it should be left under warm dung for three days. Afterward, the dissolved portion is removed by means of a **filter** <note>original: "filtrum," usually referring to a felt or cloth cone used for straining</note>. The part that remains undissolved, however, must be calcined again; after this second calcination, it is to be dissolved again in the same manner, repeating the work until the entirety of the material is dissolved.

The method involving boiling water is faster. It is performed by arranging the calcined material with vinegar in the same way, with the opening sealed so the substance cannot "breathe." The flask is then buried in a **cauldron** full of water and straw—following the order of instructions we previously gave regarding the method of distillation by water <note>the straw acts as a cushion to prevent the glass flask from cracking against the metal cauldron during the boiling process</note>. A fire is then lit beneath it until the water boils for one hour. 

Afterward, the dissolved part should be filtered and kept separate; the undissolved part is to be calcined again and dissolved again following the same order, then filtered as described above. Alternatively, these various solutions may be mixed and kept together so that a single final distillation may be performed once everything has been dissolved through repeated calcinations. Indeed, a description of what has just been stated was provided previously.

# On the Coagulation of Mercury and of Dissolved Substances.

## CHAPTER XVI.

**Coagulation** <note>The alchemical process of turning a liquid or "spirit" into a solid, stable form; the opposite of the "solution" described on previous pages.</note> is therefore the reduction of a liquid thing to a solid substance through the removal of its moisture. There are two reasons for the discovery of this process: first, the hardening of **Quicksilver** <term>Mercury: often referred to by the Latin "Argentum vivum" (living silver), viewed as a primal metallic matter</term>; and second, the freeing of dissolved **medicines** <note>In this context, "medicines" (medicinarum) refer to alchemical elixirs or tinctures intended to transform metals.</note> from the watery quality that has been mixed into them. 

This process varies according to the variety of things to be coagulated. For one kind of coagulation is required for **Quicksilver**, while another is needed for a dissolved **medicine**. Regarding **Quicksilver** itself, there is a twofold method of coagulation: one is through the removal of all the innate moisture within it; the other is through the thickening of that moisture until it becomes hard. 

However, achieving this coagulation is extremely difficult and involves great labor—a task that men approach only with difficulty and through the depth of keen industry. We shall now relate the entire artifice of that coagulation. 

Regarding the method of coagulation, some have thought it could be achieved by keeping the substance in a **tempered fire** <note>a gentle, controlled heat</note>. But when they believed they had coagulated it, they found that upon removing it from the fire, it flowed just as it did before. Because of this, they were struck with stupor and intense wonder, arguing that such a goal could not be reached. Others, however, assuming by necessity from natural principles that any moisture is converted into dryness by the heat of fire, attempted to

Yet their coagulation was not useful, because they converted it into an **imperfect body** <note>a "base" metal such as lead or tin, rather than "perfect" gold or silver</note>, and they were likewise unable to see the reason for this. 

Therefore, it is necessary for us to explain these causes so that the artisan may find the **mastery** <note>original: "magisterium"; the secret knowledge or "Great Work" of alchemy</note> of that coagulation. As we have already sufficiently explained, the substance of **Quicksilver** is uniform; for this reason, it is not possible to remove its watery nature in a short space of time by keeping it in the fire. Therefore, excessive haste was the cause of the first error. Since it is a subtle substance, it flees from the fire. Thus, an excess of fire was the cause of error for those from whom it escaped. ...because of the commonality in its nature. For these substances, since they are **fugitive** <note>original: "fugitiva"; in alchemy, this refers to substances that are volatile and evaporate easily when heated</note>, they cannot hold the Quicksilver in the struggle of the fire until it reaches the nature of a solid body. Instead, they flee away together through the burning of the fire; and this is the cause of the error of those who attempt to coagulate it in this manner. 

Furthermore, **Quicksilver** likewise possesses a **humidity** <note>the "fluid" quality of the metal that allows it to flow</note> very closely united to itself, which cannot be separated from it except through the cautious violence of fire, or through its preservation in its own fire. And its "own fire" is that which, by increasing it according to the requirements of what the substance can tolerate, removes its humidity—but without leaving behind a part sufficient for metallic **fusion** <term>melting</term>. Once that humidity is removed, it will not melt, and this is the cause of the error of those who coagulate it into that **stone** which cannot be melted. 

Similarly, it has parts of **Sulfur** naturally mixed within it—some more, others less—which it is impossible to remove by human artifice. Therefore, since it is the property of **Sulfur**, when combined with **Quicksilver**, to create a red or yellow color according to the measure of its quantity, the property of its removal will be to grant whiteness to the **Quicksilver** through the fire. This, then, is the cause of the variety of its colors after its coagulation into a **stone**. It likewise has the **earthiness** <note>original: "terreitatem"; the impure, solid dross or "earthy" part of a substance</note> of **Sulfur** mixed in, by which it is necessary that all its coagulations be tainted. And this is the cause of the error of those who [turn] it into an im- <note>The text cuts off here and continues on the next page; the catchword indicates the word is "imperfect" body.</note>

...they achieve a perfect coagulation. Therefore, due to the variety of the **medicines** <term>alchemical reagents or agents used to trigger transformation</term> used for that coagulation, it happens that diverse bodies are created; and similarly, this depends on the diversity of the substance that is to be coagulated. For if the medicine, or the substance itself, contains a mixed **Sulfur** that is **non-fixed** <note>original: "non fixum"; in alchemy, "fixed" refers to a substance that is stable in fire and does not evaporate, while "non-fixed" or "volatile" substances flee the heat</note>, a soft body must necessarily be created from it. If, however, it is fixed, the body must be hard. If it is white, the body will be white; if red, red. If the color is a pale version of white or red, the resulting body must similarly be pale. If the substance is earthy, it will be tainted; if not, it will not be. Likewise, everything that is non-fixed creates a **livid** <note>original: "lividum"; a dull, bluish-gray or leaden color</note> body; but a fixed substance does not, so far as its nature allows. Therefore, a pure substance creates a pure body, while an impure substance, on the contrary, creates an impure one.

Furthermore, a similar diversity occurs in **Quicksilver** <term>Mercury</term> alone, even without the mixture of **Sulfur**, because of the differences in how it is cleansed and prepared with the medicines. And for this reason, because of the variety of the medicine, a transformation <note>original: "illusio"; while this can mean "illusion" or "deception," in this context it refers to the various outward forms or "masks" the substance assumes</note> occurs in its coagulation: sometimes it becomes **Lead**, sometimes **Jupiter** <term>Tin</term>, sometimes **Venus** <term>Copper</term>, and sometimes **Mars** <term>Iron</term>. This happens because of impurity. At other times, however, it becomes **Sol** <term>Gold</term> or **Luna** <term>Silver</term>, which must necessarily occur from its purity and the careful observation of its colors.

## *On the Coagulation of Mercury by Precipitation.*
### CHAPTER XVII.

It is coagulated, therefore, by its frequent precipitation with violence at the severity of the fire. For the severity <note>original: "asperitatem"; referring to the harshness or intensity of the heat</note> of the fire easily removes its watery nature. This is accomplished using a vessel whose shape is of great length; in this vessel, the substance finds a place for cooling, and a place to adhere and rest within the vessel's joints <note>original: "spondilibus"; likely referring to the segments or joints of a modular alchemical vessel like an aludel</note> along its length, rather than a path of escape. It remains there until, by a repeated turn, it is precipitated back to the bottom of the vessel by the great heat of the fire. This process is repeated many times until the substance becomes **fixed** <note>original: "fixum"; in alchemy, a substance is "fixed" when it no longer evaporates or "flees" when exposed to fire</note>.

It is similarly coagulated by being held for a long time in its own fire, using a glass vessel with a very long neck and a belly shaped like a flask. The neck must be kept continuously open so that its moisture <note>original: "humiditas"</note> can afterwards evaporate.

Truly, it is also coagulated by a **medicine** <term>an alchemical reagent or additive used to trigger a transformation</term> suitable for it, and we shall describe that more clearly in the following sections. Here, however, we shall likewise state our full intention regarding it, according to what we have found through our own experiment. That medicine is the one which adheres most deeply to the Mercury and is mixed through its smallest parts <note>original: "per minima"; an early concept of molecular or corpuscular mixing</note> before the Mercury can escape. It is therefore necessary to gather this medicine from things compatible with the Mercury. All such bodies are **Sulfur** or **Arsenic**, but because we do not see any body in its own nature that...

Truly, however, it is found more easily, more closely, and more perfectly from **Mercury** <note>original: "Argento vivo"</note> alone: since nature embraces its own nature more amicably, and rejoices in it more than in a foreign one:

### *On Fixation and its methods.*

### CHAPTER XVIII.

**Fixation** is the appropriate adaptation of a thing that "flees" from the fire <note>original: "rei fugientis ad ignem"; a poetic way to describe volatile substances that evaporate easily</note>. The reason for the purpose of fixation itself is so that every **tincture** <term>a substance capable of changing the color or nature of a metal</term> and every alteration may be made permanent during the transformation, and not be changed. 

This process is likewise diversified according to the variety of things to be fixed: namely, certain bodies that are diminished from perfection, such as **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>, and **Venus** <note>Copper</note>; and according to the diversity of the **Spirits** <term>volatile substances that vaporize in heat</term> themselves, which are **Sulfur** and **Arsenic** in one grade, and **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argentum vivum"; Mercury</note> in another. In the third grade are **Marcasite** <note>Iron pyrite or similar metallic-looking minerals</note>, **Magnesia** <note>not modern magnesium, but likely manganese ore or a similar black mineral used in glassmaking</note>, and **Tutty** <note>original: "Tutia"; zinc oxide crusts collected from the flues of smelting furnaces</note>. All the aforementioned things are dissolved, coagulated, and fixed, along with others like them. Therefore, these bodies that are diminished from their perfection are fixed through their cal- <note>The word is cut off here, likely continuing as "calcination"</note>

...calcination <note>original: "calcinationem"</note>: since they are freed by it from volatile and corrupting sulfurousness. And we have sufficiently shown this **calcination** <term>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder or change its chemical state</term> in its own discourse.

**Sulphur** and **Arsenic** <note>these were regarded by alchemists as the "volatile spirits" that needed to be "tamed" or fixed to transform metals</note> are indeed fixed in two ways: namely, by the repetition of **sublimation** <term>the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again in the upper part of a vessel</term> upon them in an **Aludel** <term>a pear-shaped alchemical pot, often stacked in series, used for condensing sublimated vapors</term> vessel, until they "stand" <note>remain stable and no longer evaporate</note>. And from this, the goal of hastening their **fixation** <term>the process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to evaporation when exposed to fire</term> is achieved, so that you may devise a way to discover multiple repetitions in a short time. This is done using two **Aludels** with their double lids, in this order: namely, that they never cease from the act of sublimation until they have been fixed.

Therefore, when the substance has ascended in one, let it be cast into the other **Aludel**, and so let it be done by turns. Also, never allow the substances to remain idle adhering to the **joints** <term>spondilia; the segments or neck-parts of the stacked vessels where material collects</term> of the Aludels, but let them be in a continuous rising by fire until their rising ceases. And by however much shorter time you can multiply the repetitions of sublimation, by so much more quickly will the time of the fixation itself be shortened.

And for that reason, a second method of fixation was invented, which is through the preparation of the substance to be **sublimed** <note>original: "sublimandi"</note> for heat, so that it may remain continuously in it until it is fixed. This is done by means of a long glass vessel whose bottom is earthenware and not glass (since glass would crack into pieces), and it is joined to it skillfully with a good seal. And by means of an iron or stone spatula, when the substance has adhered to its **joints**, let it be cast down to the bottom of the heat continuously by alternating turns, until [it is fixed] <note>original catchword: "figatur"</note>.

->The fifth vessel<- <note>original: "Vas quintum"</note>

This diagram illustrates a <term>matrass</term> <note>from the French "matras," a glass vessel with a round body and a very long neck. It was used by early chemists for "digestion," a process where a substance is kept in gentle, steady heat to encourage chemical changes.</note>. 

The specific design shown here—a perfectly spherical base with an exceptionally tall, narrow neck—was intended to prevent the escape of volatile spirits. As the contents were heated, the vapors would rise into the long neck, cool, and then trickle back down into the bulb. This internal "reflux" allowed the alchemist to subject the material to heat for weeks or months at a time without the vessel boiling dry.

->Glass Vessel.<- <note>original: "Vas Vitreum"</note>

->The fourth vessel<- <note>original: "Vas quartum"</note>

->Earthen vessel<- <note>original: "Vas terreum"</note>

->Spatula.<-

In early chemistry and alchemy, the material of a container was just as important as its shape. A **glass vessel** <note>original: "Vas Vitreum"</note> was prized for its transparency, allowing the practitioner to witness the "colors of the Great Work" as they shifted. However, glass was fragile and prone to breaking under intense heat. 

For the most violent fires, an **earthen vessel** <note>original: "Vas terreum"</note> was required. Made of fired clay or "lute," these ceramic containers could withstand the high temperatures of a furnace that would melt or shatter glass. The **fourth vessel** shown here appears to be a specialized modular unit—the tall cylinder with a lid—designed to sit atop a base or furnace. 

Finally, the **spatula** <note>from the Latin for a broad blade or "spatha"</note> represents the practical, hands-on nature of the work. It was used for scraping "fixed" salts from the bottom of vessels or mixing dry ingredients before they were subjected to the fire. 

<summary>This page illustrates the "fourth vessel" and categorizes laboratory containers by their material composition, highlighting the use of glass for observation and earthenware for high-heat operations.</summary>

be fixed. The method of <term>Fixation</term><note>In alchemy, fixation is the process of transforming a volatile substance—one that evaporates easily—into a "fixed" or stable state where it can endure fire without being lost as vapor.</note> for <term>Mercury</term><note>original: "Argenti vivi"</note> is indeed the same as the method for the fixation of Sulfur and Arsenic; they do not differ from one another except in this: Sulfur and Arsenic cannot be fixed unless their thinnest, flammable parts are first separated from them by the subtle craft of division. By this final method of fixation, Mercury does not require this consideration, for it does not possess flammable parts. For this reason, Sulfur and Arsenic similarly require a more moderate heat than Mercury. They also differ in this method because they are fixed over a longer period of time than Mercury, since they rise higher due to their rarity <note>In this context, "rarity" refers to the substance being less dense or more "airy."</note> compared to Mercury. Therefore, they require a longer vessel than Mercury does for its fixation.

The fixation of <term>Marcasite</term><note>A term used for various metallic ores, often iron pyrites.</note>, <term>Magnesia</term><note>In early chemistry, this could refer to several minerals, including manganese or a variety of white ores.</note>, and <term>Tutty</term><note>Zinc oxide, often found as a crust in smelting furnaces.</note> is such that when, after their first <term>sublimation</term><note>The process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then letting it cool back into a solid, used here to purify the materials.</note>, we have obtained from them what we desire, we discard their dross and repeat the sublimation upon them. We do this by turning what has ascended to the top back down to what remained at the bottom, repeating this for each of them until they are fixed. The description of these vessels has already been provided.

## On Ceration.

# BOOK TWO

...because it lacked the ability to penetrate <note>In alchemy, "penetration" refers to the way a medicinal or transmuting substance enters and alters a base metal.</note> into a body for alteration due to the lack of its own liquefaction, it should be softened so that it might flow and gain that penetration. Therefore, some have thought that <term>Ceration</term><note>From the Latin *cera* (wax); the process of softening a hard substance to a wax-like consistency so it can melt and mix more easily.</note> ought to be performed using liquid oils and waters. But that is an error, utterly removed from the natural principles of this <term>Great Work</term><note>original: "magisterii"; the mastery or art of alchemy.</note>, and refuted by the manifest works of nature. 

For we do not see that nature has placed an easily terminable <note>Meaning moisture that evaporates or disappears quickly.</note> moisture within metallic bodies themselves, but rather one that is long-lasting, for the necessity of their softening and melting. For if she had placed such moisture in them, it would necessarily follow that through a single <term>ignition</term><note>The process of heating a substance to a high temperature in a furnace.</note>, the bodies would happen to be totally deprived of their moisture. Consequently, it would follow that after one heating, any body could neither be hammered nor melted. 

Therefore, imitating the works of nature wherever we can, it is necessary for us to follow nature's method in cerating. Now, nature herself cerates at the very root of the softening of fusible things using a moisture which, above all other moistures, withstands the heat of the fire. Therefore, it is also necessary and beneficial for us to use a similar moisture in our ceration. In no other things is this softening moisture found more effectively or more feasibly than in these: namely, in **Sulfur** and **Arsenic** closely, but even more closely and better in **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argento vivo"</note>. We do not see the moisture of these substances leave their "earth" <note>Early chemists believed matter was composed of "earth" (the solid part) and "moisture" (the part that allows melting and vapor). A "strong union" meant the substance would not break apart easily when heated.</note> because of the strong union they achieved during nature's own process of mixing.

# BOOK [TWO]

<margin>G4</margin>

In all other things that contain moisture, you will find through experience that during their breakdown <term>resolution</term><note>original: "resolutione"; the process of decomposing a substance into its component parts, often through heat or dissolution.</note>, the moisture is separated from its earthy substance; and once that separation occurs, the substance is stripped of all its moisture. 

However, in the aforementioned <term>spirits</term><note>original: "spiritibus"; in alchemy, these are volatile substances—such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic—that can be vaporized by heat but are seen as having a "permanent" moisture that does not easily evaporate like water.</note>, this does not happen at all. Therefore, there is no other way we can be excused from using them for the process of <term>ceration</term><note>original: "cerationis"; the process of softening a hard, dry substance until it reaches a wax-like consistency so it can melt and mix with other materials.</note>. 

The method of ceration, then, is performed by using these spirits: their <term>sublimation</term><note>original: "sublimatio"; a process where a solid is heated until it turns to vapor and then collects again as a solid. Here, the vapors are meant to be "multiplied" or directed onto the substance being treated.</note> must be repeated over the substance to be softened many times, until the spirits remain within that substance along with their own moisture and allow it to achieve a good <term>fusion</term><note>original: "fusionem"; the state of being melted or liquefied.</note>. 

This, however, will not happen

->before their perfect cleansing<-
->from every corrupting thing.<-

<header>THE THIRD</header>

### OF THE THIRD BOOK
# OF THE SUMMA OF GEBER,
### Most Perspicacious Philosopher,
### Concerning
## THE CONSIDERATION OF THINGS
## FROM WHICH COMES THE PERFECTION
## OF THE MASTERY,

### *P R E F A C E.*

<image-desc>A large, square decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring intricate floral and foliage patterns in a Renaissance style.</image-desc>

Therefore, now that the discussion of the principles of this <term>magistery</term><note>original: "magisterii"; refers to the "Mastery" or the Great Work of alchemy—the complete process of transmuting base metals into gold.</note> has been handed down, it is necessary—as we promised—to pursue the full intention of our art in a discourse suitable to it. This involves, namely, the consideration of every thing by which the perfection of this work may be more aptly shown. It also involves consideration of the necessity of the perfection of the <term>medicine</term><note>original: "medicinæ"; in an alchemical context, the "medicine" is the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone, believed to "cure" imperfect metals of their "diseases" (impurities) to make them perfect gold.</note>; specifically, so that it may be discussed from what substance that medicine is most effectively and directly drawn out for the perfection of everything imperfect. Likewise, it involves the consideration of the <term>crafts</term><note>original: "artificiorum"; the practical methods, techniques, and apparatus used by the alchemist.</note> by which we may know whether the perfection is complete. Thus, once these things are handed down, the entire knowledge of perfection will have been delivered, according to the requirements of our art.

<summary>This page serves as the prologue to Book Three of Geber's "Summa," transitioning from theoretical principles to the practical study of materials and the "medicine" required for alchemical perfection. The author outlines his plan to discuss the sources of the Elixir and the methods for verifying the success of the work.</summary>

<header>BOOK 2.</header>

## *Of the Essence of Sulphur and Arsenic.*

### CHAPTER I.

It is not possible to understand the transmutations of bodies, or of <term>quicksilver</term><note>original: "Argenti vivi"; literally "living silver," the common alchemical term for mercury.</note> itself, unless the craftsman attains a true knowledge of their nature according to their fundamental roots. First, therefore, we shall explain the principles of bodies: namely, what they are according to their causes, and what beneficial or harmful qualities they contain within themselves. Afterward, we will demonstrate the natures of all these bodies along with all their properties—specifically, the causes of their corruption—supported by practical experiments.

Therefore, proving first the nature of the <term>spirits</term><note>In alchemy, "spirits" refers to volatile substances that can be vaporized or sublimated by heat, primarily mercury, sulfur, and arsenic.</note>, let us present those which are the principles of the bodies: namely quicksilver, sulphur, and its <term>companion</term><note>original: "suum compar"; this refers to arsenic, which was traditionally paired with sulphur in alchemical theory due to their similar volatile and combustible properties.</note>. We say, then, that sulphur and its companion are the "fatness of the earth," as we have already stated above in the chapter on sulphur. You may derive clear proof of this from experience, through its flammability and its easy liquefaction by heat. For nothing is set on fire unless it is oily (that is, "fat"), nor does anything liquefy easily by heat unless it possesses that same nature.

Therefore, sulphur and its companion contain the cause of corruption: namely, a flammable substance and <term>earthy dregs</term><note>original: "fæculentiam"; the impure, solid sediment or dross that remains in a substance.</note>. However, they possess a moderate "perfecting cause" that lies between the two. Therefore, the cause of corruptio—

### *On the Essence of Marcasite, Magnesia, and Tutty.*
#### CHAPTER III.

It is necessary for us to hand down the natural teaching concerning the other <term>spirits</term><note>original: "spiritibus"; in alchemy, "spirits" are substances that vaporize or "fly" when heated, such as mercury, sulfur, and arsenic.</note>: namely, <term>Marcasite</term><note>original: "Marchasita"; often referring to iron pyrites or other metallic-looking ores.</note>, <term>Magnesia</term><note>original: "Magnesia"; not the modern element, but likely a term for various dark minerals like manganese or magnetite used in glassmaking and alchemy.</note>, and <term>Tutty</term><note>original: "Tutia"; zinc oxide, usually found as a crust in the chimneys of smelting furnaces.</note>, which produce a great impression on metallic bodies. Therefore, we shall state in this chapter what they are, along with their proofs.

We say, then, that Marcasite possesses a dual substance in its creation: that of Quicksilver—specifically, "mortified" <note>original: "mortificati"; mercury that has been reacted or "killed" so it is no longer a liquid metal.</note> Quicksilver that is approaching <term>fixation</term><note>original: "fixionem"; the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat.</note>—and that of "burning" Sulphur. We have discovered through clear experience that it possesses a sulfurous nature. For when it is <term>sublimated</term><note>original: "sublimatur"; heated until it turns to vapor and then collected as a solid.</note>, a clearly burning sulfurous substance emanates from it; likewise, its sulfurous quality can be perceived even without sublimation. For if it is subjected to fire, it does not sustain the heat until it is ignited and burns by the inflammation of its own Sulphur.

That it contains the substance of Quicksilver is also made evident to the senses. For it grants the whiteness of pure Silver to <term>Venus</term><note>original: "Veneri"; the alchemical name for copper.</note>, just as Quicksilver itself does. Furthermore, during its sublimation, it exhibits a <term>heavenly</term><note>original: "caelestinum"; likely a bright or bluish-white luster.</note> color and a bright...
