<header>BOOK THREE</header>

## On the Essence of the Sun.
### CHAPTER IV.

Having now expanded our discourse regarding bodies <note>In alchemy, "bodies" refers to the seven metals: Gold, Silver, Copper, Iron, Tin, Lead, and Mercury—though Mercury is often categorized as a "spirit."</note>, we shall relate their internal essence and their procreation. We speak first of the **Sun** <term>Sol</term><note>original: "Sol"; the alchemical name for Gold.</note>, afterwards of the **Moon** <term>Luna</term><note>original: "Luna"; the alchemical name for Silver.</note>, and finally of the others in the order that seems most practical, providing the proofs for each which are obtained through experiment.

The Sun is therefore created from the most subtle substance of **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum</term><note>original: "Argentum vivum"; Mercury.</note> and the clearest fixity <note>original: "fixura"; the quality of being "fixed" or resistant to the transformative power of fire.</note>. It is also composed of a small substance of clean **Sulphur** <term>Sulphur</term><note>The alchemist refers here to "philosophical sulphur," a theoretical principle of combustion and color, rather than the raw yellow mineral.</note> of a pure redness—fixed, clear, and transformed from its original nature—which tints the Quicksilver. Because variations occur in the colors of this Sulphur, it follows that the **citrinity** <term>citrinitas</term><note>original: "citrinitas"; the yellow color stage in the alchemical process, believed to be the signature of gold.</note> of the Gold must likewise possess different degrees of intensity. Indeed, some Gold is more intense in its yellowing, and some is less. 

That Gold is made from the most subtle substance of Quicksilver is weighed and proven by the fact that Quicksilver receives Gold into itself with ease <note>This refers to the formation of gold amalgams; mercury easily dissolves gold.</note>. For Quicksilver does not receive anything other than what is of its own nature. 

Furthermore, that Gold possesses a clear and clean substance is manifested by its shining brilliance and its radiance; we say and have even seen that it manifests itself not only in the day, but even in the darkness <note>A common medieval belief was that "perfected" gold or the Philosopher's Stone could emit its own light in the dark.</note>. 

That it possesses a fixed substance, which burns without being consumed by "sulphureity" <note>original: "Sulphureitate"; refers to the impurities in common sulfur that cause metals to corrode or burn away in the furnace.</note>, is evident through every operation involving fire. For it is neither diminished by the fire, nor is it set aflame. 

Finally, that it contains a "tinting Sulphur" is evident from the fact that

...mixed with **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum</term> <note>original: "Argentum vivum"; literally "living silver" or Mercury.</note> it transforms it into a red color: and because it is **sublimated** <note>Sublimation is the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then collecting the deposit; here it refers to extracting the "spirit" of the metal.</note> by strong heating from the bodies—so that their subtle parts ascend with it—it creates a most intense yellow color <note>original: "citrinissimum colorem"; referring to citrinity, the alchemical stage of yellowing that signals the approach of the Great Work's completion.</note>. It is clear, therefore, that when its substance is pure, it generates a pure color; but when it is not pure, it generates an impure one. 

He who requires a proof for the demonstration of its yellowness lacks the sense to see what is perceived through sight; therefore, the most subtle substance of Quicksilver brought to **fixation** <term>fixio</term> <note>The state in which a substance becomes "fixed" or resistant to the transformative power of fire.</note>, and its purity, and the most subtle matter of **Sulphur** <term>Sulphur</term>—which is fixed and **non-burning** <note>original: "non adurens"; a theoretical "philosophical sulphur" that provides color and form without being consumed by fire like common brimstone.</note>—constitutes the entire essential matter of Gold itself. 

A greater quantity of Quicksilver than Sulphur is proven to be within it, because of the easy entry of Quicksilver into it. Therefore, whatever you wish to alter, alter it according to this example, so that you may bring those things to this same equality; and we have already provided the method for that. 

And because it possesses subtle and fixed parts, those parts were able to be greatly condensed. This was the cause of its great weight. Through a long and temperate **decoction** <term>decoctio</term> <note>A process of "cooking" or sustained heating intended to mature the substance.</note>, there resulted from it a gradual resolution, a good thickening, and an ultimate mixture, so that it melts when heated. From the preceding points, it is clear that a large quantity of Quicksilver is the cause of perfection, and a large quantity of Sulphur is the cause of corruption. Uniformity in substance is the cause of perfection, which occurs through mixing in natural decoction; and diversity in substance is [the cause of...]

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 119 regarding the elemental composition of metals. This page explores how variations in the quality and quantity of these two "parents" of metals lead to either the "perfection" of Gold or the "corruption" found in base metals.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>
<header>THIRD BOOK</header>

...cause of corruption; whereas hardening and thickening, which is achieved through long and temperate **decoction** <term>decoctio</term> <note>In alchemy, decoction is the process of "cooking" or heating a substance in its own moisture over a long period to mature it, similar to how fruit ripens.</note>, is the cause of perfection—indeed, the opposite of corruption. Therefore, if **Sulphur** <term>Sulphur</term> should fall upon that **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum</term> <note>original: "Argentum vivum"; literally "living silver," the common name for Mercury.</note> improperly, it is necessary that diverse corruptions be introduced according to the variation of that Sulphur. 

For the Sulphur which falls upon it may be entirely **fixed** <term>fixum</term> <note>A "fixed" substance is one that remains stable and does not vaporize when exposed to fire.</note> and non-combustible, or it may be entirely combustible and **volatile** <note>original: "fugiens"; literally "fleeing," referring to a substance that evaporates or sublimes easily when heated.</note> in the nature of common Sulphur. It might also hold a part that is volatile and a part that is fixed; it might possess the nature of Sulphur in one part and not in another. It may be entirely pure or impure, or it may be of small or great quantity—dominating the mixture or being dominated by it—or perhaps it is neither dominant nor dominated. Finally, it may be white, or red, or of an intermediate color. From these diversities, therefore, it was necessary for diverse bodies and similar things to be created in nature; all of these we must describe with clear proofs.

It is now clear from what has been said that if pure, fixed, red, and clear Sulphur falls upon a substance of Quicksilver that is pure, clean, fixed, and clear—and if the Sulphur is not dominant, but rather of small quantity and dominated—pure **Gold** <term>Aurum</term> is created from this. If, however, it should happen that pure Sulphur falls...

...stable, fixed, white, and clear, placed upon a clean substance of **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum</term> <note>original: "Argentum vivum"; literally "living silver," the standard term for Mercury.</note>, then pure **Silver** <term>Luna</term> <note>original: "Luna"; alchemists associated the Moon with Silver and the Sun with Gold.</note> is produced. Though it may not exceed [Gold] in quantity, it nevertheless possesses a purity diminished from the purity of Gold, and a density coarser than that of Gold. The evidence for this is that its parts are not condensed to such an extent that it equals the weight of Gold; nor does it possess a substance as fixed <note>A "fixed" substance is stable in fire and does not evaporate.</note> as that of Gold. The sign of this is its loss of mass when exposed to fire; and its **Sulphur**, which is neither fixed nor incombustible, is the cause of this. That the Sulphur within it is of such a nature is proven by its tendency to ignite. However, one should not consider it impossible that this "fixed" or "not-fixed" state is relative; for the sulfurous nature of Silver, when compared to the sulfurous nature of Gold, is non-fixed and burning; but when compared to other metallic bodies, it is fixed and non-burning.

## *On the Essence of Iron.* <note>original: "De Martis Essentia"; Mars is the alchemical name for Iron.</note>
### CHAPTER VI.

If, however, there should be a fixed, earthy Sulphur mixed with an earthy Quicksilver, and both of these are not of a pure whiteness but of a leaden, bluish color—and if the quantity of the fixed Sulphur is in the greatest surplus—**Iron** <term>Ferrum</term> is created from these. This is because the excess of fixed Sulphur prevents melting. Therefore, it follows that through the process of smelting, the Sulphur loses its ability to liquefy more quickly than the Quicksilver. For that which has more **Fixed Sulphur** <term>Sulphur fixum</term> melts more slowly; whereas that which has more of the **burning** [kind] <note>original: "adurente"; refers to volatile or combustible sulfur that has not been "fixed" by fire.</note> undergoes fusion more easily and quickly—which remains for us to prove clearly enough. 

That fixed Sulphur causes slower fusion is shown by this: it is never fixed unless it is **calcined** <term>calcinetur</term> <note>Calcination is the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile matter or reduce it to a powder (calx).</note>; and once calcined, it yields no fusion. For by rushing toward its fixation, which is accomplished through **precipitation** <term>præcipitationem</term>, it is fixed and changed into earth. But through its successive and repeated **sublimation** <term>sublimationem</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then collecting the condensed solid; here used repeatedly to "purify" the mercury into a stable form.</note>, it is likewise fixed and yet *not* turned into earth; indeed, it yields a metallic fusion. Therefore, let the **blessed and glorious most high God** be praised, who created it and gave it its substance and its properties of substance, which no other thing in nature happens to possess; so that in it, this perfection might be found through some artifice, which we find in it as a **proximate potential** <term>potentiâ propinquâ</term> <note>A term from Aristotelian philosophy meaning a substance is "ripe" or "ready" to transform into its final, perfect form—in this case, Gold.</note>. # On the Essence of Copper <term>Venus</term> <note>original: "De Veneris Essentia." In alchemical tradition, Venus is the planetary name for the metal copper.</note>

## CHAPTER VII.

Returning, therefore, to our purpose, we say that if there should be **Sulfur** <term>Sulphur</term> <note>Along with Quicksilver, Sulfur was believed to be one of the two "parents" of all metals.</note> that is impure, fixed, and coarse for its greater part—but for its smaller part, unfixed, leaden-hued, and red—and if as a whole it neither overcomes nor is overcome <note>Meaning the proportions are balanced so neither element completely dominates the other.</note>, and it should fall upon coarse **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum</term>: it is necessary that **Copper** <term>Æs</term> <note>The Latin 'Æs' can refer to copper, bronze, or brass, but here specifically describes the metal formed by this particular alchemical combination.</note> be created from it.

Therefore, it is necessary to bring forward proof of all these things through the data of its own nature. For when it is placed in the fire for ignition, you will be able to discern a **sulfurous flame** from it; this is a sign of sulfur that is not "fixed" <note>In alchemy, "fixed" substances resist the fire, while "unfixed" or volatile substances evaporate or burn away.</note>, as well as the loss of its volume through evaporation <note>original: "exhalationem"</note>. The **fixed sulfur** within it, however, is revealed through its frequent burning. For from this comes the slowing of its melting <note>original: "fusionis"</note> and the hardening of its substance, which are signs of an abundance of its fixed sulfur. 

That it is red, impure, and joined to impure quicksilver is evident to the senses; therefore, it requires no other proof. You may thus draw out the secret through experiment: for you see that every thing changed into earth by the action of heat is easily dissolved and reduced to the nature of water. This happens because the parts are refined <term>subtiliatio</term> <note>The process of making a substance "subtle"—breaking it down into its finest, most potent components.</note> by the fire. Therefore, a thing that is more refined in its own nature, toward...

...having been reduced to this earthy nature, it is further refined; therefore it is more easily dissolved, and thus those things which are most refined are most easily dissolved. From these things, therefore, the cause of the **corruption** and **staining** <note>original: "infectionis." In alchemy, this refers to the way a metal's surface changes or degrades when exposed to external forces.</note> of these two bodies—namely **Iron** <term>Mars</term> and **Copper** <term>Venus</term>—is evident. For through that vapor, the **alum-like quality** <term>aluminositas</term> <note>This refers to the formation of metal salts or "vitriols" on the surface, which the ancients associated with the properties of alum.</note> of their sulfur "blooms" upon the surface, created by that refining heat.

And if you place these two bodies into a **sharp liquor** <term>liquor ponticus</term> <note>A "pontic" or sharp liquid usually refers to a potent acidic solution, such as a mineral acid or highly concentrated vinegar.</note>, many of their parts will be easily dissolved by boiling them in it.

And if you look into the **ores** <note>original: "mineris"</note> of these two, you will find a dissolved substance of this alum-like quality clearly dripping from them and clinging to them; this quality, through its sharpness and easy dissolution, is changed into water. For nothing else is found to be sharp and easily soluble except Alum and that which shares its nature.

**Blackness** <term>Nigredo</term>, however, is created in each of these two bodies when they are placed in the fire, caused by the **non-fixed sulfur** <note>Volatile sulfur that reacts and chars rather than remaining stable.</note> that is contained within them in large amounts, as is the case in Copper. In Iron, however, there is very little of it, as its nature approaches that of fixed sulfur; therefore, it is not easy to remove such an impression <note>Referring to the blackening or oxidation caused by the fire.</note> from Iron.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the composition of metals, specifically the roles of Sulfur and Quicksilver (Mercury) in determining a metal's "perfection" and its behavior when heated.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>

->[THIRD] BOOK.<-

<margin>H 5</margin>

It was clear that from non-fixed **Sulfur** <term>Sulphur</term> <note>original: "Sulphurè non fixo." In alchemy, "non-fixed" or "volatile" sulfur is the part that evaporates or burns away easily when heated.</note>, **melting** <term>fusionem</term> occurs and is even aided; however, from fixed sulfur, melting does not occur and is instead hindered. He knows this to be necessarily true who, by no clever methods of melting, could preserve **Sulfur** in a molten state after its **fixation** <term>fixionem</term> <note>Fixation is the process of making a volatile substance resistant to the heat of fire so that it no longer evaporates.</note>. 

But he fixed **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum</term> <note>Mercury.</note> through the frequent repetition of **sublimation** <term>sublimationis</term> <note>A process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then cooled back into a solid to purify it.</note>, whereby it accepts a good melting. From this, therefore, it is manifest that those bodies containing more **Quicksilver** are of greater **perfection** <note>In alchemy, "perfection" refers to how close a metal is to becoming gold or silver.</note>, and those containing less are of lesser perfection. 

Study, therefore, in all your works, to let **Quicksilver** prevail in the **mixture** <term>commixtione</term>; and if you can achieve perfection through **Quicksilver** alone, you will be a seeker of the most precious perfection—a perfection that surpasses the work of nature. For you will be able to cleanse it inwardly in a way that nature has not reached. 

The proof of this—that those things containing a greater quantity of **Quicksilver** are of greater perfection—is indeed the very easy acceptance of **Quicksilver**. For we see that a more "perfect" body receives **Quicksilver** amicably <note>This likely refers to amalgamation, where mercury easily blends with gold and silver but less so with base metals like iron.</note>. From the preceding discourses, it follows that there is a double **sulfurousness** <term>sulphureitatem</term> in bodies: one indeed in the depth of the **Quicksilver**, enclosed at the very beginning of its **mixture**, but the other coming upon it from the outside. One of these is removed with great labor, while the other can be removed by no cleverness of the arts, except for that which is perfected through fire, to...

<summary>The author argues that Mercury (Quicksilver) is the key to metallic perfection and that higher-quality metals contain more Mercury than Sulfur. He identifies two types of sulfur within metals—one internal and one external—which affect the metal's purity and its ability to melt.</summary>

<page-num>128</page-num>

->THIRD<-
<note>original: "TERTIUS" - referring to the Third Book of the treatise.</note>

...so that our work may reach its end appropriately and usefully, since it has already been made united with that same creation. This is proven by experiment, for we see that **combustible sulfur-content** <note>original: "sulphureitatem adustibilem." This refers to the impure, oily part of sulfur that causes metals to blacken or burn in fire.</note> is destroyed by fire, but **fixed sulfur-content** is not destroyed at all. 

If, therefore, we say that bodies are cleansed by **calcination** <term>calcinatione</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state, often turning it into a fine powder.</note>, you should understand this to mean they are cleansed of the **earthy substance** which is not united at the root of its nature. It is not possible to cleanse that which is already united through the ingenuity of fire alone, unless the **medicine of Quicksilver** <term>Argenti vivi medicina</term> <note>Mercury, often called the "medicine" or "solvent" of the philosophers, believed to be the key to purifying and transforming metals.</note> arrives, hiding and tempering it, or separating it from the mixture. 

Moreover, the separation of the earthy substance that is united in the root of nature to the metal occurs from the mixture in one of two ways. It happens either through **elevation** <note>Also known as sublimation; causing a substance to rise as a vapor through heat.</note> with things that elevate the substance of **Quicksilver** and leave behind the earthy **Sulfur** because of their affinity with them—such as **Tutty** <term>Tutia</term> <note>A historical term for zinc oxide, often found as a crust in the flues of zinc-smelting furnaces.</note> and **Marcasite** <term>Marchasita</term> <note>A common iron sulfide mineral; in this context, it refers to a metallic stone used to help process other metals.</note>, because they are "smokes" <note>In alchemy, "smokes" or "fumes" refer to volatile substances that evaporate easily when heated.</note>. The greater part of these consists of a quantity of **Quicksilver** rather than **Sulfur**. You can see the proof of this: if you join these with bodies through a strong and sudden **melting** <term>fusione</term>, the spirits will draw the bodies with them in their flight-like ascent. Therefore, you will be able to elevate with them, or through the mixture of **Quicksilver** which we have described. For **Quicksilver** holds onto that which is of its own nature, but rejects the other.

# On the Essence of Jupiter.
<note>In alchemy, "Jupiter" is the standard name for the metal Tin.</note>
## CHAPTER VIII.

This investigation having been proposed, we follow our intention and suggest that if **Sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphur"</note> is present in the root of the mixture with little **fixation** <term>fixione</term> <note>The process by which a volatile substance is made stable under heat so that it does not evaporate.</note>, sharing in a whiteness that is not pure—not dominating the mixture but instead dominated by **Quicksilver** <term>Argento vivo</term>—and combined with a partially fixed white substance that is not purely mixed, then **Tin** <term>Stannum</term> will result from it. You will find the proof of these matters through preparation: for if you **calcine** <term>calcinaveris</term> <note>The process of heating a metal until it oxidizes and turns into a fine powder or "ash."</note> Tin, you will perceive from it the stench of Sulfur. This is a sign of Sulfur that is not fixed. Yet, because it does not produce a flame, you should not assume it is fixed; it fails to flame not because of its stability, but because the abundance of Quicksilver in the mixture protects it from burning.

Thus, a double **sulfur-nature** <note>original: "sulphureitas"</note> and a double substance of Quicksilver are proven to exist in Tin. One sulfur-nature is less fixed, because when it is calcined it stinks like Sulfur. Through the first, the experience of the mixture is proven; the other is proven by its endurance in its own **calx** <note>The powdery residue left after a metal has been calcined.</note> when exposed to fire, for being more fixed, it does not stink.

Indeed, a double substance of Quicksilver is proven to be within it. One of these is fixed. [Regarding the other]: before its calcination, the metal **creaks** <note>original: "stridet." This refers to the "cry of tin," a distinct crackling sound made when bending a bar of pure tin, caused by the internal crystal structure shifting.</note>; however, after it has been calcined three times, it no longer creaks. This is because the **fugitive** <note>Volatile; easily evaporated or lost to heat.</note> substance of the Quicksilver, which causes that creaking, has flown away. As for that Quicksilver...

that the substance of **Quicksilver** <note>original: "vivi" referring to "Argenti vivi"</note> is present in it is proven by the washing of **Lead** <note>original: "Plumbi"</note> with **Quicksilver** <term>Argento vivo</term>: for if Lead is washed with Quicksilver, and after this washing you melt it—provided the heat does not exceed the temperature of its own melting—a part of the Quicksilver will remain with it. This part brings to the Lead that characteristic **creaking sound** <note>original: "stridorem." This refers to the "tin cry," the distinct crackling sound made when bending a bar of tin.</note> and converts it into **Tin** <term>Stannum</term>. Conversely, you can observe the changing of Tin back into Lead. For through multiple repetitions of **calcination** <term>calcinationis</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to oxidize it.</note> and through the application of a fire suitable for **reduction** <term>reductionis</term> <note>The chemical process of returning a metallic oxide (calx) to its metallic form.</note>, it is converted into Lead. This happens especially when it is calcined with a great fire and its **dross** <note>original: "scorii"; the stony waste matter or slag separated from metals during smelting.</note> is removed. You can be certain of the differences between these substances through the ingenious methods of preserving them with proper instruments, and by the manner of the fire that divides them. We have arrived at this knowledge through persistence and have seen with our own certainty that we have judged the truth by this means. 

It is therefore necessary for us to make you certain of what remains after the removal of the two "unfixed" elements—namely, the **Sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphuris"</note> and **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argenti vivi"</note> of **Jupiter** <note>original: "Jove"; the alchemical name for Tin.</note>—so that you may completely understand the composition of Jupiter itself. That which remains is bluish-gray and heavy, like Lead, yet it possesses a greater whiteness than Lead, even the purest Lead. In it, there is an equality of **density** <term>spissitudinis</term> between the two components—namely Quicksilver and Sulfur—but not an equality of quantity; for in the mixture, the Quicksilver dominates. The sign of this is the ease of **melting** <note>original: "facilitas in- [gresio]". The catchword "gresio" likely completes "ingressio," referring to the metal's low melting point or "entry" into a liquid state.</note>

# BOOK I.

...of the entry <note>original: "gressionis," likely "Ingressionis"</note> into it of **Quicksilver** <term>Argenti vivi</term> in its own nature. Therefore, if there were not a greater quantity of Quicksilver in it, it would not easily adhere to it in its nature. And for this reason, it does not adhere to **Iron** <note>original: "Marti" (Mars), the alchemical name for Iron.</note> except by the most subtle skill; nor to **Copper** <note>original: "Veneri" (Venus), the alchemical name for Copper.</note> because of the small amount of Quicksilver in them in their mixture. From this, therefore, it is clear that it adheres to Iron with the greatest difficulty, but to Copper more easily, because of the larger amount of Quicksilver in it; the sign of this is its easy melting, while the melting of Iron is very difficult. 

The **fixation** <term>Fixio</term> <note>The process of making a volatile substance stable in fire so that it does not evaporate.</note> of these two substances approaches a firm fixation, yet it is not permanently firm. The proof of this is the **calcination** <term>calcinatio</term> <note>Heating a metal until it turns into a powder or oxide.</note> of the body itself: for after calcination and exposure to a strong fire, no separation will occur through it; instead, the entire substance will rise <note>evaporate or sublime</note>, though more purified. We see, therefore, that the substance of **Sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphuris"</note> adhering in **Tin** <note>original: "Stanno"</note> is more easily separated than in **Lead** <note>original: "Plumbo"</note>; and you see the experiment of this through the easy hardening of **Tin** <note>original: "Jovis" (Jupiter), the alchemical name for Tin.</note>, its calcination, and the improvement of its brightness. 

And thus we consider that these corrupting elements were not in its "root" <note>original: "radice," meaning its fundamental essence or origin.</note>, but came to it later. And because they were not closely joined to it in the first mixture, they can therefore be easily separated. Therefore, the alterations in it are a work of speed—namely its purification, its hardening, and its fixation—and you can clearly consider the causes of these through what has already been handed down by us. And because after these operations—namely calcination and reduction—we have consider- <note>original: "considera-"; completed by the catchword as "consideravimus" (we have considered).</note>

## On the Essence of Saturn.
### CHAPTER IX.

It remains for us, therefore, to set down a description of **Saturn** <note>The alchemical name for **Lead**.</note>, and we say that its reduction to its nature is not differentiated from **Jupiter** <note>The alchemical name for **Tin**.</note> through the repetition of **calcination** <term>calcinatio</term> <note>The process of heating a metal to high temperatures to remove volatile impurities or reduce it to a powder.</note>, except because it has a more impure substance, mixed from two coarser substances—namely **Sulfur** and **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum</term>—and because the burning Sulfur in it is more adhesive to its own substance than to the Quicksilver substance, and that it has more of the substance of fixed Sulfur in its composition than Jupiter does. We will bring forth proofs of these things through manifest experiments. That it is of a greater earthy and foul <note>original: "fætulentæ," referring to dross or sediment.</note> part than Jupiter is made manifest by sight and by washing

A similar sign of this is that something of it remained in the bottom of the instrument, which necessarily leaves us with one of these three signs: either there is no quantity of **combustible Sulfur** in it, or very little, or a great deal joined in the proximity of the principles at the root of the **mixture** <term>mixtio</term>. <header>BOOK THREE</header>

...the nature of **fixed Sulfur** <term>Sulphur fixum</term> <note>In alchemy, "fixed" substances are those that resist the heat of fire and do not evaporate, representing stability.</note> approaches, being uniformly mixed within the substance of **Mercury** <term>Argentum vivum</term> <note>original: "Argenti vivi," literally "living silver." Along with sulfur, it was considered one of the two primary components of all metals.</note>. And therefore, when its smoke rises, it necessarily rises with non-combustible Sulfur, whose property is to create **citrinity** <term>citrinitas</term> <note>A yellowing or golden color, often considered a stage of transition in the alchemical Great Work.</note>. Truly, that a greater quantity of non-burning Sulfur is in it [Lead] than in **Tin** <term>Jupiter</term> has been demonstrated by us with truth; through this, we see its whole color change into yellow. In contrast, the color of **Tin** changes into white during its **calcinations** <term>calcinationibus</term>. 

And therefore, a way is opened for us to investigate the cause of this work, by which during calcination **Tin** is more easily changed into a hard state than **Lead** <term>Saturn</term>. Yet it does not reach a slowness of melting more quickly than Lead; and that is because the cause of true hardness is **fixed Sulfur** and **fixed Mercury**. 

Truly, the cause of melting is twofold: namely, **Mercury** and **combustible Sulfur**; either of these is sufficient for the perfection of fusion at every stage, specifically through the ignition of **Mercury**. 

Therefore, because in **Tin** there is a great quantity of **Mercury** that is not truly fixed, a great speed of melting remains in it, and it is not easily removed from it. Truly, the cause of softening is twofold: namely, **Mercury** and **combustible Sulfur**. Since, therefore, the combustible sulfurousness is removed from **Tin** more easily than from **Lead**, once one of the causes of softness is removed, it must necessarily harden quickly. <header>BOOK</header>

...because it strongly possesses the causes of both types of softness joined together, it is not easily hardened. There is, however, a difference between the softness caused by **Mercury** <term>Argentum vivum</term> <note>original: "Argentum vivum," literally "living silver." In alchemical theory, Mercury provided metals with their fluidity and luster.</note> and that caused by **Sulfur** <term>Sulphur</term>. Softness resulting from **Sulfur** is "yielding" <note>original: "cessiva," meaning it gives way or compresses under pressure.</note>, while softness from **Mercury** is "extensible" <note>original: "extensiva," meaning it can be stretched or hammered thin without breaking.</note>. 

This is necessarily proven by the fact that we see bodies containing much **Mercury** possess great extensibility, while bodies with little possess little. For this reason, **Tin** <term>Jupiter</term> is more easily and finely extended than **Lead** <term>Saturn</term>; **Lead** more easily than **Copper** <term>Venus</term>; **Copper** more easily than **Iron** <term>Mars</term>; **Silver** <term>Luna</term> more finely than **Tin**; and **Gold** <term>Sol</term> more finely than **Silver**. It is clear, therefore, that since the cause of **hardening** <term>induratio</term> is **fixed Mercury** or **fixed Sulfur**, the cause of **softness** <term>mollities</term> will be the opposite.

The cause of **melting** <term>fusio</term> is twofold: namely, **volatile Sulfur** <term>Sulphur non fixum</term> and **Mercury** of any kind. However, **volatile Sulfur** is necessarily the cause of melting without **incandescence** <term>ignitio</term> <note>original: "ignitio," referring to the state where a metal glows red-hot before melting. Some substances act as "fluxes" to lower this temperature.</note>. You can clearly see an experimental proof of this by projecting **Arsenic** <term>Arsenicum</term> onto bodies that are difficult to melt; for it makes them melt easily and without incandescence. Similarly, **Mercury** is a cause of easy melting. In contrast, the cause of melting that requires incandescence is **fixed Mercury**. 

Therefore, the cause of any impediment to melting is **fixed Sulfur**. From this, you can draw out a very great secret: since the bodies of the highest perfection <note>In alchemy, "perfect bodies" usually refer to Gold and Silver, which do not corrode in fire.</note> are found to contain a very large quantity of **Mercury**, and therefore those bodies [moving away] from perfection...

-> ### THIRD <-

...with a **diminished** <note>original: "diminuta," completing the phrase from the previous page: "melting with a diminished flame."</note> flame: it is necessary to approach closer to perfection. Thus, it happens that bodies with high **sulfurousness** <term>sulphureitas</term> <note>In alchemical theory, sulfur was often seen as the source of impurity or "corruption" that made metals combustible or prone to decay.</note> are also bodies of great corruption. For this reason, it is clear from what has been said that **Tin** <term>Jupiter</term> approaches most closely to the perfect state, since it participates more in perfection; **Lead** <term>Saturn</term> indeed less; and still less **Copper** <term>Venus</term>; and least of all **Iron** <term>Mars</term>, which depends on the perfecting agent.

On the other hand, the situation is different when considering the **medicine** <term>medicina</term> <note>An alchemical "medicine" or elixir is a substance intended to "cure" the defects of base metals to turn them into gold or silver.</note> that completes and supplies what is lacking—thinning the imperfect and masking the thickness and darkness deep within the bodies under a substance of shining brilliance. From this perspective, **Copper** <term>Venus</term> is the most **perfectible**; **Iron** <term>Mars</term> is less so; **Tin** <term>Jupiter</term> even less; and **Lead** <term>Saturn</term> least of all.

Through truthful and laborious investigation of these things, it has been found that because of the diversity of these bodies, it is necessary to find diverse medicines through preparation. For a hard, combustible body requires one medicine, while a soft and non-combustible body requires another. One medicine acts by softening and thinning to the depths, maintaining the equality of its substance; the other acts by hardening and thickening its hidden parts.

It is now beneficial for us to move on to the medicines themselves, as clear experiences of these different substances provide us with the reasons for their discovery—both what they leave diminished and what they bring to completion.

# BOOK 137
I 2
<meta>catchword: dura</meta>

# On the twofold medicine for any
# imperfect body and for Mercury—namely,
# the white and the red.

## CHAPTER X.

We prove, therefore, that **spirits** <note>original: "spiritus." In alchemy, "spirits" refers to volatile substances like Mercury, Sulfur, or Arsenic that can penetrate and "animate" solid metals.</note> are more closely assimilated to bodies by the fact that they unite with bodies more closely and more amicably than other things in nature; for nature attracts nature, and like applauds its like. Through this observation, it has been brought to our attention, as our first discovery, that these spirits are the true **medicine** for the alteration of bodies.

Therefore, we have applied ourselves with every kind of ingenuity we could muster, so that through these spirits we might transform each of the imperfect bodies, by a stable and firm change, into a perfect **Lunar** <term>Silver</term> or **Solar** <term>Gold</term> body. For this reason, it was necessary to create diverse medicines from these same spirits, according to the different intentions of the materials to be altered.

Since there are two types of things to be altered—namely, **Mercury** <term>Argentum vivum</term>, which can be perfectly coagulated <note>To "coagulate" Mercury is to turn the liquid metal into a solid, stable form resembling silver or gold.</note>, and the bodies that fall short of perfection, which are indeed multiple—different approaches are required. Some bodies are **hard** and base <note>original: "ignibilia," implying they are "non-noble" or stubborn when subjected to fire.</note>, while others are **soft**, though still base. The hard and base bodies are **Iron** <term>Mars</term> and **Copper** <term>Venus</term>. The soft ones are **Tin** <term>Jupiter</term> and **Lead** <term>Saturn</term>. Thus, it is necessary that the perfecting medicine be similarly multiple. For Mercury requires one perfecting medicine, while the bodies to be transformed require another. And even others require...

...hard and base metals, such as **Venus** <term>Copper</term> and **Mars** <term>Iron</term>; while others are soft and not [entirely] base, such as **Saturn** <term>Lead</term> and **Jupiter** <term>Tin</term>. Since both of these types differ from one another in their nature, it necessarily follows that they likewise require different **medicines** <note>In alchemy, a "medicine" is a substance—often a form of the Philosopher's Stone—capable of "healing" a base metal's imperfections to turn it into gold or silver.</note>. 

Indeed, **Mars** and **Venus** belong to one class—that of hardness—but they differ in a specific property. For this one (**Venus**) is fusible <note>original: "fusibilis." This refers to a metal's ability to be melted. Copper melts at a lower temperature than the iron available to medieval metallurgists.</note>, whereas that one (**Mars**) is not fusible. Therefore, **Mars** is perfected by one medicine, and **Venus** by another; for the former is entirely unclean, while the latter is not. Furthermore, the former partakes in a certain dusky whiteness, while the latter partakes in redness and greenness <note>The "greenness" refers to the characteristic green oxidation (verdigris) of copper.</note>, which characteristics likewise impose a necessity for diversity in the medicine.

There are also bodies of the other class—that of softness—namely, **Jupiter** and **Saturn**. Since these differ from each other in a similar way, it is necessary that they also require different medicines. For **Jupiter** is clean, whereas **Saturn** is not. Yet all four of these mutable <note>original: "mutabilia." These are the "changeable" metals that can be transformed, unlike the "fixed" Gold and Silver.</note> bodies are made into bodies of perfection—sometimes **Lunar** <term>Silver</term> and sometimes **Solar** <term>Gold</term>.

Therefore, it is necessary for each metal to have a twofold medicine: one that is yellow <note>original: "citrinam." The yellow or "citrine" stage of the alchemical work (xanthosis) was the final step before achieving Gold.</note> and transforms the body into a **Solar** body, and another that is white, transforming it into a white **Lunar** body. Since a twofold medicine—namely **Solar** and **Lunar**—is applied to each of the four imperfect bodies, there will be eight medicines in total that perfect these bodies into the form of the noble metals. **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum</term> is likewise perfected into a **Solar**

...and Lunar, and therefore concerning the medicine that alters it, it happens that there is a **twofold difference**. There will therefore be ten medicines in total which we have discovered, in their completeness, for the complete alteration of any imperfect body. To be sure, this discovery comes only with a long duration of time, the persistence of labor, and the industry of great investigation; but we wish to be excused from the labor of discovering these ten individual medicines through the benefit of a **single medicine**. 

For we have found—through a long and especially laborious inquiry, and with certain experience—one medicine by which indeed the hard body is softened, and the soft body is hardened, and the "fleeing" <note>original: "fugitivum." In alchemy, this refers to volatile substances like Mercury that evaporate easily when heated.</note> is "fixed" <note>original: "figitur." To "fix" a substance is to make it stable and resistant to heat, preventing evaporation.</note>, and that which is foul is illuminated with an unspeakable splendor, in a state that exists beyond nature. 

Therefore, it is useful to bring forth an individual discourse on all these medicines, along with their causes and the manifest experiences of their proofs. First, indeed, we shall discuss the series of the ten medicines; then, the principles of all bodies; then, concerning **Quicksilver** <term>Mercury</term>; and finally, we must pass to the medicine of the perfection of the **Magistery** <note>The "Magistery" refers to the master-work or the highest achievement of the alchemical process—the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone.</note>. 

However, since these substances require an initial preparation, let us first set forth a narrative concerning the preparations of the imperfect bodies, lest we be bitten by the envious <note>A common trope in alchemical writing where the author defends themselves against critics or those who might misuse the knowledge.</note> due to any perceived insufficiency in our teaching of the art. # BOOK THREE

From here, however, a sufficient and fitting account of all the medicines must be added.

## *Concerning the administration and cleansing of bodies, diversifying them according to the diversity of their essences.*

### CHAPTER 11.

From the discourses already related by us, it appears what nature leaves behind—whether superfluous or diminished—in its works within each of those bodies which are imperfect, according to a sufficient part. But regarding their completion, those things which we omitted in the previous sections, we shall here complete entirely with a sufficient discourse. 

First, therefore, since it happens that there are two types of imperfect bodies: the changeable ones—namely the soft ones—and those that are not resistant to fire <note>original: "non ignibilia." In this context, it refers to metals that melt easily or are altered by fire rather than standing firm against it.</note>, such as **Saturn** <term>Lead</term> and **Jupiter** <term>Tin</term>; and the hard ones that are either not fusible, or fusible only with intense heat <note>original: "cum ignitione." Literally "with ignition," referring to the high temperatures needed to make metals like iron flow.</note>, such as **Mars** <term>Iron</term> and **Venus** <term>Copper</term>. The latter [Copper] is indeed not [easily] fusible, while the former [Iron] melts only with intense heat. Nature, by informing us, has necessarily taught that because of the diversity of their essences at the very root of nature, different preparations must be administered according to their specific requirements.

## *On the Preparation of Lead in particular.*
### CHAPTER XIII.

**Lead** and **Tin** <note>original: "Saturnus & Jupiter"</note> are therefore prepared through manifold preparations, according to how closely they must approach a greater perfection—namely, by both a common and a special mode of preparation. The common mode proceeds through multiple degrees of approaching perfection. For there is one degree of proximity, namely **Sulfur** <term>in alchemy, this refers to the "soul" or active principle of a metal, not just common brimstone</term> and a clean substance; another is hardness, along with the fiery heat <term>ignitione</term> of its melting. <page-num>145</page-num>
<header>BOOK TWO <note>original: "2 LIBER."</note></header>

...of a fugitive substance. <note>The sentence completes a thought from the previous page regarding the volatile nature of these metals.</note> They are therefore purified and made brilliant in three ways: either by cleansing substances, or by the method of calcination <term>heating a metal until it becomes a powder or "lime"</term> and reduction <term>the process of returning that powder back into a solid metal</term>, or by solution.

By cleansing substances, they are purified in two ways: either reduced to a lime <term>calx</term>, or in the natural state of their bodies <note>as solid metal rather than powder</note>. When reduced to lime, they are purified in this manner: either by Salts and Alums, or by glass. It is done so that when the body—specifically **Tin** <term>Jupiter</term> and **Lead** <term>Saturn</term>—has been calcined, then the water of Alums or Salts is poured over their lime, or crushed glass is mixed into it, and it is reduced back into a solid body. This process should be repeated, alternating these steps, until they show themselves to be completely clean. For since Salts, Alums, and Glass melt by a different kind of fusion than the metals do, they are separated from them and carry the earthy substance <note>the "foul" impurities</note> away with them, leaving only the purified metal behind.

In the natural state of the bodies, they are purified in a similar manner, which is by filing these two metallic bodies into very fine shavings. After this, they are treated with those same alums, salts, and glass, and afterwards reduced back into a solid body. This should be repeated in alternating turns until they appear cleaner.

They are also purified by a bath of **Quicksilver** <term>mercury; original: "Argentum vivum"</term>, the method of which we have already described. Similarly, both types of these bodies are purified by repeated turns of calcination and reduction with a sufficient amount of fire, until they appear cleaner.

For through this [method], these **lesser bodies** <note>original: *minuta corpora*; referring to base metals such as lead and tin which are "lesser" compared to gold and silver</note> are purified from that which hinders their perfection by removing two types of corrupting substances. One of these is flammable and **volatile** <note>original: *fugitivâ*, literally "fugitive" or "fleeing"</note>, while the other is a drossy, earthy substance. This is because fire lifts up and consumes all volatile substances. Likewise, in the **method of reduction** <term>the process of returning a substance to its metallic state after it has been dissolved or calcined</term>, that same fire separates all the earthy substance according to its own proportion.

We have written about this proportion in our other volume titled *The Investigation of Perfection* <note>original: *de perfectionis inveſtigatione*; likely referring to the Geberian text "De investigatione perfectionis"</note>, which precedes this book in its proper order. In that work, we wrote down whatever we investigated according to the reasoning of our mind. In this book, however, we have defined what we have actually seen and touched completely, according to the order of scientific practice.

These metals are also certainly purified through the **solution** <term>dissolving a solid into a liquid</term> of their substance, the method of which we have already described. They are likewise purified through the reduction of that which has been dissolved from them. For by this same method of preparation, the metal is found to be cleaner and more perfect than by any other kind of preparation. No other method can be compared to this one, except for that which is perfected through **sublimation** <term>the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collecting it as a solid again</term>; therefore, sublimation is equivalent to this method.

There is also a similar preparation for these metals: the hardening of their soft substance by the heat of their fusion. Our goal is to contrive a way to mix into their depths the substance of **fixed Quicksilver** <term>Mercury that has been treated so it no longer evaporates when heated</term>, or of **fixed Sulphur**, or something similar. This can also be done with hard and non-fusible things, such as **Talc**, **Marcasite** <term>iron pyrites, often called "fool's gold"</term>, and **Tutty** <term>zinc oxide, often collected from the flues of smelting furnaces</term>. This order is both useful and necessary.

## On the Preparation of **Jupiter** <term>the alchemical name for Tin</term> in Particular.
### CHAPTER 14.

The specific preparation of these metallic bodies—and first that of **Jupiter**—is multifaceted. One method is through **ceration** <term>the process of softening a substance to a wax-like consistency, often to prepare it for further transformation</term>; through this, its substance is hardened further, a result which does not occur with **Saturn** <term>the alchemical name for Lead</term>. **Alums** <term>a specific group of mineral salts used as astringents and in chemical processes</term> work in a similar way, for these specifically harden Jupiter. Another method involves keeping the metal in the fire of its calcination. Through this, it loses its **shriek** <note>original: *stridorem*; a reference to the "cry of tin," the distinctive crackling sound made when bending a piece of pure tin</note> and likewise the brittleness of its body. This similarly does not happen to Saturn, because the shriek...

## CHAPTER 15.

Following, therefore, the order of what has been established, let us describe the preparation of the "hard bodies" <note>in this context, metals with higher melting points or greater physical resistance compared to tin and lead</note>. First, indeed, that of **Copper** <term>Venus</term>; and afterward, that of **Iron** <term>Mars</term>. There is, therefore, a manifold method for the preparation of Copper. One is accomplished through **elevation** <term>sublimation; the process of vaporizing a solid and re-condensing it</term>; the other is performed without elevation. Thus, its more subtle part may be elevated, which is found to possess a most brilliant luster. Without sublimation, however, it is prepared either through substances that perform cleansing, or in its **calx** <term>the powdery oxide produced by burning metal</term>, or into a body [using substances] such as **Tutia**, **Salts**, and **Alums**...

## On the Preparation of Iron <note>original: *Martis*</note>.
### CHAPTER 16.

The preparation of **Iron** <term>Mars</term> is likewise manifold: for some is completed through **sublimation** <term>the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</term>, while some is completed without sublimation. That which is done by sublimation is performed with **Arsenic**, the method of which is as follows. Let us devise, as thoroughly as we can, a way to unite non-fixed <note>unstable or volatile</note> Arsenic with the iron, so that it melts together with it during fusion. Afterward, let it be sublimated in a vessel appropriate for sublimation; and this preparation is found to be better and more perfect than the others.

There is also another preparation of Iron itself, through Arsenic sublimated from it many times, until some quantity of the Arsenic remains with it. For if this is **reduced** <term>returned to its metallic state from a powder or oxide</term>, it will emerge white, fusible, clean, and prepared. There is similarly a third method of preparing the same, by melting it with **Lead** <term>Saturn</term> and **Tutia** <term>zinc oxide</term>; for from these it flows clean and white.

## On the Preparation of Copper <note>original: *Veneris*</note>.

### On the Preparation of Iron <note>original: Martis</note>.
## CHAPTER 16.

The preparation of **Iron** <term>Mars</term>, indeed, is similarly manifold: for some is completed through **sublimation** <term>vaporizing a solid and re-condensing it to purify it</term>, while some is performed without sublimation. 

That which is done by sublimation is performed with **Arsenic**, the method of which is this: Let us strive, as thoroughly as we can, to unite non-fixed <note>original: *non fixum*; meaning volatile or easily evaporated</note> Arsenic to the metal, so that it may melt together with it during fusion. # On the softening of hard bodies
## and on the hardening of soft ones.
### CHAPTER 17.

But lest we seem insufficient, since we promised that we would determine the ingenious **softening** <note>original: *mollificatione*</note> of hard bodies, and the **hardening** <note>original: *induratione*</note> of soft ones by way of **calcination** <term>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical state, often turning it into a powder</term>, let us therefore not omit it. We shall treat the hardening of soft bodies first, and the hard ones afterward. 

Namely, the method is this: let **precipitated Quicksilver** <term>Mercury; original: Argentum vivum</term> be dissolved, and let the **calcined body** <note>the metallic substance reduced to powder by fire</note>—which is intended to be hardened—also be dissolved. Let both of these solutions be mixed together. From these, the calcined body is to be mixed by turns: by grinding, **soaking** <term>original: *imbibendo*; the process of adding liquid to a dry substance until it is saturated</term>, calcining, and **reducing** <term>restoring a metallic powder back into a solid metal</term>, until it becomes hard and is capable of being melted <note>original: *fusibile*</note> upon ignition. This same result is successfully achieved not only with Mercury, but also with **Talc**, **Tutty** <term>zinc oxide found in the flues of furnaces</term>, and **Marcasite** <term>an iron sulfide mineral often mistaken for gold or silver</term>, provided they are calcined, dissolved, and soaked; and indeed, the purer these substances are, the more perfectly they transform the body.

Hard bodies are similarly softened by a related ingenious method. It is thus: let them be joined and **sublimated** <term>the process of vaporizing a solid and re-condensing it to purify it</term> with **Arsenic** many times. After the sublimation of the Arsenic, let them be roasted <note>original: *assentur*</note> with the proportion of fire appropriate to them, the method of which we have described in the book of furnaces. Finally, let them be reduced by the expression of their fire, as stated in its proper order, until they soften in fusion according to the specific requirements of the body's hardness. And indeed, all these alterations are of the **first order** <note>fundamental processes in the alchemical hierarchy</note>, without which the **Mastery** <term>original: *Magisterium*; the completion of the Great Work or alchemical goal</term> is not perfected.

## On the Washing of Mercury.
### CHAPTER 18.

Therefore, from what has been said before, it is necessary to give a complete account of the purification <note>original: *mundationem*</note> of **Quicksilver** <term>Mercury; original: Argentum vivum</term>. We say, therefore, that Quicksilver is purified in two ways: either by **sublimation** <term>the process of heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then collecting the cooled, purified solid</term>, the method of which we have already provided, or by **washing** <note>original: *lavacrum*</note>, the method of which is as follows.

Let the Quicksilver be poured into a glass or stone pan, and let a quantity of **vinegar** <note>original: *aceti*</note> be sprinkled over it, enough to cover it. Then let it be placed over a gentle fire and allowed to heat up, just enough so that it can still be handled with the fingers. Then let it be stirred with the fingers until it is divided into the tiniest parts, resembling a powder, and let it be stirred for as long as it takes for all the vinegar that was poured in to be consumed. 

Then, indeed, whatever **earthiness** <term>original: *terreitatis*; the impure, solid dregs found within the metal</term> is found in it should be washed with vinegar and cast away. Let this work be repeated upon it so many times until its earthiness is changed into a most perfect **celestial color** <note>original: *cælestinum colorem*; a sky-blue or bright silvery-blue hue</note>, which is the sign of a perfect washing. From these things, therefore, one must proceed to the medicines <note>referring to the alchemical "medicines" or elixirs used to transform metals</note>.

# THE SUMMA OF GEBER
## THE FOURTH BOOK
### *Of the Most Clear-Sighted Philosopher,*

->On<-
->MEDICINES IN A UNIVER-<-
->SAL DISCOURSE, AND ON THE FIVE DIF-<-
->FERENCES OF THE PERFECTION<-
->OF THEM,<-

### P R E F A C E.

Let us, therefore, first bring forth a universal discourse on medicines, with their causes and clear experiments. We suggest, therefore, that unless every superfluity—whether by the medicine or by the method of preparation—is removed from the imperfect [metals], they will not be perfected; namely, that all superfluous sulfurousness <note>original: *sulphureitas*; the volatile, impure quality of sulfur</note> and every unclean earthiness <note>original: *terreitas*; the solid, dross-like impurities</note> be taken from them, in such a way that it is separated from the mixture during melting <term>fusione; the process of liquefying metals with heat</term>, after the casting <term>projectionem; the act of throwing a small amount of the transmuting "medicine" onto a larger mass of molten base metal</term> of the medicine that alters them. And when you have indeed found this, you already possess one of the differences of perfection. Similarly, therefore, unless the medicine also brightens and changes [the metal] into a white color, or into a lemon-yellow <note>original: *citrinum*; in alchemy, a yellowing or "citrination" is the stage where silver-like metal begins to turn into gold</note> color, according to the inten-

-tion <note>Continuing from the previous page: "according to the intention"</note> which you seek, which brings about the splendor of brilliance and the clarity of beauty, the bodies diminished in perfection <note>The "imperfect" base metals like lead or tin</note> are not fully perfected in their completion. Furthermore, unless the medicine brings about a specific Lunar <note>original: *fusionem Lunarem*; melting into silver</note> or Solar <note>original: *fusionem Solarem*; melting into gold</note> fusion, it is not alterable in its completion; for it will not remain stable during the trials <note>The chemical tests used to prove the purity of a metal</note>, but will be completely separated from the mixture and disappear. This point is demonstrated more extensively by us in the chapter on **Cupellation** <term>Cineritio; a refining process where ores or alloyed metals are treated under high heat to separate noble metals, like gold and silver, from base metals</term>. 

Moreover, unless the medicine is made permanent through a firm alteration of its "impression" <note>original: *impressio*; the physical change or "stamp" the medicine leaves upon the metal</note>, the transformation is of no value; for the impression does not last, but fades away. In addition, unless the weights of perfection are achieved, it does not transform into its firm and true completion of nature—one that cannot be faked through the error of belief. For the **weight of nature** <term>pondus naturae; the specific gravity or density of the metal, which must match that of true gold or silver</term> is one of the signs of perfection. 

It is clear, therefore, that since there are five differences of perfection, the medicine of our Mastery <note>original: *Magisterii*; the Art or Great Work of Alchemy</note> must necessarily provide these five differences in its perfection. From this, it becomes evident from what sources our medicine is extracted. For it is drawn from those things which are most closely joined to the [metallic] bodies, and which cling to them in a friendly way, deep within, al- <meta>catchword: teran- (completing the word "alterantibus" or "altering")</meta>

the utility of the substance of bodies, and uniformity in their essence. For through this, their parts can be made dense, since nothing intervenes <note>The author argues that perfection comes from eliminating gaps between particles</note>, and the densification of parts leads to an increase in weight, which is the mark of its perfection. It is clear, therefore, that both in the preparation of managing the bodies and in the method of the perfecting medicine itself, it is necessary to seek out "subtlety" <note>Refinement or extreme fineness of particles that allows the medicine to penetrate the metal</note> through the skills of the work. ## *On the Threefold Order of Medicines.*
### CHAPTER II.

We have indeed indicated that there are necessarily three different classes of Medicines. One is of the first order, another of the second, and another of the third. I call a "medicine of the first order" any preparation of minerals which, when cast upon bodies diminished in perfection <note>Base metals like lead, tin, copper, or iron that lack the balance of gold or silver</note>, leaves an alteration that does not bring about a sufficient completion; instead, the altered metal eventually changes back and is corrupted, as the "impression" <note>original: *impressio*; the physical quality or "stamp" of color and property imparted by the alchemical agent</note> of that medicine evaporates entirely. An example of this is any whitening sublimation <term>Sublimatio; a process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collecting the solid deposit formed as it cools</term> of **Venus** <note>original: *Veneris*; the alchemical name for Copper</note> or **Mars** <note>original: *Martis*; the alchemical name for Iron</note> that does not undergo fixation <term>Fixio; the process of making a substance stable so that it no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to intense fire</term>. Of this same kind is every addition of the color of the **Sun** <note>original: *Solis*; Gold</note> and the **Moon** <note>original: *Lunæ*; Silver</note>

or of those mixed with Copper <note>original: *Veneris*</note>, placed upon the furnace of cementation <note>A refining process where metals are heated with powdered reagents</note>, such as Verdigris <note>original: *Zimar*; likely referring to copper acetate or oxide</note> and similar things. For this [medicine] changes the metal in a way that does not last, but rather diminishes itself through evaporation <note>original: *exhalationem*</note>. We call a "medicine of the second order" any preparation which, when cast upon bodies lacking perfection <note>Base metals</note>, alters some aspect toward completion, yet leaves behind some modes of corruption entirely; such as is the calcination of bodies <note>Heating a substance to high temperatures to remove volatile impurities</note>, by which everything volatile <note>original: *fugitivum*</note> is destroyed. And a medicine of this kind is one that permanently turns Silver <note>original: *Lunam*</note> golden <note>original: *citrinans*</note>, or even permanently whitens Copper <note>original: *Venerem dealbans*</note>, while leaving other corruptions within them. I call a "medicine of the third order" every preparation which, when it reaches the bodies, removes all corruption through its projection <note>The final act of casting the transmuting agent upon the base metal</note>, and perfects it with every mark of completion. This indeed is one and only; and therefore, through it, we are excused from the labors of discovering ten different medicines. Thus, the work of the first order is called the Lesser Work <note>original: *opus minus*</note>. The second, the Middle. The third, indeed, the Greater Work <note>original: *opus majus*</note>. And this is a sufficient distinction between all the medicines.

<header>BOOK FOUR</header>

...some are of the first order, others of the second, and others indeed of the third; and the same applies to Quicksilver <note>original: *Argenti vivi*</note> similarly. Let us therefore recount the differences of all medicines of the first order, then the second, and finally the third. First, we deal with the bodies <note>The "bodies" are the solid metals: gold, silver, copper, iron, tin, and lead.</note>. Afterward, we shall deliver a report on the medicine of Quicksilver, with a full discourse and in the proper order. 

We say, then, that among the medicines of the first order for bodies, some are for hard metals and others for soft ones. Of the hard metals, some are for Copper <note>original: *Veneris*; the metal associated with the planet Venus</note>, others for Iron <note>original: *Martis*; associated with Mars</note>, and others for Silver <note>original: *Lunæ*; literally "of the Moon"</note>. For Copper and Iron, the medicine consists of a pure whitening <term>dealbatio|The alchemical process of "whitening" a base metal to make it resemble silver</term> of their substance. For Silver, however, it is a reddening <term>rubificatio|The process of "reddening" silver to give it the appearance and properties of gold</term>, along with a golden-yellowing <note>original: *citrinitate*</note> of pleasant brilliance. 

Because reddening does not occur for Copper and Iron when using a medicine of the first order that shows brilliance—since they are entirely impure—they are not fit to receive the brilliance of redness until a preparation is applied to them that induces such brilliance. Let us therefore first describe all the medicines of Copper, and then those of Iron, which fall within the first order.

# *On the Medicines of Copper in the first order.*
## *CHAPTER IV.*

There is, then, a medicine for whitening Copper, one by means of Quicksilver, and another by means of Arsenic. By means of Quicksilver, the medicine that whitens it is completed in this way: First, precipitated Quicksilver is dissolved, and similarly the [calcined]...

<term>calcination|The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder or oxide</term>, and both of these solutions are mixed into one; then they are indeed coagulated, and their medicine is cast upon the body of <note>original: *Veneris*; the metal copper, named for the planet Venus</note> Copper itself. For this whitens and cleanses, yet it does not bring about a perfect completion, since it often happens that the altered metal is changed and corrupted. Furthermore, and in another way, <term>precipitated|A solid formed out of a liquid solution</term> Quicksilver <note>original: *Argentum vivum*; mercury</note> is dissolved, and <term>Litharge|A lead oxide mineral, often used as a flux in refining</term> is dissolved, and both of these solutions are joined into one. Then, the calcination of the body of Copper—the focus of which is to be whitened—is dissolved and joined with the aforementioned solutions. They are coagulated, and then indeed they are cast upon that body: for it is whitened by this means. In another way, however, a quantity of Quicksilver is <term>sublimated|The process of heating a substance so it turns to vapor and then cools back into a solid, bypassing the liquid phase</term> alternately from its body, until a part of the Quicksilver remains fixed with it, with the ignition completed. From then on, it should be ground with distilled vinegar, soaking it frequently, so that it may be better mixed into its depths. From this point it is indeed roasted, and finally in a similar manner, the Quicksilver is sublimated from it, and it is repeatedly soaked and roasted. And thus the work is repeated upon it so many times until a great quantity of Quicksilver remains stable in it, with the ignition completed. For this is a good <term>whitening|original: dealbatio; the alchemical process of making base metals look like silver</term> of the first order. In another way, however, upon the precipitated Quicksilver, Quicksilver in its own nature is sublimated so many times until it is fixed within it and allows for melting. Then indeed it is cast upon the substance of Copper, for it whitens it remarkably well. In another way indeed

Silver <note>original: *Luna*; the alchemical "Moon," representing silver</note> is dissolved and Litharge <note>original: *Lithargirium*; an oxide of lead</note> is dissolved, and the solutions are joined together. From these, the substance of Copper <note>original: *Veneris*; the metal "Venus"</note> is whitened. But indeed, it is whitened even better if Quicksilver <note>original: *Argentum vivum*; mercury</note> is made permanent in all the medicines.

It is also whitened by means of sublimated <term>Arsenic|A chemical element often used in alchemy to whiten copper, creating a silver-like alloy, though it often made the metal brittle</term>, as when the <term>calcination|The reduction of a metal to powder through heat</term> of Copper is taken, and its <term>sublimation|The process of vaporizing a solid and re-solidifying it to purify it</term> is repeated through it, until the arsenic remains with the copper and whitens it. But unless you exercise your own ingenuity with the methods of sublimation, the Arsenic will not persist in it with any lasting change. And it is so that, after the first stage of sublimation, you repeat the process a second time, as we described in the sublimation of Marcasite <note>original: *Marchafitæ*; in this period, a term for various metallic sulfides like pyrite used as a source for sulfur or arsenic</note>.

It is also whitened in another way, such as by casting sublimated Arsenic onto Silver, and then casting this whole mixture onto Copper, for it whitens it particularly well. Or first mix Litharge, or burnt Lead <note>original: *Plumbum uftum*; lead oxide</note>, dissolved with Silver; then cast Arsenic upon this; and this whole mixture cast upon Copper whitens it, and it is a good whitening of the first order. Or onto only dissolved and reduced Litharge, let sublimated Arsenic be cast, and this whole mixture onto the fusion of Copper, because it whitens it with a bright appearance. Or let Silver and Copper be mixed together, and upon these let every whitening medicine be cast. For Silver is a greater "friend" to Arsenic than any other metallic body, and therefore it removes the brittleness <note>original: *fractionem*; the tendency of the metal to crack or break when alloyed with arsenic</note> from it; Lead <note>original: *Saturnus*; the metal "Saturn"</note> is secondary in this regard, and therefore it is mixed with them. In another way...

We melt sublimated **Arsenic**, until it forms chunks. Then, indeed, we cast it piece by piece onto **Copper** <note>original: *Venerem*; the metal associated with the planet Venus</note>. We instruct that it be cast in chunks rather than in powder, because powder ignites more easily than a chunk; therefore, it vanishes more easily than a chunk and is consumed before it can fall upon the ignited body of the metal.

Furthermore, that same redness <note>The natural reddish-orange color of copper</note> is also removed in another way, namely with **Tutty** <term>Tutia|A historical term for zinc oxide, often collected from the chimneys of smelting furnaces and used to make brass</term>, and the metal is whitened. But because the whitening effect of Tutty is not sufficient, it only performs **Citrination** <term>Citrinatio|The alchemical process of "yellowing," or turning a metal a gold-like color, considered a step between whitening and the final transformation into gold</term>. Any yellowing, however, is related to whitening.

The method for this is as follows: every kind of Tutty is dissolved and <term>calcined|Reduced to a powder through high heat</term>, then the Copper is treated likewise. These two solutions are then joined, and with these, the substance of Copper is yellowed. If you practice with Tutty, you will find it profitable. Copper is also whitened by means of sublimated **Marcasite** <note>original: *Marchasitam*; likely iron pyrites or similar metallic sulfides</note>, just as it is with sublimated **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argento vivo*; mercury</note>, and the method is one and the same.

## Concerning the medicines of Iron in the first order.
### CHAPTER V.

But because Iron does not possess a proper melting point <note>Iron has a much higher melting point than the other metals known to early alchemists, making it difficult to alloy or "whiten" through simple melting</note>, it is necessary for us to whiten it using a "fusing medicine" <note>A flux or additive to help the metal flow</note>. Therefore, every medicine that whitens Copper also whitens Iron, following a preparation of the same order, but...

## *On the medicines of Silver in the first order.*

### CHAPTER VI.

Following, therefore, the order of the preceding things, let us speak with certain truth regarding the **medicine for Silver** <note>original: *Lunae*; Silver</note>, a **yellowing substance** <term>citrinantem|Relating to citrination, a stage in the Great Work where the metal or substance turns yellow, often as a precursor to the gold-making stage</term> in the order of the first kind. And this is indeed the yellow medicine, which adheres to it [the metal] in its depth, and by adhering colors it, whether by its own nature or by the artifice of this Mastery. Let us therefore speak of the medicine which, innate to it from its own root, adheres to it; and then of the artifices by which we make a thing of any kind adhere with a firm penetration. 

We extract it, however, either from **Sulphur**, or from **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argento vivo*; mercury</note>, or from a mixture of both; but through Sulphur it is indeed more diminished, while through Quicksilver it is more perfect. It is similarly extracted through certain mineral things which are not of this kind: such as **Vitriol** <term>Vitriolum|A sulfate of a metal, like iron or copper, often used as a corrosive or coloring agent</term> and **Copperas** <term>Cuperosa|Another name for vitriol, specifically green or blue mineral salts</term>, which is called "gum of copper" or a "dripping" <note>original: *stillicidium*; likely referring to mineral-rich water found dripping in mines</note> of the same. 

Let us therefore first present all the methods for medicines that arise from Quicksilver. Then, appropriately, those from Sulphur or from a mixture of both. Lastly, those from the gum of copper and similar things. The method, therefore, for that which

If, however, the Quicksilver has grown red in the precipitation to the perfection of its projection, the stated administration is sufficient, without the mixing of [another] thing to color it. It is indeed made red through Sulphur, but its reddening is difficult and immensely laborious. However, the necessity of the work leads us to this: that we first calcine it, and afterwards fix it, which is a great deal of labor. Then indeed, let us administer [it] by this same preparation, and let us pour the same projection upon the substance of Silver; and yet, a shining yellowing does not result from it, but rather one that is dark and leaden with a deadly earthiness. ## *On medicines of the second order.*

### CHAPTER 7.

It remains, therefore, to move on to the medicines of the second order, since the requirements of our discourse have been sufficiently met with true proofs—both those that are hidden and those that are manifest—alongside true experience. Since there is one medicine for cleansed bodies and another for **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum|The alchemical name for Mercury</term>—namely, the perfection of its **coagulation** <term>coagulatio|The process of turning a liquid or vaporous substance into a solid</term>—let us first describe the complete medicines of the bodies. From there, we shall proceed to the medicines for the coagulation of **Quicksilver** itself into a true **Sun-making** <term>Solificum|A substance or process that turns base metals into Gold</term> or **Moon-making** <term>Lunificum|A substance or process that turns base metals into Silver</term> state.

A medicine of the second order, then, is that which completes imperfect things by the addition of a single quality of perfection. Truly, since there are many causes of corruption in each of the imperfect bodies—as in **Saturn** <term>Saturnus|The alchemical name for Lead</term>, for instance, there is a volatile sulfurousness and the "flight" of **Quicksilver**, through which corruption is necessarily brought about, as well as its earthiness—let a medicine be made which either removes one of these entirely, or masks it by "cloaking" <note>original: *palliando*; to "palliate" or disguise the outward appearance of a metal's flaws without necessarily changing its inner "root"</note>, leaving behind only the other causes of imperfection. Because there is something unchangeable in bodies, which is innate in the very root of those bodies, it cannot be removed by a medicine of the second order; therefore, every medicine of that order...

<summary>This page introduces 'medicines of the second order,' which are alchemical agents designed to treat specific imperfections in base metals like Lead (Saturn) or to solidify Mercury into Gold or Silver. The author distinguishes between fundamental changes and 'cloaking' techniques that mask surface-level flaws.</summary>
